Related pane-reading trap seen in the same runs: a stale `✻ Cogitated for 1m 16s` spinner row survives above the box after CC ends a turn via its "hook blocked the turn 9 consecutive times — overriding" cap, so `_classify_boundary` keeps reporting `busy` for a pane that is genuinely idle.
- **Before choosing ANY similarity threshold to decide "is this the same artifact, older version?", measure the real version-distance distribution out of git history — the intuitive answer is routinely wrong.** #64 needed to tell "the autopilot `/goal` template, older version" from "a goal the user wrote". Replaying all 62 commits that touched `skills/autopilot/SKILL.md` showed the two ranges OVERLAP: two CURRENT but different variants sit at ratio 0.7100, while one variant against its own three-week-old version sits at 0.7279 — so no threshold separates them, and a false positive would silently overwrite a live loop's instructions. The working answer was an EXACT hash over a NORMALIZED form (parenthetical citations, backtick spans and punctuation dropped): it absorbs the editorial trimming a printing agent really does (a live loop was 0.9808 from the template and byte-matched NO shipped version, yet normalized to it exactly) without absorbing substance (26 distinct raw historical versions → 25 normalized). Pair it with tracking-by-OBSERVATION (record the variant the first time a session is seen matching a CURRENT version) so anything never observed matching is untouchable by construction rather than by threshold.
- **Live-proving a watchdog job that drives a REAL Claude Code session: two harness traps beyond the documented `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` recipe.** (a) Launch the binary through a symlink NAMED `claude` (`ln -s <versions>/<ver> $SCRATCH/bin/claude`) — invoking the versioned path directly makes tmux report `pane_current_command` as `2.1.220`, which `_reconcile_candidate_panes` correctly rejects, so every sweep silently sees zero panes and the job looks broken. (b) A scratch session armed with an autopilot-SHAPED goal will actually start doing autopilot work — one here spawned background agents and grew to 82 K context inside minutes. Give the throwaway session a stop condition that keeps it idle, and tear it down (`/goal clear`, kill the pane, then confirm no orphan process survives the `tmux kill-session`) the moment the proof lands.
- **A classifier-style hook (allow-list / block-list over real commands) can only be verified by REPLAYING a real command CORPUS through it — unit tests and a hand-written smoke list both miss the shapes that actually occur.** #80: after the unit tests were green and a 15-case smoke list of "realistic" commands passed, replaying ALL 687 of gatekeeper's real main-agent Bash commands from one day (extract them with a small script over the transcript's `isSidechain=false` assistant `tool_use` blocks, then run the hook once per command with the payload built via `json.dumps`) still surfaced three whole false-positive CLASSES that no hand-written case had: an output reducer after a pipe (`gh pr merge … 2>&1 | tail -2` — the single most common coordination idiom in the corpus), a bounded verification peek (`head -5 /tmp/out`, `tail -3 SKILL.md` right after writing it), and an assertion (`grep -c`). Block rate went 18.6% → 14.1% and each class became its own RED→GREEN pair. The corpus replay is also the only honest way to state the blast radius of a classifier change ("14% of a real day's commands would block, and here is what they are") instead of guessing. Do the replay BEFORE `push` — a hook deploys to every managed box and gates every session's Bash.
- **When a guard hook "never fired", read its BYPASS log before concluding the classifier is too lenient — a self-servable PERMANENT escape marker is a session-wide kill switch, and an agent WILL arm it the first time the hook false-positives.** #80's ticket reasoned from the ABSENCE of `/tmp/airuleset-main-exec-block.log` that #66's allow-list was letting everything through; the real chain was `/tmp/airuleset-main-exec-bypass.log` with 332 entries — the main session hit a false positive on the mandated `cat > body.md <<'EOF'` recipe, `touch`ed the documented exception marker at 01:24 with a rationalization in its own transcript ("Hook's documented exception applies… Taking it (it's logged)"), and the hook was fully dead for the next 17 hours. Two design lessons for any future guard: (a) an escape hatch must be ONE-SHOT (honoring it consumes it) so abuse costs a turn each time instead of once, and (b) arming the escape hatch must itself be exempt from every other gate in the same hook, or the guard sits in front of its own only exit. Diagnose in this order: bypass log → block log → classifier.
- **A guard whose acceptance forbids ever stopping the loop should be built as a RATE-LIMITED NUDGE, not a cap: make the block itself reset the counter it fires on.** #80's per-dispatch Bash counter blocks once past N and immediately zeroes, so re-running the same command right after a nudge passes — the worst case is one instructive block per N calls, never two in a row, even if the reset-on-dispatch wiring (`PreToolUse` Agent/Task/Workflow) were to fail entirely. That property is what makes it safe to deploy a behavioral guard to 6 live boxes at once. Related wiring rule: register such a reset on EXACT tool-name matchers (`"Agent"`, `"Task"`, `"Workflow"` as separate entries), never a regex like `"Agent|Task|Workflow"` — an unsupported regex silently never matches, and a counter that never resets is exactly the failure mode the nudge design is insuring against.

- **Where content actually LOADS in Claude Code — settled empirically + from the 2.1.220 bundle (#91), do not re-derive.** A **skill body** enters context ONLY on an explicit `Skill` call; session start carries just a `skill_listing` attachment of name+description (measured 25,401 B ≈ 6.4k tokens for 34 skills — paid every session whether or not any skill is ever invoked). A **`rules/*.md` + `paths:`** file auto-injects its FULL body as a `nested_memory` attachment (`"type": "User"` for `~/.claude/rules`, base = session CWD; `"Project"` for `<repo>/.claude/rules`, base = repo root) — but the trigger is **Read / @-mention / IDE-open ONLY**: `Edit` and `Write` never push `nestedMemoryAttachmentTriggers`, so content that must land when the model is about to WRITE code cannot live there. A **`PreToolUse` hook returning `hookSpecificOutput.additionalContext`** injects on the ACTION and is the only automatic surface for action-shaped content (`gh pr merge`, `git push`, a deploy, an `Agent` dispatch); the same field also works on `UserPromptSubmit`, `PostToolUse`, `Stop`, `SubagentStop`. Gitignore semantics (the `ignore` npm package) apply to `paths:`, a trailing `/**` is stripped, an uncompilable pattern silently matches NOTHING, and `paths: "**"` silently turns the rule back into an always-on file.
- **Proving "does this content reach the context" costs one cheap print-mode run — never assert it, and never test it against the real profile.** `export CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR=/tmp/<scratch>/config` FIRST (a `VAR=val cmd` prefix does not survive into later commands), rsync the real `~/.claude` in minus `projects/ jobs/ telemetry/ plugins-heavy dirs`, copy `~/.claude.json` + `.credentials.json`, then run the raw binary (`~/.local/share/claude/versions/<ver>`), never the bashrc `claude` wrapper (its `_has_conversation()` reads the REAL `$HOME`). Make the probe **falsifiable in both directions**: ask for a fact that exists ONLY in the candidate body (`grep` it to 0 hits in `modules/**` and `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` first) and, in the same run, a fact from a body that should NOT have loaded — a one-sided "it knew the answer" proves nothing, since the model may know it from the always-on rules. The transcript then shows the structural proof: an `attachment` entry of type `hook_additional_context` (`hookEvent`/`hookName`) or `nested_memory`.
- **A hook that classifies a COMMAND must strip what the command CARRIES before matching — heredoc bodies first, then quoted spans.** Re-learned the hard way within an hour of shipping `inject-situational-rule.sh` (#91): a `gh issue comment -F body.md <<'EOF' … EOF` whose body merely DESCRIBED the trigger table matched nine topics and injected 65.3 kB into the live session. Same class as #80's `cat > body.md <<'EOF'` false positive, and it will recur in any new command-matching hook. Pair it with a total-size cap per call that **defers** rather than consumes (leave the dedup marker unset for what you drop, so its own action can still load it later).
- **`.claude/` is gitignored here, and a directory-pattern ignore cannot be negated from inside it** — git never descends, so `!.claude/rules/x.md` alone does nothing. The working carve-out is `.claude/*` followed by `!.claude/rules/` (#92).
- **Before concluding from a transcript corpus that a mechanism "never fires", check WHEN the mechanism was wired.** A 7,941-transcript / 4.5-month sweep found zero `rules/*.md` injections and read as proof the surface was dead; the user-level symlinking (`2fc29a6`, #40) had actually landed 25 hours before the sweep, so the corpus could not contain a single hit. The same sweep's finding for SKILLS was real (months of availability, still ~0 invocations) — the two look identical in the data and are opposite conclusions.

- **The always-on STUB is what actually enforces.** `test_dynamic_application.py`
  caps every stub at `< 8 lines`, so the stub must carry the enforcement-critical
  CORE tersely. `e9d1022`'s batch stub kept the bundling invariants and dropped
  the per-issue design cycle — that single omission was the whole bug.
- **A conversion is safe when a HOOK backs it**, not when the prose moved.
  `git-fetch-first` (session-start-fetch + pre-push-base-sync),
  `local-builds` (block-tier0-local-build), `deploy-from-clean-tree`
  (pre-deploy-clean-tree) survive because a hook enforces the core regardless of
  which surface loaded.
- **Measure invocations before trusting a skill conversion.** Lifetime census over
  all transcripts:
  `cd ~/.claude/projects && grep -rhoE '"skill": ?"[a-zA-Z0-9:_-]+"' --include=*.jsonl . | sed 's/.*: *"//;s/"//' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn`
  (one single pass — a per-name loop over this tree times out past 2 min).
  2026-07-27 results: `version-on-dashboard` **0**, `pr-merge-policy` 1,
  `deploy-ssh` 1, `ci-push-discipline` 1, `verify-issue-still-valid` 1,
  `view-image-urls` 1. A skill with ~0 invocations holds knowledge that is, in
  practice, deleted.
- **`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-06-13-pipeline-v2.md:619` is DISPROVEN** — it says
  in-session subagents "boot with a reduced system prompt and no rules". They do
  not; they inherit the rules fully. The real gap is skills. Do not redesign the
  dispatch mechanism on the strength of that sentence.

**Probing is cheap and settles these questions in ~20 s** — dispatch a `haiku`
`general-purpose` agent whose whole task is "report what is literally in your own
context, edit nothing". Prefer that over reasoning about how Claude Code *should*
behave; this repo has repeatedly shipped fixes whose stated cause was wrong.

**Verifying a rule/agent change actually reached runtime:** dispatch a real
`autopilot-worker` on a real ticket with `isolation: "worktree"` and a guard —
"make NO commits or pushes; stop when you would create the first code commit" —
and a prompt that never names the behavior under test. If the behavior shows up
unprompted, that is proof; a prompt that hints at it proves nothing. Salvage any
work the guarded run produced onto its ticket before `git worktree remove`.

### #105 resolved — `rules/*.md` path-scoped injection is MAIN-session-only, never reaches a dispatched subagent

Settled the row #104 left open. A dispatched subagent has its OWN transcript
file, separate from the parent: `<projects-dir>/<encoded-cwd>/<session-id>/subagents/agent-<id>.jsonl`
(found by `find <projects-dir> -newer <marker>`, or from the parent's
`SubagentStop` payload). Reading it directly (not the parent transcript) is
what proves the negative: a real subagent, dispatched from a session whose cwd
correctly contained the matching file, produced ZERO `nested_memory` entries —
only `deferred_tools_delta` and `skill_listing` — across its whole transcript,
while the identical Read in the parent MAIN session produced two. This is the
structural proof form (grep the transcript for `"type":"nested_memory"`, read
the `.attachment.path`), never a self-report ("did you see rule X?" — a model
can answer correctly from always-on content it already has, proving nothing).

Fix pattern for any FUTURE `rules/*.md` conversion whose topic a dispatched
worker must act on (CI config, migrations, anything editable): restore a short
(<8 line) always-on stub at the module's old path, alongside — never instead
of — its `rules/*.md` entry in `profiles/universal.profile`. Confirmed safe:
`airuleset.py`'s profile parser branches on the `rules/` prefix and ONLY
symlinks that entry into `~/.claude/rules/` — it never `@import`s it into
CLAUDE.md — so adding the stub doesn't change or duplicate the MAIN-session
path-scoped behavior; it only adds the subagent-reachable half.
- **A behavioural claim about the ruleset is settled by REPLAYING a closed ticket at `<red>^` and grading with the RED test that actually shipped — never by an LLM judge on the primary axis.** #94: the ground truth is free (the real fix already landed), the test is black-box, and it is falsifiable in both directions (red before, green after), so "did this run fix it" needs zero opinion. Two things this technique surfaces that a synthetic prompt cannot: (a) a ticket body that PROPOSES its own fix biases a run toward implementing the proposal instead of diagnosing the cause — #88's body proposed "strip `$( … )`" and the minimal-ruleset run did exactly that and solved the stated case, while the real landed cause was line-continuation splicing with no substitution involved; so an oracle drawn from what SHIPPED legitimately rewards distrusting the ticket, and that has to be stated when reporting, not hidden; (b) grading must read the WORKING TREE (`git diff <base> -- .`), not just commits — a budget- or timeout-killed run can hold a complete, correct fix that it never got to commit, and scoring it as "produced nothing" would be simply wrong.
- **Standing up a scratch `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` profile for an experiment: strip `mcpServers` and `projects` out of the copied `~/.claude.json`, and drop every `notify-*` hook from the copied `settings.json`.** MCP servers are constant across arms and only add startup cost and tool-definition noise; the notify hooks would fire the experiment's own `❓`/`✅`/api-error pings at the user's real phone and queue real compact requests. Keep every `block-*` / `stop-check-*` gate — those are the enforcement under test. When an experiment defines a "minimal ruleset" arm, `inject-situational-rule.sh` belongs with the RULE TEXT, not with the gates: its only function is injecting rule bodies and it blocks nothing, so leaving it enabled makes the minimal arm not minimal.
- **A SUBAGENT that must wait on a long EXTERNAL process launches it `setsid nohup … &` from a FOREGROUND Bash call and then polls in bounded foreground loops.** This is not the banned `run_in_background` shape (`ci-monitoring.md`): the detached OS process is not a Claude Code background task, so nothing re-invokes the PARENT and nothing SIGTERMs on compaction — the subagent stays alive, and each poll call covers ~9 minutes of waiting with the Bash `timeout` near its 600000 ms cap. Poll on the run's own terminal ARTEFACT (the result file the process writes last), never on a process-liveness check alone.
- **The `mv`-the-hook-out RED→GREEN technique (above, #7/#10/#11) also applies to EXTENDING an EXISTING hook, not just brand-new ones — `git stash push -- <the files carrying the fix>` (implementation + doc), commit the test-only diff, confirm the new tests genuinely fail, `git stash pop`, then commit the implementation.** Used for #45/#47 (2026-07-27): both tickets modified an existing hook/existing skills rather than creating new files, so the "mv the file out" trick doesn't apply — stashing exactly the implementation-carrying files (never the whole tree) while leaving the test files staged gets the same true-RED proof with no risk of losing the implementation (`git stash pop` restores it verbatim).
- **This repo pushes DIRECT to `main` (no dev branch, no PR, no CI) — GitHub still auto-closes an issue from a `Closes #N` in a commit message pushed straight to the default branch, no PR needed.** Confirmed live for #45/#47 (2026-07-27): both closed within the same second `git push` landed. Don't assume the closing-keyword mechanism needs a PR merge event — it fires on any push to the repo's default branch that CONTAINS the closing commit.
- **Splitting one working-tree diff across TWO tickets' GREEN commits when both land in the SAME file: `git add -p <file>` and answer per hunk (`y`/`n`), one call per file** — `git`'s hunk splitter already separates independent edits (a doc-comment + constant tuple change vs. a new-function + call-site change) into distinct hunks automatically when they don't touch adjacent lines; feeding `printf 'y\nn\n' | git add -p <file>` stages only the hunks answered `y`, leaving the rest for a later `git add` + commit. Used for #25/#61/#98 (2026-07-27): all three tickets' fixes lived in `airuleset.py`, and this got a clean per-ticket commit history with zero manual patch surgery. Works only when the unrelated hunks are non-adjacent (they naturally were here); an interleaved single hunk would need `s`/`e` (split/edit) instead.
- **A watchdog job's DELIBERATELY NARROW scope pinned by an existing test is a signal to add a SIBLING job for the general case, never to widen the pinned job.** #44: job 12 (`model_reconcile`) only restarts a session still parked on the hardcoded `fable`/`opus-4` substrings — `test_sonnet_session_is_skipped` (tests/test_watchdog.py) pins that a non-matching model must NOT be touched, encoding the #37 cost-migration exception as deliberate, not incomplete. Widening job 12's own filter to "any mismatch vs `target_model`" would both break that pinned test AND violate the hard "never auto-downtier the user's `/model` choice" rule (model-awareness.md) — a session where the user manually chose a non-default model would get force-restarted back to the config default. The fix (job 23, `model_generation_reconcile`) is a NEW job comparing the session's LAUNCH-time model (`transcript_first_model`, reads the transcript FORWARD — opposite direction from `transcript_last_model`) against the current target, but restarts ONLY when the session's CURRENT model still equals its own launch model (`transcript_last_model(tpath) == transcript_first_model(tpath)`) — i.e. the user has NEVER touched `/model` in that session at all. That single guard is what makes an otherwise-invasive "restart on config drift" mechanism safe: a deliberate manual choice, in EITHER direction, is never fought, only a session purely coasting on a stale launch default. Reuse this exact test — first-vs-last-model comparison, never widen a pinned narrow job — for any future "should we restart on X changing" watchdog job.
- **The #96-documented `cmd | python3 - <<'PYEOF'` stdin-vs-heredoc trap recurs easily — it bit AGAIN (#24) inside a routine "append this bash variable's lines to a JSON file" helper.** The tell: reaching for a pipe to hand a MULTI-LINE bash variable (here, a newline-joined list of just-cancelled run ids) to an embedded Python heredoc "for stdin-style iteration" — `python3 - <<'PYEOF'` ALREADY claims stdin for the SCRIPT SOURCE, so the piped data is silently swallowed and the script quietly does nothing with it (no error, no crash — just an empty/unwritten result, which for a "write to a file" helper looks like nothing happened rather than like a bug). Reflex fix: pass the variable via argv (`python3 - "$PENDING_FILE" "$NEWLY_CANCELLED" <<'PYEOF' ... sys.argv[2].splitlines() ...`) instead of piping it, every time a NEW embedded-Python-in-a-heredoc helper needs bash-variable DATA alongside its heredoc SOURCE — never just when writing a brand-new hook from scratch (this one was an addition to an already-existing hook).
- **A hook that must resolve "what does THIS box's own `~/.ssh/config` say about alias X" (not a guess, not a hardcoded per-host table) reads the file directly at hook-EXECUTION time with a minimal single-stanza parser: scan for an EXACT `Host <alias>` line (space-separated multi-pattern lines never match — best-effort, no wildcard/Match/Include support), track `User`/`IdentityFile` only while inside that one matching block, return `(None, None)` on any missing/unreadable file.** #68 (`_resolve_ssh_config_host` in `block-subdev-ssh-misuse.sh`): this lets a bare `ssh subdev` (no explicit user/`-i` — the real `process-subdev` nudge shape) be authorized ONLY on the box whose deployed config actually resolves it to the sanctioned identity (gatekeeper's `Host subdev { User root; IdentityFile ~/.ssh/subdev_admin }`), while leaving every OTHER box (dev1 has no such stanza) completely unaffected — reading at execution time means the hook's behavior tracks whatever is ACTUALLY deployed, never a snapshot baked into the allow-list. Reuse this shape for any future "trust this box's own local config, not a guess" ssh/host-identity hook.
- **A "keep it generic, reword the worked example to historical" decision (the established `_FOREIGN_TMUX_USERS = ()` precedent) needs its wording precedent FOLLOWED, not just its keep/delete verdict.** #34: the wider sudo-hosted-stream mechanism (`_foreign_user`/`_foreign_session_info`/`_foreign_transcript_goal`, job 7's `hosted_users` merge, job 10's `waiting=True` hosted path) was still describing montalu in PRESENT tense ("montalu's claude runs in THIS tmux") four call-sites away from the ALREADY-migrated `_FOREIGN_TMUX_USERS` comment block, which correctly says "used to run… now has its own tmux on subdev." A `grep -n "montalu" <file>` sweep found ~25 hits; most were fine (dated incident references), but four docstrings/comments genuinely presented a dead shape as live. Fix: match the ALREADY-established wording pattern verbatim (past tense + "no live pane matches this today" + the migrating ticket number) rather than inventing new phrasing — a repo that already solved this exact wording problem once should solve it the same way everywhere.
- **Blind-grading an A/B experiment's diffs needs a GENUINELY separate context, not "I'll generate the mapping and then pretend not to have seen it" — dispatch a FRESH subagent that only ever sees the anonymized patch files + issue bodies + a rubric stated up front, and reveal the condition mapping only after it reports back.** #106: a single-context agent that builds its own blind-mapping.json cannot un-see it in the same turn; a `general-purpose` subagent given ONLY `blind106/<task>-X.patch` / `-Y.patch` (never the source `diff-<task>-<cond>.patch` names, never the mapping file) is actually blind. Worth the extra dispatch — this round's blind grade genuinely disagreed with the objective-oracle numbers on the SAME data (grader favored the minimal-ruleset diff on 2/3 pairs while the oracle pass-rate favored full), which is itself evidence neither axis has separated a real effect from noise yet.
- **A transcript "hook activity" metric built on a bracketed `[hook-name]` text-scan (`count_hook_blocks`'s `HOOK_BLOCK_RE`) essentially never matches real Claude Code hook feedback — hooks return prose (`🚫 BLOCKED: …`) or escaped JSON (`{"decision": "block", ...}`), never a bracket tag — so it reads 0 even when hooks genuinely fired.** But the OBVIOUS-looking fix (grep the transcript text for the JSON `"decision":"block"` shape) is WORSE, not better: on a ticket that is literally about fixing a hook, the model reads/cats that hook's own SOURCE FILE repeatedly, and the source contains that exact JSON shape in its own comments/echo strings — a naive text grep over-counts by 20-50x from pure self-reference, not real block events. A trustworthy count needs a per-line JSON-structural parse that only counts a `tool_result` entry genuinely marked `is_error` (a real PreToolUse denial) — and even that still misses the Stop-hook-specific shape entirely (no `stop_hook_summary`/`preventedContinuation` marker was found in ANY of 6 real transcripts checked by hand, non-interactive `claude -p` sessions may not surface Stop-hook rejections the same way an interactive session does — unconfirmed, not investigated further). Don't ship a regex "fix" for a metric like this off a quick grep in either direction; verify structurally or state the gap honestly and stop (#106, avoiding this repo's #80/#91/#96/#88 wrong-stated-cause pattern a sixth time).
- **Verifying a stated Claude Code HARNESS-behavior claim (not our own hook code) when live in-session reproduction is out of reach: `gh issue view <N> --repo anthropics/claude-code --json state,title,body,comments,labels` against the REAL public repo is a legitimate primary source — and you must check whether the cited issue actually CORROBORATES the claim, not just that it exists.** #107: `ci-monitoring.md` had asserted "silently KILLED (SIGTERM) on context compaction" as "confirmed CC behavior", citing `#25188` and `#43944`. Both citations were wrong on inspection — `#25188` closed as a duplicate of a narrower terminal-close scenario (not mid-session auto-compaction), and `#43944` is titled "Background processes … NOT cleaned up on session exit" — the literal OPPOSITE failure mode (leaking/orphaned, never killed), cited BACKWARDS as if it supported "killed". A `WebSearch` for "<product> changelog <behavior> fix <year>" surfaced the actually-relevant, correctly-reproduced issue (`#29193`, "has repro" label): across compaction the OS process usually SURVIVES, but the session's in-process task-handle REGISTRY is dropped and recreated fresh, so the NOTIFICATION LINKAGE is what's lost, not necessarily the process. When a genuine live repro is out of reach inside the ticket's own budget (forcing real mid-session compaction would burn a large amount of context), say so explicitly in the rule text rather than upgrading primary-source research into a false "confirmed live" claim — the honest form is "verdict rests on GitHub issue research, not a live in-session repro", which is itself a legitimate, sourced answer per the #88 "verify the mechanism, don't inherit the ticket's hypothesis" lesson, just reached by document research instead of code fuzzing this time.
- **Splitting a monitoring/wait rule by EXPECTED DURATION, and pairing a background-mechanism recommendation with an explicit RECOVERY step, makes the guidance correct regardless of exactly HOW the failure mode works.** #107's fix doesn't hinge on settling "is it SIGTERM or orphaning" — either way the practical risk is identical (no notification ever arrives), so the rule states: short wait → foreground bounded loop (nothing to recover, unchanged); long wait → ONE background waiter, but ONLY paired with "on your next turn, re-derive status from the DURABLE resource being waited on, and relaunch if the waiter is gone." This is a reusable shape for any future case where the EXACT harness mechanism is genuinely uncertain (undocumented, closed-source, or contested in the primary sources) — write the rule so it holds under either explanation, rather than blocking the fix on resolving the uncertainty.
- **The memory-pressure REAP is a SECOND, distinct death mode for a long-wait background waiter — separate from the #107 compaction-boundary orphaning above, and far more urgent on a memory-tight box (#448, 2026-08-14).** Claude Code CLI (verified in the installed 2.1.232 binary, newer than the ticket's own 2.1.220 cite) registers a `process.on("memoryPressure")` handler for a `Bash(run_in_background:true)` task ONLY when `agentId===undefined` — i.e. **main-session launches only; a subagent-launched bg shell is EXEMPT** (it gets a time cap instead, `CLAUDE_SUBAGENT_BG_SHELL_MAX_MS`). On the event the handler SIGTERM→SIGKILLs any still-`running` main-session bg shell and fires the `task_local_shell_pressure_reap` telemetry — the decompiled guard is `if(i===void 0&&!On()&&!Y.CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_BG_SHELL_PRESSURE_REAP){...process.on("memoryPressure",...)→Se("task_local_shell_pressure_reap"),MTa(...,"killed",...)}`. Unlike compaction (the OS process usually SURVIVES; only the notification handle is orphaned), the reap kills the PROCESS outright and hits in MINUTES: the montalu incident this was hardened from saw **3 of 6 identical `timeout 43200 bash -c 'ssh poll loop'` waiters reaped within ~1–2 min of launch** on a box with ~287 MB free RAM, 9.9 GB swap used, 4 concurrent `claude` processes. STEP 0 re-confirmed the mechanism is present in the installed binary AND that CC's OWN in-session bg-shell help text names it ("On macOS/Linux: memory pressure kills idle bg tasks after 30+ min idle + no running turn"), on a box that itself had 1.4 GB free RAM + 4 GB swap + `earlyoom` running + a live main-session `gh run view` waiter at risk. The recovery is the SAME the #107 fix already prescribes (re-derive from the durable resource on the next turn, relaunch ONE fresh waiter — here it is genuinely re-CREATING a dead process, not re-linking a handle). Two cheap, zero-machinery mitigations, documented as GUIDANCE (no new hook — FREEZE; the nudge-hook family fires only on FOREGROUND polls and `exit 0`s on `run_in_background:true`, so it is the wrong surface for a mechanical aid here): (a) on a pressure-tight box run the long waiter INSIDE a subagent doing FOREGROUND bounded polls, each chunk under the Bash 600 s cap (exempt from the reap, and NOT the banned subagent `run_in_background` CI poll); (b) `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_BG_SHELL_PRESSURE_REAP=1` on the CLI process env (not per-command) restores the documented waiter semantics — a fleet-wide managed-launcher export of it re-enables unbounded resident bg shells on exactly the swap-thrashing boxes CC added the reap to protect — a genuine product decision, now MADE and SHIPPED as the managed-launcher default (#460, user decision 2026-08-14): `CLAUDE_LAUNCH_SCRIPT_CONTENT` exports it for every managed mode except the vanilla `plain` escape hatch, on every box via install/push (the same launcher surface that already bakes `--model`/ultracode). **ROLLBACK CRITERION (recorded, not a promise):** any OOM/pressure incident on any box attributable to the disabled reap (earlyoom kills, an OOM-killer line in dmesg, swap death — dev1 camera-box build pressure is the named risk, its own Tier-0-build-ban enforcement defect tracked separately as #470) reverts the export per-box or globally — a one-line revert (delete the `[ "$mode" = plain ] || export ...` line from `CLAUDE_LAUNCH_SCRIPT_CONTENT`). The named guidance lives in `modules/core/ci-monitoring.md` (tight, cap-constrained), the `verify-launched-work-liveness` skill (full), and its always-on module stub (worker-reachable, #104/#105). **Two CI-poll measurements relocated here from the `ci-monitoring.md` accretion cap (#448):** the `INSIDE ONE Bash call` rule's own count — measured 2026-07-26 on the gatekeeper box, **121 `gh run view` + 116 `gh run list` calls in the MAIN agent**, each re-sending the whole context; and the `,jobs` fail-fast cost — measured via `GH_DEBUG=api`, the run-level-only call is 2 HTTP requests (run + workflow name), adding `,jobs` makes it 3 (one page covers today's largest runs), while a separate second `gh run view --json jobs` call would cost 5.
- **A `compact_boundary` timestamp is when the summarization FINISHED, not when `/compact` ran — subtract `compactMetadata.durationMs` or every timing diagnosis inverts.** #109: the raw send→boundary gaps across 12 real sends were 111–242 s, which reads as "the keystrokes sat queued for two minutes every time". Subtracting `durationMs` (110–150 s — the summarization itself) showed 9 of 11 actually STARTED within ~6 s of the send (genuinely atomic) and only 3 had really been parked (+24 s / +77 s / +98 s). The bug is a 25 % minority, not the norm, and a fix aimed at the raw gap would have been aimed at nothing. Same primitive as the #74 cost bullet above, used for timing rather than tokens.
- **Replay a historical gate decision by feeding the SHIPPED function a transcript truncated to `timestamp <= cutoff`, never by re-implementing its logic in the replay script.** Build a temp `projects/<encode_project_dir(cwd)>/<sid>.jsonl` containing only the entries at or before the decision instant, then call the real `wd._stop_already_rejected` / `wd._compact_not_at_boundary` / `transcript_last_marker` against that dir. This keeps the earlier "a historical replay needs a backward walk with a cutoff filter" rule (those functions read to EOF = today) while closing the prototype-≠-shipped gap that would otherwise let a replay validate logic the repo never ships. Report it BIDIRECTIONALLY on the same table — deferred-and-genuinely-bad, deferred-but-fine (false positives), sent-and-fine (unchanged), sent-but-bad (residual misses) — since a gate that only ever shows "caught N" cannot be distinguished from one that silences everything.
- **A hook regex built from CONSUMING character classes (`[^A-Za-z0-9_]do[^A-Za-z0-9_]`) silently cannot match when two required tokens are separated by a SINGLE character — that one separator would have to serve as both the closing boundary of the first token and the opening boundary of the second.** #111: the ordered poll-loop detector's first green draft matched `do gh run view; sleep 30; done` fine but went quiet on `until [ -s /tmp/x ]; do sleep 5; done` — 243 real polls in the corpus — because the lone space between `do` and `sleep` gets consumed by the `do` boundary and is then unavailable to `sleep`. Every unit test missed it: they all happened to put a command BETWEEN the two tokens, so the shared-separator case never appeared. Two fixes, prefer the second: lookarounds (Python only — `grep -E` has none), or NORMALIZE first (`tr -c 'A-Za-z0-9_' ' ' | tr -s ' '` turns the command into a flat single-space token stream) and write the pattern so each separator is claimed exactly once (` do( .*)? sleep( .*)? done `). The normalization also solves grep's line-orientation for free, since a multi-line command becomes one line. Whenever a new hook pattern requires ≥2 ordered word tokens, hand-test the TIGHT spelling (tokens adjacent, one space) explicitly — it is the case unit tests forget.
- **`grep` inside a HOOK is GNU grep; `grep` in your own Bash tool call is a ugrep shim — so `grep -V` (and any flag test you run interactively) does NOT describe what the hook will do.** #111: `grep -V` reported `ugrep 7.5.0` while `/usr/bin/grep --version` reported `GNU grep 3.11` (`type grep` → "grep is a function", from the user profile the Bash tool's shell loads; a hook running under `bash hook.sh` gets the real binary). This matters most for `-z`, whose meaning is OPPOSITE between them — NUL-separated data in GNU grep, *decompress* in ugrep — so a `grep -Pzq` multiline pattern that tests green interactively is not portable to the hook, let alone to the other managed boxes. Test a candidate hook pattern under `/usr/bin/grep` explicitly (and ideally under both), never under bare `grep`. Portable ERE over a normalized single-line stream avoids the question entirely; reach for `-P` only with a measured reason.
- **Extend the #80 corpus-replay recipe with the OUTCOME ORACLE: pair each `tool_use` with its `tool_result` so the replay reports PRECISION and RECALL, not just "the firing rate went down".** #111: a classifier fix that only reduces firings is indistinguishable from deleting the hook. Walking each transcript once, keeping a `{tool_use_id: input}` map and matching the later `tool_result` entry (its `toolUseResult.stdout/stderr`, plus the two entries' `timestamp` delta for real wall-clock duration) gives a free ground-truth label per command — here "did the harness actually SIGTERM this call" and "did it really run past ~110 s". That turned the report from "983 → 793 nudges" into "983 → 793 while keeping 39 of the 41 calls that genuinely died", and it identified the 6 dropped long calls individually so each could be checked by hand (all bare `sleep`s with no loop = correct drops, not regressions). **Two scaling notes:** pre-filter with a provable SUPERSET of the hook's own gate (here: the command must contain both substrings, since both gate regexes require them) so only ~2 k of 66 k uniques need a subprocess; and if a full-corpus cross-tab is wanted, write a Python MIRROR of the hook's decision and VERIFY it against the shipped script on every candidate (`mismatches 0`) before trusting it for the 77 k-row pass — a mirror that was never diffed against the real hook is just another hypothesis.

- **Proving a flake is FIXED needs a rep count sized to the measured base rate, and the wall-clock delta is the structural tell.** #113: at the observed 1-in-60 failure rate, a clean 60-rep run happens by luck ~37% of the time — it cannot distinguish "fixed" from "got lucky", so the arm was 360 reps per side (before `34e10a5` 350/10 = 2.8%, after 360/0 ≈ 4e-5 by chance). Cheap recipe: a `repeat.py` that imports the test module by path and runs ONE `TestCase(method).run(TestResult())` N times in-process (a fresh child process per rep still exercises the real race, while amortising pytest startup — 360 reps in 92s), driven by a `setsid nohup` runner that starts the load, appends one progress line per rep to a log, and tears the load down in a `trap`. Poll the LOG, never sit in a silent multi-minute foreground call. The decisive evidence was not the pass count but that the fixed version ran at **half** the wall clock (0.31s vs 0.62s per rep): a readiness POLL returns when the event happens, so every banned band-aid (longer sleep, retry, wider timeout) moves that number the WRONG way — which makes it a falsifiable check that the race was removed rather than padded.
- **A heredoc body is not inert, and the reason is `cat > f <<EOF` + run — not just `bash -s`.** #112 proposed stripping heredoc bodies before matching, on the ticket's own figure of "~71 spurious nudges for ~2 real polls". The count replicated (75 on 77,433 calls) but the LABEL did not: categorising the removed set showed only ~6 are prose, while **49** are `cat > poll.sh <<'EOF' … EOF` followed by running that script (`poll_pr.sh`, `wait_map.sh`, `suite_watch.sh`; one ran 120s) and **15** are `ssh host 'bash -s'` / `python3 - <<PY` bodies executing remotely (up to 152s). So the hook is RIGHT on ~64 of the 75, and the discriminator the ticket proposed (strip unless the heredoc's owner is a shell/interpreter) is wrong on its dominant class by construction, since `cat` is not an interpreter yet its body runs. **General lesson for every classifier replay here:** a delta count is not a verdict — the REMOVED set must be characterised item by item, because "the firing rate went down" is indistinguishable from "real detections were deleted". This is the sixth stated-cause in this repo that survived its own unit tests; the corpus + oracle is what caught it.
- **Choosing between a ticket's candidate fixes: look for a SECOND victim of the same root cause — it discriminates in one measurement where reasoning about the reported path cannot.** #114's ticket offered three (daemon flag / arm the timer after a successful bind / cancel it on the failure path) and all three fix the reported bind-failure path. Ctrl-C on a *successfully serving* endpoint turned out to hang identically (rc=0 after 5.93s of a 6s TTL — nothing to do with binding), which instantly rules out the two that are per-exit-path fixes and leaves only the one that fixes the property. Cost: ~20 lines of throwaway script. This is the constructive companion to the repo's recurring "the ticket's stated cause was wrong" lesson — the ticket need not be *wrong* for its framing to be too narrow to pick a fix from.
- **Extending a test module that locks "exactly one `subprocess.Popen(`" (`test_upload_url.py`, #113): SPLIT the existing helper, never add a second start.** #114 needed a server that can bind NOTHING — the readiness poll `_serve()` wraps is meaningless for it (it never serves) while its exit code and timing are the entire contract. Extracting `_spawn()` (Popen + `addCleanup`) and leaving `_serve()` as `_spawn()` + poll keeps the count at one, so the lock still does its job: no call site can grow a private start-and-guess. A test asserting a process's own exit behaviour is a legitimate reason to bypass a readiness wait — but not a reason to bypass the shared launcher.
- **An anti-accumulation test written as a set-diff over the polluted directory can HIDE IN ITS OWN LITTER — assert the exact artefact path as well.** #115's litter lock (`before/after` over `glob("/tmp/airuleset-upload-*.log")`) passed once during the RED run, on a box carrying 1186 leftovers: `_free_port()` happened to return a port whose log file was already in `before`, so the new write produced no new set member. That is the worst possible failure for a leak test — it goes green exactly when the leak is at its worst. Fix: pick a port whose expected path does NOT already exist (loop a bounded number of times), then assert `not legacy.exists()` next to the set diff. Generalises to any "did this run leave a file behind" check whose namespace the bug has already filled.
- **Reproducing a FOREIGN-owned file error without touching another user's files: `chmod 0o000` a file you own, and guard `os.geteuid() == 0`.** #115's live crash was `PermissionError: [Errno 13] ... '/tmp/airuleset-upload-8811.log'`, a montalu-owned leftover on a shared `/tmp`. A test cannot create a file owned by someone else, but a mode-0 file you DO own raises the identical exception at `open(..., "ab")` — with a `skipTest` when running as root, which ignores the mode bits. Companion cleanup discipline for any shared-`/tmp` mess on the managed boxes: `find /tmp -maxdepth 1 -name '<pat>' -user <you> -delete` — never a bare glob `rm`, which would try (and rightly fail) on other users' entries; say plainly which files are left behind and whose they are. And when live-verifying the upload endpoint by hand, send the filename in the shape the page's JS actually sends (`encodeURIComponent`): a raw space in a `curl` URL produces an invalid request line and a bare `http=000`, which reads like a server defect and is not one.

- **`pkill -f '<pattern>'` inside a Claude Code Bash call MATCHES ITS OWN SHELL and kills the call mid-script — every command after it silently never runs.** Hit live tearing down #116's verification endpoint: `pkill -f 'upload_server.py <token>' && … ; rm -rf <dirs>` returned exit 144 with no output, the server WAS killed, and the cleanup that followed on the same line was not — the leftovers only surfaced because the next turn re-checked instead of trusting the exit. The Bash tool's own `-c` command line contains the pattern text, so `-f` (full-command-line match) always finds it. Either drop `-f` and match the process name, bracket a character to break self-match (`upload[_]server.py`), or — best for a teardown — put the kill and the cleanup in SEPARATE calls and verify the state afterwards (`ss -tln | grep :<port>`, `ps -eo pid,args | grep 'upload[_]server'`). The same bracket trick is what makes a `ps | grep` check honest.

- **Proving a browser-encoded value round-trips needs a REAL browser drop, not a curl PUT of what you believe the page sends.** #116's whole bug lived in the gap between the page's `encodeURIComponent` and the server's (missing) decode, so a curl request hand-built from the same assumption could only ever confirm the assumption. Recipe: stage the fixture under `<repo>/.playwright-mcp/<subdir>/` (Playwright MCP refuses any path outside its roots — see the bullet above), `browser_navigate` the LIVE endpoint URL, `browser_click` the drop zone (`#drop` calls `f.click()`, which opens a file chooser the MCP reports as `Modal state: [File chooser]`), then `browser_file_upload` with the absolute path — the page's own JS then builds the request. Verify on BOTH sides: `sha256sum` the fixture against the saved file, and `python3 -c` the raw bytes of the saved name (`repr(n), n.encode()`) rather than eyeballing a terminal rendering, since a mangled and a correct Slovak name can look alike in a listing. Delete the whole `.playwright-mcp/` dir afterwards — it is not meant to be committed.

- **Proving a browser-console fix needs `browser_console_messages` with `all: false` — `all: true` replays the WHOLE session, including the errors from before the fix.** #117: the post-fix call returned the header `Total messages: 0 (Errors: 0, Warnings: 0)` and then printed the pre-fix `favicon.ico` 404 underneath it (that error belonged to the earlier navigation against the unfixed server), which reads exactly like a fix that did not work. `all: false` scopes to the last navigation and is the only form that answers "is the console clean NOW". The `browser_navigate` result header is a second, independent read of the same fact — it says `Console: 1 errors` before and omits the line entirely after.
- **The upload endpoint's log is APPENDED per PORT, not per process (`~/.claude/upload-logs/upload-<port>.log`) — so a before/after count over that file silently mixes runs.** #117: `grep -c favicon` returned **2** on a fixed server that had never been asked for one, because both hits predated the restart on the same port 8799. Any live before/after verification against this log must anchor past the new process's own startup banner (`grep -n 'upload-server: .../<new-token>/'` → `tail -n +<line>`) — the fresh token in the banner is what makes the boundary unambiguous, since the port and dest are identical across restarts. Same trap for the `SAVED` lines.
- **A `data:` URI embedded in an HTML attribute must be percent-encoded and ASCII-only — `#` is the killer.** A data URI with no explicit `;charset` defaults to **US-ASCII**, so a raw emoji or any non-ASCII byte in an inline SVG is out; and a raw `#` (every hex colour, e.g. `fill='#38bdf8'`) starts a URL **fragment**, silently truncating the icon mid-SVG. Encode `#`→`%23`, `<`→`%3C`, `>`→`%3E`, space→`%20`, and keep single quotes inside the SVG so the HTML attribute can stay double-quoted (`urllib.parse.quote(svg, safe="/:='.,-")` produces exactly this). Verify by round-tripping `unquote()` back to the source SVG, then in the browser via `new Image()` with `onload`/`onerror` — a broken favicon does NOT log a console error, so decode success has to be asserted positively rather than inferred from a clean console (#117).
- **A `~/.claude/projects/*/*.jsonl` glob finds ~1% of the transcript corpus — SUBAGENT transcripts live two and four levels deeper.** #108: the corpus is `<project>/<session>.jsonl` (94 files) **plus** `<project>/<session>/subagents/agent-*.jsonl` and deeper (7,964 more, 8,058 total / 1.65 M entries / 4.7 GB on dev1). A two-level glob silently under-samples by 99% and every rate computed from it is wrong; use `glob(..., "**", "*.jsonl", recursive=True)` or `rglob`. Sweep recipe that stays inside a Bash timeout: ONE streaming pass, cheap **substring** pre-filter per raw line (`"hook error:"`, `"Stop hook feedback"`, `'"decision"'`) and `json.loads` only on survivors, writing a compact working-set `.jsonl` to scratch; the whole corpus reduces to ~35 k candidate lines in ~70 s, and every later question is answered against that working set in seconds instead of re-reading 4.7 GB. Print a progress line every N files — a silent multi-minute call gets killed.
- **The transcript shapes that actually mark a hook rejection (#108), since none of them is the shape you would guess.** A **PreToolUse denial** is an entry with `type`/`message.role` = `user` carrying a `tool_result` block with `is_error: true` whose text BEGINS `PreToolUse:<Tool> hook error: [<command>]: <stderr>` — and `<command>` is the whole command line (`bash ~/devel/airuleset/hooks/block-x.sh`), never a bare hook name, which is why a `[hook-name]` bracket regex scores **0 of 1,098**. A **Stop-hook rejection** is `type`/`role` = `user` whose `message.content` is a plain STRING starting `Stop hook feedback:` (1,688 corpus-wide; 931 name their hook in a leading bracket, 757 are bare prose because the hook blocked by RETURNING `{"decision":"block"}` instead of exiting non-zero). **Do not reach for `stop_hook_summary` / `preventedContinuation`:** they are routine per-turn fields on 21,990 entries with `preventedContinuation` **`false` on every one** (reproduces #109 on a larger corpus). And `claude -p` DOES surface Stop-hook rejections — those runs write `entrypoint: "sdk-cli"`, and #108 found the shape in `-p` transcripts *and* in #106's own runs, refuting the standing "maybe headless mode hides them" hypothesis without spending anything.
- **Grepping a transcript for `"decision":"block"` to count hook blocks is exactly backwards — 0 % precision AND 0 % recall.** #108 measured all 258 corpus hits: **none** is a denial (75 % are the model reading a hook's source, 16 % the model *authoring* hook code, the rest attachments/queue entries), while all 1,098 real denials are missed because a genuine denial carries the hook's stderr PROSE (`🚫 BLOCKED:`) and the JSON shape lives only in hook source, docs and settings. The decisive case is unfixable by any regex: a run that *executes the hook* in a repro harness captures a real `{"decision": "block", …}` payload as ordinary Bash stdout — identical bytes to a denial, opposite meaning, separated **only** by `is_error` on the tool_result. Whenever "count how often X fired" is asked of a transcript, parse the JSON structure per line; a match over the concatenated text blob cannot tell a thing that HAPPENED from a thing the model READ or WROTE.
- **Before trusting a classifier's RED→GREEN, run the new test suite against the TEMPTING WRONG FIXES — not just against HEAD.** A red that is only a signature/TypeError mismatch proves nothing about discrimination. #108 wrote both plausible wrong fixes as throwaway functions (a *repaired* bracket regex over the text blob, and the JSON grep) and fed them the same fixtures: the repaired regex counted a decoy-only transcript as a denial, the grep returned nothing for a real denial — so the suite provably rejects both, and GREEN means "picked the right mechanism", not "changed something". Pairs with the #112 rule that a delta count is not a verdict: characterise the changed set item by item (56 hand-checked here) **and** show the suite kills the near-misses.
- **Templating a hook's BLOCK MESSAGE with a multi-line value: use bash `${VAR//__TOKEN__/$VALUE}`, never `sed -e "s|__TOKEN__|$VAR|"`.** #118: the generic-bucket branch substituted a two-line prelude (`RID=$(gh run list …)` + the waiter), and `sed` died with `expression #3, char 78: unterminated 's' command` — a newline in the REPLACEMENT terminates the `s` command. Worse than a visible crash: the hook had already `touch`ed its blocked-state file and written its block log before the message emit, so it exited **1** instead of 2 — the harness treats that as a hook ERROR, the command runs anyway, and the state now says "blocked" for a command that was never actually blocked. Read the heredoc into a variable (`MSG=$(cat <<'EOF' … EOF)`) and use parameter expansion: newline-safe, no escaping, and the replacement is never re-expanded so a literal `$RID` survives intact. Same family as the repo's existing `python3 - "$ARG" <<'PYEOF'` rule — reach for bash's own expansion first when the substitution is plain text.
- **Adding an EXEMPTION to a stateful "first one is free" hook can INCREASE the total block count — report newly-unblocked and newly-blocked as SEPARATE numbers, never a net delta.** #118: exempting mutating commands and short generic waits took the block count 863 → **883**, which reads like the narrowing failed. It did not: an exempt command no longer CONSUMES the free first slot, so the next genuine poll becomes the first (allowed) and the one after it blocks. The honest split was 66 newly unblocked (the wrong-block classes, driven to 0) and 86 newly blocked (genuine repeat polls that previously rode a slot burned by an exempt command — spot-checked as `timeout 580 bash -c 'until …; do sleep 20; done'` waits). Any hook whose state has a consume-a-slot semantic has this coupling; a single net number hides it in both directions.
- **A test built from a corpus specimen can pass because its state KEY differs, not because the fix works — make the specimen collide with the blocked key on purpose.** #118's mutating-action exemption test used a `gh run list`-driven merge command after a block on a specific run-id: different key → generic bucket → free first loop → **green before the fix existed**. It only became a real test once rewritten to name the SAME run-id as the block. Whenever a hook's decision is keyed (session, run, path, repo), a new test must state which key it lands in and pick the one that exercises the branch — otherwise the assertion is about the keying, not the behaviour.
- **Replaying only the CANDIDATE commands validates the detector; replaying EVERY command of the affected sessions is what exposes a BACKSTOP's false positives.** #118 stage 1 fed the 6,280 loop-shape commands through the hook and looked clean. Stage 2 fed all **56,038** commands of the 185 sessions that had produced a block — the only way the post-block backstop's inputs (non-loop commands) are ever seen — and found 112 of its 139 firings were post-mortems (`gh run view <id> --json jobs --jq 'select(.conclusion=="failure")'` then `--log-failed`), i.e. blocking the debugging of a red run. Extract the affected session files from stage 1's own output and re-scan just those; it is a small, cheap second pass. Pair it with the tool_use→tool_result duration oracle (#111) for the "waste actually eliminated" figure — 939 blocked polls carried **89.0 measured hours** of foreground wall clock, median 375 s.
- **`gh run view` DENSITY is not a waiting signal — it measures verbosity.** The intuitive backstop "two or more `gh run view` in one command must be a poll" is wrong on its dominant real class: a run that FINISHED is inspected with several views in one command (status, then jobs, then `--log-failed`). Key a wait-detector on the SLEEP instead, which is the only thing that actually costs wall clock; on the same corpus that took the backstop from 139 firings to 55, all of them genuine `sleep 300 && gh run view` continued waits, while 4,394 single/diagnostic view calls stayed free. This is the #112 lesson in a new costume: the removed set has to be characterised item by item, because "the firing rate went down" and "real detections were deleted" look identical in a count.

- **`jq`'s `has("field")` is TRUE for an explicit `null`, and iterating a null or non-array yields NOTHING — so a "prove zero" gate built on `has()` + `length == 0` FIRES on a payload that proved nothing.** #121: `notify-compact-subagent-boundary.sh` must only compact when the SubagentStop payload's `background_tasks` contains zero non-self entries. The first cut gated on `has("background_tasks")` and then `[.background_tasks[]? | select(...)] | length`; `background_tasks: null` and `background_tasks: "notalist"` both sailed through as "zero live workers" and fired, which is the exact guess the hook exists to prevent. Gate on `(.background_tasks | type) == "array"` instead — presence is not shape. Whenever a hook's decision is "the absence of evidence in field X", the type check is part of the gate, not a nicety.
- **Unit tests written from the payload shape you EXPECT will not find that bug — enumerate the degenerate shapes explicitly in a throwaway smoke pass.** The #121 suite had 13 hook tests, all modelling well-formed arrays (self-only, sibling, pending, shell, absent), and all green while both `null` and non-array fired. A ~20-line script looping `run(payload, label)` over null / non-array / object / absent / missing-`id` / null-`id` / numeric-`id` / garbage-stdin / empty-stdin found it in one call. Do that pass for any new hook BEFORE `push`, then promote the shapes it breaks on into real RED tests (#121 did: `65514b1` → `45a4898`).
- **`background_tasks` needs OPPOSITE handling depending on WHO is asking, and the `/tmp/airuleset-bgtasks-*` ledger does not apply to the session-level question at all.** The #29 ownership problem — the array is SESSION-WIDE, so a stopping SUBAGENT must intersect it with its own launch ledger or siblings deadlock it — is about an AGENT asking "what do *I* own". A hook asking "does this SESSION still have anything live" needs no ledger: the array already *is* that session's task registry, and the only filter required is dropping the self entry at `id == agent_id`. Reaching for the ledger there finds nothing, because `post-record-subagent-bg-launch.sh` deliberately never records main-session launches ("the supervisor pattern — never recorded"). Both #121 hooks then agree by construction: `subagent-stop-check-bg-work.sh` blocks a stop exactly when live OWNED tasks exist, and every such task is a non-self entry, so whenever it blocks, the compact hook has already deferred.
- **A hook whose INPUT never appears in the corpus is still corpus-replayable — RECONSTRUCT the payload from transcript-derived state and pipe it to the real script.** Hook payloads are not persisted anywhere, so #121 looked unverifiable. But the fields the gate reads are derivable: walk each parent transcript for `Agent`/`Task` tool_use with `input.subagent_type == "autopilot-worker"` (launch id from `toolUseResult.agentId` or the `"Async agent launched … agentId: X"` tool_result string) and for task-notification entries carrying BOTH `<task-id>` and `<status>` (completion); the outstanding set at each completion instant IS the `background_tasks` array. Feeding 601 reconstructed payloads to `bash hooks/notify-compact-subagent-boundary.sh` with a fresh scratch `$HOME` per event gave a real bidirectional verdict (176/176 fire at zero-outstanding, 0/425 fire with work in flight, 0/60 on non-`autopilot-worker` types) without reimplementing a line of the gate.
- **`MANAGED_MODEL`'s `[1m]` suffix is a CLI-level ALIAS that NEVER appears in a transcript's `message.model` — never compare one against the other.** A session launched `--model "claude-opus-5[1m]"` writes `message.model = "claude-opus-5"`; across every real transcript on dev1 the string `claude-opus-5[1m]` occurs **zero** times in ~119k model entries. Watchdog job 23 compared `transcript_first_model` (the API id) against `MANAGED_MODEL` (the alias), so its "is this session stale?" condition was PERMANENTLY TRUE for every session on every box — it restarted panes whose model was already current, including `claude-opus-5 -> claude-opus-5[1m]` (#132). Any future model comparison must normalise both sides to what the API actually reports, or compare a launch FLAG against a launch FLAG — never one of each. Check cheaply with `find ~/.claude/projects -name '*.jsonl' -mtime -7 | xargs grep -ho '"model":"[^"]*"' | sort | uniq -c`.

- **A watchdog dedup keyed on SESSION ID is structurally defeated by anything that ends the session** — the replacement gets a new session id, so the claim that was meant to stop a retry is looked up under a key that no longer exists and the job fires again immediately. #132: this is what turned job 23's permanently-true condition into an unbounded restart LOOP, and why the pz-server pane was hit twice in three minutes by two different jobs. Any state whose purpose is "never do this twice to the same thing" must be keyed on something that OUTLIVES the action (the pane id, the cwd, the repo), never on an identifier the action itself replaces.

- **A live verification with no CONTROL ARM proves nothing, and this repo has already shipped one.** #70 "verified" job 18 by restarting a session and confirming the newly deployed hook then fired — true, and vacuous, because it never asked whether the hook would have fired WITHOUT the restart. It would have: hooks are re-read per event (the bullet below). Two years of that job's existence rested on the missing arm. Whenever a verification is of the shape "I did X, then Y worked", the mandatory companion is "and without X, Y did NOT work" — otherwise the result is consistent with X being a no-op.

- **Proving a fix RED once it is already committed: `git checkout HEAD~N -- <impl files>`, run, then `git checkout HEAD -- <impl files>` — `git stash push` is a silent no-op on a clean tree.** Reaching for the documented stash technique (used for pre-commit RED proofs elsewhere in this file) AFTER committing stashes nothing, the test runs against the NEW code, passes, and reads as "the replay does not reproduce the bug" when it was never given the buggy code (#132, hit live). The tell is `git stash pop` reporting "No stash entries found" — treat that as an invalidated run, not a passing one.

- **Running the REAL `run_once` against the REAL panes without any risk: pass a `run` proxy that executes read-only tmux subcommands for real and refuses + records everything else.** `list-panes`/`display-message`/`capture-pane` go through to `wd._default_run`; anything containing `send-keys` is appended to a list and never executed. Combined with `dry_run=True`, a scratch `state_path`, and a no-op `send_fn`, this is the strongest available evidence about a keystroke-sending change — it exercises the shipped code path against genuine live pane state, and it is bidirectional: check out the pre-fix implementation and the SAME probe shows what the old code would have done to the SAME panes at the SAME instant. #132 used exactly this to show the pre-fix code still had the user's own airuleset session queued for a restart, held off only by the transient `skip bg-agent` guard, while the fixed code proposed nothing.

- **A deliberate `systemctl --user stop api-watchdog.timer` does NOT survive a deploy unless `~/.claude/api-watchdog.disabled` exists.** `setup_watchdog_service()` ends in `systemctl enable --now`, and every `airuleset.py push` runs `install` — so a fleet-wide emergency stop silently reverts on the next push by anyone. #132 found the timer live on all 6 boxes the morning after it was stopped as an incident mitigation, with a restart-capable job actively evaluating live panes. Touch the marker to make a stop stick; delete it to hand control back to `install`. When stopping the fleet for an incident, set the marker on every box in the same pass as the stop, and re-verify with `systemctl --user is-active` afterwards rather than trusting the stop command's own exit code.

- **A hook added to `~/.claude/settings.json` mid-session DOES take effect in that already-running session — registration is re-read live, not snapshotted at session start (#123, measured 2026-07-28; INDEPENDENTLY RE-MEASURED and extended in #132 the same day — an entry REMOVED mid-session also stops firing immediately, so the config is genuinely re-read per event in both directions, and watchdog job 18, which existed solely on the opposite premise, was deleted).** This is the fact the whole "is the fix even active" class of question turns on, and the intuition is wrong in the safe direction, so measure it rather than assume: the probe is a **2×2**, and only the 2×2 is conclusive. Arm (a) a brand-new hook ENTRY appended to `settings.json` and (b) a brand-new LINE inside an ALREADY-registered hook SCRIPT, both writing to their own marker file, then trigger the event. (b) is the control — it fires whenever the event fires at all, so a null result on (a) means "snapshotted" instead of "the event never happened". Measured in a session started 36 h before the edit: (a) 2/2, (b) 3/3. Repeat it on the SPECIFIC event you care about (a `SubagentStop` capture hook appended at 09:54:58 caught a subagent's stop at 09:55:08) — do not generalise from `PostToolUse` to `SubagentStop` for free. Back up `settings.json` (`cp -p`) and restore it byte-identically with a `sha256sum` check; `airuleset.py push` rewrites the file anyway, so any hand edit is transient by construction.
- **`SubagentStop` fires once per PARALLEL TOOL-CALL BRANCH, not only per dispatched subagent — and those payloads carry `agent_type: ""` (#123, live-captured).** Two consequences. (1) Volume: the `agent_type != <the one you want>` class outnumbers the interesting one by 2–3 orders of magnitude (four `""` payloads inside three minutes on one session), so any per-decision logging in a SubagentStop hook needs the high-volume class THROTTLED (a heartbeat gated on a marker file's `stat` mtime — O(1), no read-modify-write, concurrency-safe) while the interesting class stays unconditional. (2) Shape: the ephemeral branch agent is NOT in `background_tasks` (only genuinely dispatched tasks are), whereas a dispatched agent's own self entry IS present at its own stop — so the self-entry exclusion at `id == agent_id` is still required and still sufficient. The branch payload also carries `effort: {level: …}`, and `last_assistant_message` is the Bash tool's `description` string, which is how you identify what it was.
- **A guard whose SUCCESS artefact is deleted on success has THREE states collapsing onto one observation, not two — enumerate them before concluding "it never ran".** #123's ticket framed it as never-ran vs declined-every-time; the third state was ran-fired-and-delivered, because `cmd_compact_request` clears the request the instant `deliver_compact_now` succeeds, so an empty `compact-requests.json` is equally consistent with a perfect fire. Whenever a mechanism's only trace is consumed by its own success path, "no trace" is not evidence of anything. Companion trap in the same ticket: the ticket's own evidence `grep "subagent-stop\|origin" compact-sync.log → zero hits` was a NULL TEST — `_compact_log` never emits either word, so that grep could not have matched even on a successful delivery. Check that a cited grep CAN match before treating its emptiness as a finding.
- **A hook's decision log makes the classifier auditable in the field; report the RESULT WORD of anything it shells out to, not just its own verdict.** #123 records `result=<recorded|delivered|dup|skip|error>` from `cmd_compact_request`, which the hook previously discarded with `>/dev/null 2>&1` — an accepted boundary dropped DOWNSTREAM was otherwise as invisible as a declined one. Field-proof shape for such a log: a real boundary of the type under test produced `DECLINE reason=live-tasks n=1` and an unrelated session produced `RECORD result=delivered` eight seconds later, which is the first evidence the mechanism fires at all. Note the residual ambiguity that finding surfaced (#125): `delivered` is truthy-return, so it also covers a downstream `DROP` — pair a `RECORD` line with `compact-sync.log` at the same timestamp for the actual disposition.
- **A reconstructed-payload corpus replay validates the CLASSIFIER but cannot be trusted for a RATE — measure the reconstruction's leak and report a BRACKET.** #121's method (playbook bullet above) treats the outstanding launch set at each completion instant as `background_tasks`; a launch whose completion notification never appears therefore stays outstanding forever and biases every later event toward "work in flight". Measured on 8,098 transcripts: **33.8%** of ids appearing in registries never complete, and replaying the same 608 worker events raw vs de-leaked brackets the fire rate at **0.7%–94.1%** — useless as a rate, decisive as a classifier check (9,892 events, 0 mismatches, 0 non-zero exits, 0 stdout). Extend the reconstruction to background Bash launches (`toolUseResult.backgroundTaskId`) and EVERY `subagent_type`, not just the one under test: the live registry contains shells and other agent types, so a single-type reconstruction is a strict subset of reality and can only over-report fires (#121's own 176/601 vs this 4/608 is that difference, not a regression). State the bracket; let the deployed decision log answer the rate.
- **A mutation-based "teeth" test must target a string the shipped script still contains — pin the mutation to the GATE's semantics, not its formatting.** #123's widened-accept mutant was written as `s.replace('[ "$OTHERS" = "0" ] || exit 0', …)` and stopped applying the moment the same gate grew a `|| { … }` block, failing with "the mutation did not apply" — which is the mutant harness working correctly (it refuses to certify teeth against a script it did not actually mutate), but only because `_mutant` asserts the replacement changed something. Always make that assertion, and prefer the shortest stable fragment (`'[ "$OTHERS" = "0" ] ||'` → `'true ||'`). Verify the mutant BY HAND once and record both outputs — real script `DECLINE reason=live-tasks n=1` vs widened mutant `RECORD result=error` — since an `assertRaises(AssertionError)` also passes when the mutant simply logs NOTHING, i.e. for the wrong reason.

- **A heredoc feeding a command substitution must have its BODY INSIDE the `$( … )`, not after the closing paren** — `VAR=$(cmd <<'EOF') || VAR=""` followed by the body parses as an unterminated here-document: bash prints `warning: command substitution: N unterminated here-document` to **stderr on every call** and still produces the right answer, so every unit test passes. Write `VAR=$(cmd 2>/dev/null <<'EOF'` … `EOF` … `) || VAR=""`. Caught only by #118's own `assertEqual(out.stderr.strip(), "")` on an ALLOWED command — worth adding that assertion to any hook test harness, because a hook that pollutes stderr while exiting 0 is otherwise invisible (#124).
- **A hook LIBRARY (`hooks/lib-*.sh`, sourced never executed) has NO shebang and NO `set -euo pipefail`** — re-declaring shell options reaches into every caller, and `pre-write-script-check.sh` only demands `set -euo pipefail` from a new `.sh` that HAS a shebang, so omitting the shebang is both correct and the way past the gate (never the `# airuleset:script-ok` bypass). Source it with `. "$(cd "$(dirname "${BASH_SOURCE[0]}")" 2>/dev/null && pwd)/lib-x.sh"`, and make any mutant-test harness copy the WHOLE `hooks/` dir (`for f in HOOKS.glob("*.sh")`) so a mutated hook still resolves its library.
- **Prefer a classifier rule whose safety does NOT rest on an exhaustive enumeration.** #124's first draft decided "is this written file executed here?" by enumerating runners (`bash <path>`, `./path`, `chmod +x`) and therefore called `sshpass ssh host 'bash -s' < /tmp/x.sh` inert — three real corpus specimens that ran 119 s, 135 s and 183 s, because that shape names no interpreter beside the path. Replacing it with "the file is inert only if EVERY later mention of it is a text-file argument" needs no list of ways a file can be executed and took the changed set from 48 to 8. When a draft rule's correctness depends on having listed every case, that is the tell to invert it.
- **Replay a STATEFUL hook in real session order before concluding it wrong-blocks — an artificial N×-repeat over-states the blast radius.** #119: replaying each documentation write 4× inside one synthetic session produced 30 blocks and read as a serious false-positive class; the same corpus replayed in true per-session order (own state dir per session, #118's `replay1.py` method) produced 0 `git add` blocks and exactly 1 file-write-shaped block, which turned out to write a real 30-minute curl poller. The repeat pattern a session actually performs is the thing being measured; a synthetic repeat measures the fixture.
- **A branch-message test must assert the CLAIM, not a word.** #119's generic branch legitimately says "raises no task-notification", so `assertNotIn("notification", err)` forbade the exact sentence that made the branch honest. Name each branch's load-bearing phrase (`"harness ALREADY wakes you"` / `"Nothing will wake you"`) and assert each ABSENT from the other branch — strictly stronger than a word check, since a single-message hook then fails in both directions. Same family as the "a lock test will match its own prose" rule above.
- **Known residual of `hooks/lib-poll-payload.sh`, both measured and deliberate:** (a) a loop quoted inside an INTERPRETER heredoc (`python3 - <<PY`) still fires, because that body genuinely executes — it hard-blocked a probe during #124 itself, and telling it apart would need to parse the payload's language; (b) `cat >> docs/autopilot-log.md <<'EOF' … EOF && git add docs/autopilot-log.md` still fires, because the path reappears outside a text-sink flag (5 unique corpus commands; 0 of them ever produced a real BLOCK, so the rule was left conservative rather than widened on speculation). Both are nudges in practice. Do not "fix" either without a replay that separates written-and-run from inert.
- **Corpus replay and adversarial review catch DISJOINT classes — a classifier change needs both, and the replay passing is not evidence the review would.** #119/#124: an 8,107-transcript replay through the real scripts reported 8 newly-silent / 0 newly-firing and looked settled; an adversarial read of the same diff then found FOUR ways a value-based `str.replace` deleted live control flow (a flag value that is also a loop token — `until grep -F "done" log; do sleep 30; done` lost its own `done`; a whole-command git/gh gate that licensed stripping `ssh -t '<loop>'`; two byte-identical heredoc bodies judged from one verdict; a here-string `<<<'x'` whose never-closed body swallowed the command). Re-running the full replay AFTER the fixes gave byte-identical numbers — the four holes were shapes the corpus happens not to contain. So: **make every edit POSITIONAL (line index for bodies, character offset for quoted spans, spliced in reverse), never by value**, and treat "the replay is clean" as covering only the shapes that occurred, never the shapes that could.

- **A corpus replay is only as good as its assumed corpus — for anything that scans REPOS, the corpus is `$HOME`, not `~/devel`.** #138: job 24's replay over all 29 git checkouts in `~/devel` was clean and would have shipped a false-positive class. The live watchdog found it in **six minutes**: `~/varos/eft5000`, a GitLab repo whose `origin/master` last moved 2019-09-07 (2,515 days) while real delivery goes to `develop-50`, which took a merge the same day the alert fired. Its `origin/HEAD` is unset, so a base-ref fallback picks a branch abandoned in 2019 and correctly reports 3,248 commits permanently "undelivered". Enumerate with `find "$HOME" -maxdepth 4 -type d -name .git -not -path '*/node_modules/*'` (40 repos here vs 29) — the directory you *think* holds the projects is a guess, and the replay inherits it. Companion design lesson: **a one-ended threshold cannot separate "stopped receiving" from "was never a target"** — from below, 2,515 days and 17 days are the same shape — so bound such a window on BOTH ends. The upper bound is free when the alert is a daily re-ping: anything that reaches it has already pinged every day on the way there.
- **The api-watchdog executing the working tree every 60 s makes LIVE DEPLOYMENT the strongest verification available, not merely a hazard to work around.** The existing bullet above frames "an uncommitted edit is in production the moment you save" as a caution; #138 is the other half — a new job wired into `run_once` starts producing real journal lines and real state-file entries on the very next tick, so `journalctl --user -u api-watchdog.service | grep <job-tag>` plus `~/.claude/api-watchdog-state.json` give a genuine production readout minutes after writing it, against real repos and real panes no fixture can model. Verify the FIX the same way and insist on a bidirectional read: after #138's bound landed, the false-positive repo vanished from BOTH the journal and the state file (self-healing, since a non-candidate is popped) while the true positive kept logging every sweep and correctly did NOT re-ping inside its dedup window. A job whose detections log unconditionally (issue #36) but whose ping is deduped gives you both signals for free — never add a job whose only trace is the ping.
- **A source-slicing lock test must use `inspect.getsource`, never a hand-rolled slice to the next `\ndef `.** #138's detection-only lock ("this job must never contain `send-keys`") sliced from the function's `def` to the next top-level `def` — which, for a function followed by a long module-level constants block, runs straight into the NEXT job's section comment, and that comment legitimately discusses keystroke delivery. The test failed on prose belonging to another job. This is the "a lock test will match its own prose" trap (above) in its neighbour-prose form: bound the region with the tool that knows where the function ends.
- **`pre-write-script-check.sh` blocks `except Exception: pass` in new Python — the fix is a documented sentinel RETURN, not the `# airuleset:script-ok` bypass.** #138's probe wanted to swallow a failed `git fetch` and carry on. Returning a small dict instead (`{"fetch_error": repr(e)}`) satisfies the gate, keeps the caller's contract, and — the real win — forces you to write down what the degraded path actually DOES: here, that without the fetch the caller re-reads the same local refs so its verdict simply stands, i.e. the feature degrades to a local-only heuristic rather than going silent. The hook is pushing for exactly that sentence; treat it as a prompt to decide the degradation deliberately, not an obstacle.

- **Enforcing an agent action needs BOTH an in-band gate and an out-of-band reconciler — neither alone covers the failure set.** A `SubagentStop` gate is the only mechanism that restores a REAL card (the worker's own plain-Slovak text) but is structurally blind to a worker that DIED mid-run (it never stops) and to a delivery that failed after the worker returned. A watchdog reconciler sees both but can only report a gap, never fill it well. Ship both, and make the gate bounded (one block per session+issue) so a worker that genuinely cannot deliver finishes rather than wedging — the reconciler is what makes that safe to allow.
- **`last_assistant_message` on a `SubagentStop` payload is a plain STRING carrying the subagent's final message verbatim** (live-captured 2026-07-28 via the documented arm-a-capture-hook-then-restore recipe). So a gate that judges a worker's evidence block needs no transcript parsing at all and is immune to the async-transcript-write race (#28). Full key set observed: `agent_id`, `agent_transcript_path`, `agent_type`, `background_tasks`, `cwd`, `hook_event_name`, `last_assistant_message`, `permission_mode`, `prompt_id`, `session_crons`, `session_id`, `stop_hook_active`, `transcript_path`.
- **Replaying a STATEFUL hook must isolate the state PER ARM, or the second arm is deduped by the first and reports zero.** #134's gate blocks once per (session, issue) and writes `/tmp/airuleset-runcard-gate-<sid>`. Re-running the replay with the same synthetic session ids took arm A from 179 blocks to 0 — which reads exactly like a broken classifier and cost a real debugging detour before the harness was suspected. A per-arm `uuid` nonce in the session id, plus cleanup of the nonce's own state files, makes each arm an independent measurement. Same family as #119's "replay in real session order" rule, one level more specific.
