- **A `blocked()` helper in a hook test must match the repo's actual block contract (`{"decision":"block"}` on stdout, exit 0), never "any non-zero exit".** During a RED run the hook file does not exist, `bash <missing>` exits 127, and the looser helper reports the gate's positive tests as PASSING before a line of the gate is written. Red for the wrong reason is the same defect as green for the wrong reason, and it is much easier to miss because the number of failures still looks healthy.
- **When two candidate classifier rules score identically on the corpus, prefer the one built on POSITIVE evidence over the one built on an enumeration of negatives.** #134's merge detector first required the sha to START the `merge_sha:` value, which missed 6 real merges that write one sha PER ISSUE (`#133 = 2223f12 ; #134 = 4d56813`). Two widenings both scored 213/213 with 0 false positives: "a hex token anywhere, unless the value starts with NOT MERGED / n/a / STOPPED / …", and "a hex token attached to an issue number". The second shipped — the first's safety rests on that negative list staying complete, so a new refusal phrasing that happened to carry a sha would make the gate demand a card for a ticket that never merged.
- **Live deployment beat both the unit tests and the corpus replay, again — and this time it invalidated the design's own stated reasoning.** Job 25 shipped arguing `gh` was unnecessary because "the `Closes #N` in a merge commit is the same fact, free, and local". True for airuleset and parovanie-produktov; flatly wrong for **tvdole**, which had 20 commits in the 48h window and ZERO `Closes` trailers in its last 200 commits (its issues close from the PR body, a GitHub-side fact no local read can see) — and tvdole is one of the two repos in the ticket's own evidence, the one that has NEVER produced a card. The watchdog runs the working tree every 60 s, so the gap appeared in `journalctl` within minutes of saving the file. Bound the fallback to the exact signature that needs it (fresh merges on the base branch AND zero trailers) and the cost discipline the local-only design was built around survives intact.
- **Check a "did X get reported?" detector for a POSITIVE control before believing its negatives.** Job 25's first live output was "airuleset n=20 unreported", which is indistinguishable from a broken marker lookup until you also see `carded=16` on the same repo. Print both halves (`closed48h=36 carded=16 unreported=20`); a detector that reports every item as missing is the most common shape of a silently broken key.

- **A "resolve only on EVIDENCE, never on a timer" invariant still needs a BOUND — check whether every evidence path can be unavailable AT ONCE, because that is the state it will wedge in.** #78/#72 built the `/compact` claim to resolve only via CONSUMED (a `compact_boundary` newer than the send) or FAILED (process gone / session id replaced), explicitly "never a timer". Each path is individually correct and the trio has a common blind spot: a session whose process stays alive, whose id never changes (`claude -c` continues the same transcript), and whose queued `/compact` never drains (CC drains type-ahead only at an ACCEPTED Stop, #84) satisfies none of them ever. Measured 2026-07-28: 21h26m of refused boundaries on montalu@subdev to 346,944 tokens, ended by the USER hand-compacting; forestshop@dev1 the same signature at ~500K. The fix is a TTL evaluated AFTER the evidence paths, so proof still wins and the timer only ends what nothing can prove — and its safety argument is measured, not asserted: 30 min is ~18x the worst HEALTHY delivery latency in this repo's own data (#109: 9 of 11 sends started within ~6 s, worst park +98 s), and a second guard (`_pane_has_queued_compact`) already sits at both send points so a genuinely-parked `/compact` is skipped rather than typed twice. When writing the next "never on a timer" rule, write down which state makes ALL its evidence unreachable and what ends it there.
- **Bidirectional replay where only ONE arm can accept an injected `now`: SHIFT every real timestamp by `now - cutoff` instead of trying to inject.** The pre-fix arm is by definition the old signature, so a new `now=` parameter cannot be passed to it. Shifting the claim `ts` and every transcript entry by the same delta preserves each real INTERVAL exactly while letting both arms evaluate against wall-clock now; only the absolute epoch moves, which nothing under test reads. Pair it with the existing truncate-at-cutoff rule above — a to-EOF reader otherwise reports TODAY's boundary and silently resolves the very claim you are replaying (hit live on the first run of #140's replay: the wedge case came back "released" in BOTH arms because the 2026-07-28 boundary that ENDED the wedge was in the fixture).
- **Read the KEPT set of a classifier change, not only the CHANGED set — the strongest control is usually already in the corpus.** #140's replay over 22 real `SEND`→next-event pairs from dev1's own `compact-sync.log` released 3 (gaps of 944m, 82m, 37m) and KEPT 2 — and both kept cases were at a gap under 30 seconds, i.e. exactly the rapid double-fire #78 was built to prevent. That is a positive control drawn from real data at zero cost, and it answers "did the fix delete the feature" far better than any synthetic case. Add the DISCRIMINATION control next to it: rerun with the new mechanism disabled (`AIRULESET_COMPACT_CLAIM_TTL_S=0`) and require the arms to agree on every case — that proves the new path is the ONLY behavioural change, so every other release is still evidence-driven.
- **Live-proving a DETECTION-only watchdog job when no candidate currently exists: wrap the job in a delegating probe and call the REAL `run_once` with a read-only `run` proxy.** `m.patch.object(wd, "<job>", probe)` where `probe` records its arguments and then calls the real function, plus `dry_run=True`, a scratch `state_path`, and a `run` that executes read-only tmux subcommands for real but refuses anything containing `send-keys`. #140's job 26 was shown reachable in production wiring — invoked once, handed the real map of all 7 live sessions, zero keystrokes attempted — while correctly reporting nothing, because every outstanding claim on the box genuinely had a newer boundary. Do NOT reach for a lowered threshold to force a positive: with `AIRULESET_COMPACT_STALL_S=1` the job stayed (correctly) silent, since the gate that excluded every candidate was the boundary check, not the age check — a lowered-threshold run that stays quiet proves nothing either way.
- **On the subdev box the managed users SHARE a PID namespace, so `ps -eo … | grep claude` from one user's ssh lists OTHER users' sessions — resolve ownership through the parent chain before attributing anything.** While verifying #140 the only `claude` visible from montalu's shell turned out to be marek's: `ps -o args -p <ppid-of-its-ppid>` printed `tmux new-session -d -s marek …`. Attributing it to montalu would have produced a confident, wrong conclusion about which session was alive. The reliable ownership question is `tmux list-panes -a` **as that user** plus the process's own ancestry, never a bare box-wide grep.

- **`IFS=$'\t' read -r ...` in bash still COLLAPSES consecutive tab delimiters — tab is one of the "IFS whitespace" characters (space/tab/newline) regardless of what you explicitly set `IFS` to, so an EMPTY field between two tabs silently vanishes and every field after it shifts left by one.** Hit live building `block-ungated-issue-filing.sh` (#137): a Python-to-bash handoff printed `verdict\trepo\ttitle\t\tsid` (empty criterion field) and the bash `while IFS=$'\t' read -r VERDICT REPO TITLE CRIT LOGSID` loop silently read `CRIT=sid` and `LOGSID=""`. Fix: never emit an empty tab-separated field from the producer side — substitute a non-empty sentinel (`"none"`) instead of `""`. This generalizes to any NEW hook that hands multi-field data from an embedded Python heredoc back to its wrapping bash script via a delimited line.
- **A hook that classifies REAL user-authored text (an issue title, a commit subject) cannot reuse `block-gh-invalid-json-flag.sh`'s plain-regex `SEGMENTS_RE.split()` top-level command splitter** — that shape is an accepted, DOCUMENTED limitation there because its job is narrower (matching `--json` near a `gh` write subcommand). A REAL issue title routinely contains a bare `;`/`|` (camera-box #827's own title has one), which the naive split treats as a command separator and corrupts the following segment's flag parsing. Any hook whose whole point is reading a real title/body needs a QUOTE-AWARE top-level splitter (walk the string once, track single/double-quote state, only split outside quotes) — see `split_top_level()` in `hooks/block-ungated-issue-filing.sh` (#137), reusable verbatim for the next such hook.
- **`gh api`'s `-f`/`-F`/`--field`/`--raw-field` flags mean `key=value` (typed vs raw field), completely unlike `gh issue create`'s `-F`/`--body-file`, which means "the value is a FILE PATH."** The two commands overload the SAME flag letters for different semantics — a hook resolving an issue body must branch on `tk[1] == "api"` vs `tk[1] == "issue"` before deciding whether a `-F` value is a filename to read or a `key=value` pair to split on `=`. Found writing `block-ungated-issue-filing.sh`'s `resolve_body()` (#137).
- **Deciding whether a NEW `REMOTE_HOSTS` entry is safe to register with NO `identity` field (i.e. relying on the default-key/sshpass fallback): check watchdog job 16's exposure, not just `cmd_push`'s.** #406/#408 (2026-08-12, forestshop-dev + spinbike-vps): `cmd_push`'s deploy loop is the ONE-TIME-per-run exposure most REMOTE_HOSTS reasoning implicitly considers ("worst case, the operator's next push fails cleanly for that one host"). But `_watchdog_fleet_fetch` (job 16, `fleet_burn_job`, dev1-only, hourly) ALSO walks the FULL `REMOTE_HOSTS` list every UTC hour via the identical `_remote_ssh_prefix()` fallback (`sshpass -p newlevel ssh ...` when no `identity` is set) — so an entry with no identity and no working default-key auth gets the SHARED dev1/dev2 password sent to it roughly once per hour, INDEFINITELY, not just once at the next push. This is bounded (`NumberOfPasswordPrompts=1` caps each attempt to one try; `_fleet_remote_row`/`_watchdog_fleet_fetch` never raise, degrading to `{"error": ...}` on a bad host) but is a real, silent, recurring exposure against a box this repo does not own whenever the default-key assumption for a NEW no-identity entry turns out to be wrong. When registering a target whose default-key auth has NOT been independently verified live (as opposed to, e.g., forestshop-dev's `admin@` account, which had direct sibling-project evidence), post a durable ticket comment flagging this BEFORE the supervisor's first push/merge rather than only reasoning about the push-time cost — a wrong guess here is not "a one-line fix next time it's noticed", it is an unattended recurring probe against a third party.

- **A single `subprocess.TimeoutExpired`/`rc=-1` in a corpus replay is environment noise, not a classifier bug — confirm by isolating the ONE specimen before touching anything.** #129's re-verification of #136's design-gate replay hit exactly one mismatch (`rc_on=-1`) out of 969 real commits on a box that was concurrently running a 76 s pytest pass — re-invoking the real hook standalone with the SAME commit message 5x in a row came back `rc=2` (correctly blocked) every time at 0.12-0.16 s, nowhere near the script's 15 s subprocess timeout, and a full clean re-run of the whole replay reported 0/969 mismatches. The diagnostic is cheap and general for ANY corpus-replay script that shells out per specimen: extract the one offending input, re-run it standalone several times outside the loop, and only escalate to "real bug" if it reproduces.
- **The design-before-code gate (`block-commit-without-design.sh`, #136) is deliberately scoped to `agent_type == "autopilot-worker"` — a main/human session referencing an issue in a commit is NEVER mechanically gated, by design, and that is correct, not a residual gap.** #129's own re-close verified this live (worker session → real `exit 2` block; identical commit from a session with no `agent_type` → real `exit 0` pass) and confirmed it is unit-tested by name (`test_main_session_no_agent_type_is_never_gated`). This repo's own development is dominated by direct interactive main-session commits (see the rest of this file) — gating those would be a materially larger, more invasive change than the ticket that asked for the gate, and would violate the ticket's own explicit safety requirement ("must not block ordinary commits that have nothing to do with an issue"). Don't re-litigate this scope in a future ticket without new evidence that the WORKER population itself is still non-compliant.
- **Proving a rule-module row is "already hook-covered" (before trimming it, #95 item 8): invoke the ACTUAL hook with a real STDIN payload against EACH distinct quoted alternative in the row separately — never one sentence built by concatenating all the alternatives, and never reasoning from the regex source alone.** Concatenating alternatives risks one clause dragging in an unrelated match that masks another clause's real non-match; reading the regex source alone risks exactly the coincidental-substring trap this ticket hit: a row about self-invented technical obstacles ("investigate the cause / ship an estimate / show n/a — your call?") looked hook-covered because the phrase happens to END in "your call?", which trips a completely unrelated merge-bypass regex in `pre-ask-auto-answer.sh` — real match, wrong reason, and dropping the row on that basis would have deleted a row two other tests already lock verbatim (`tests/test_ask_before_assuming.py`, `tests/test_ci_monitoring_scope.py:105`). The `test_ci_monitoring_scope.py` lock is easy to miss too: it doesn't say "ask-before-assuming" in its own filename, so a grep for `ask-before-assuming` across `tests/*.py` (not just files with an obvious name) is the only way to find every existing lock before editing the table.
- **A Stop-hook with a per-session retry cap (`RETRY_FILE`/`MAX_RETRIES`, e.g. `stop-check-untracked-work.sh`'s cap of 3) will silently stop blocking after N calls if a test script reuses ONE session id across multiple probe phrases in a loop** — the 4th and 5th phrases then read as "not covered" when the hook's own regex would have matched fine; the false negative looks exactly like a real coverage gap. Give every probe call its OWN fresh `session_id` (e.g. `f"test-{uuid.uuid4().hex[:10]}"`) unless the test is SPECIFICALLY about the retry-cap behavior itself.
- **`stop-check-untracked-work.sh`'s "asking permission to file issues" gate (Group 3) requires TWO separate regex groups to BOTH match the SAME message** — a create/file/open verb near issue/backlog/ticket wording, AND a permission-asking phrase (give the word, ready to, should i, want me to, once you confirm, ...). A single-clause probe phrase missing either half won't block even though the row it's meant to prove IS genuinely covered; test with a phrase carrying both, drawn straight from the row's own quoted text.
- **A PLAIN top-level `Stop` event payload ALSO carries `background_tasks` — not "unresearched", confirmed live (#120, 2026-07-28, headless `claude -p`, CC 2.1.220).** Same debug-`tee` recipe as the `SubagentStop` capture above (temporarily prepend a `tee -a /tmp/…-debug.log` entry to `~/.claude/settings.json`'s `Stop` array, spawn a real `claude -p "…"` process so it genuinely fires a top-level Stop, read the log, restore the settings.json byte-for-byte from a `cp -p` backup). Full observed key set: `session_id`, `transcript_path`, `cwd`, `prompt_id`, `permission_mode`, `effort`, `hook_event_name`, `stop_hook_active`, `last_assistant_message`, `background_tasks`, `session_crons`. `background_tasks` is the SAME harness-authoritative shape `SubagentStop` exposes (`{id, type: shell|subagent, status, description, command?|agent_type?}`), confirmed for all three shapes: a `run_in_background` Bash job (`type:"shell"`), an async-dispatched Agent (`type:"subagent"`, `agent_type` set), and nothing in flight (`background_tasks: []`, key still present, empty array). Registration is SYNCHRONOUS in every capture — the entry existed the instant the launching tool call returned. **Unlike `SubagentStop`, a top-level Stop's `background_tasks` has NO sibling problem** — it is this session's own list only, so `stop-check-working-liveness.sh` needs no ownership ledger, just `select(.status=="running")`. Used to block a turn ending on the `⏳ WORKING` marker with nothing the harness is actually tracking — the exact restreamer (idle pane, false claim) and eft5000 (orphaned process from an abandoned tool call, never entered into the registry in the first place) failure shapes. `session_crons` (scheduled routines) is present but deliberately unused — a cron doesn't wake THIS turn's session, it's an independent schedule.
- **`block-commit-without-design.sh` scans the WHOLE commit message TEXT for issue references, not just a `Closes #N` trailer — mentioning an UNRELATED already-closed issue in prose (`"the #99 gate"`, `"#48's threshold"`, `"#123 already logs"`) blocks the commit demanding a design marker for THAT issue too.** Hit twice writing #125/#126 (2026-07-28): a commit body citing `#99`/`#48`/`#123` purely for context (gates and a prior ticket the change interacts with, not tickets being implemented) failed with `no design comment posted yet for #99 #48 #123`, even though only #125 was actually being closed. Fix: in a commit message, refer to historical/context tickets by DESCRIPTION only ("the no-work gate", "the small-context gate", "the subagent-boundary decision log") and reserve bare `#N` for the issue(s) this commit's `Closes #N` trailer actually names.
- **Splitting ONE deeply-interleaved implementation edit across TWO tickets' own RED/GREEN pairs, when `git add -p`'s hunk boundaries can't separate them (both changes touch the SAME lines, e.g. a return-value rewrite plus a control-flow wrap around those same returns) — temporarily hardcode the SECOND ticket's condition to its "off" value, run/commit the FIRST ticket's GREEN, then flip the hardcoded value back to the real condition as the SECOND ticket's own GREEN (with its own RED test commit in between, run against the still-hardcoded-off code).** Used for #125 (word-vocabulary rewrite) + #126 (origin-based gate exemption): both edited the exact same `return` statements in `deliver_compact_now`, so no hunk-level split existed. `proven_boundary = origin == "subagent-stop"` stood in as `proven_boundary = False` for #125's GREEN commit (proving the word-vocabulary fix alone, since none of #125's own tests exercise `origin="subagent-stop"`), then flipped back for #126's GREEN once #126's RED tests were captured failing against the same hardcoded-False code. This is the same family as the existing `git add -p` / `git stash` splitting techniques above, for the case neither can reach.
- **Measuring "how often does a rare compact-mechanism branch actually fire" (#122): tally `~/.claude/compact-decisions.log` filtered to `type=autopilot-worker` (the proven-boundary/`origin=subagent-stop` population) BEFORE reasoning about a fix — never guess from the code alone.** Each RECORD line's `result=` word tells you exactly which of `sent`/`claim-queued`/`queued-compact`/`dropped-no-work`/`dropped-small-context`/`dup`/`recorded` (the last means it fell back to job 14's polled retry) the synchronous path reached; `grep "type=autopilot-worker"` isolates the population a ticket about `origin="subagent-stop"` cares about from the much larger `not-autopilot-worker`/`type=stop-hook` noise in the same file. Cross-check against `journalctl --user -u api-watchdog.service --since -72h | grep -oE '(skip|OK) [a-z-]+ \(compact-request[^)]*\)' | sort | uniq -c` for job 14's OWN outcome tally (no origin field there, so it's a population-SIZE check, not a per-request join). Watch for a stale legacy word in old lines: `result=delivered` in the log predates the #125 word-vocabulary rewrite (the literal word `cmd_compact_request` used to print for every truthy disposition) — a line's timestamp against the fix commit's `git log --format=%ad` tells you which vocabulary era it's from, never assume the log is homogeneous mid-rollout.
- **A busy-pane keystroke-safety finding earned by ONE `/compact` sender does not automatically transfer to a SECOND sender for the SAME command — check whether the second sender runs inside a live Stop-hook batch before reusing it.** `deliver_compact_now` (the synchronous #65 path — invoked from inside `notify-compact-subagent-boundary.sh`'s own SubagentStop-hook execution as of #400, 2026-08-12; originally from inside `notify-compact-request.sh`'s own Stop-hook execution, before that hook became a permanent no-op) established "a short send-keys reliably queues even into a busy pane" is safe — but that safety claim is scoped to a specific risk (#109/#84: keystrokes typed DURING a Stop hook's own execution, before that turn's accept/reject verdict exists, can park and fire at some arbitrary LATER accepted Stop). Job 14 (`compact_ticket_boundary`) is a plain ~60s poll that never runs inside any Stop-hook batch, so that specific risk does not apply to it at all — #122 reused the finding there safely, for the proven-boundary origin only, by re-checking the SAME `_compact_not_at_boundary` boundary gate immediately before the busy-pane send (already present, unchanged). Before extending a keystroke-into-busy-pane finding to a THIRD sender, name which specific risk validated it for the first two and confirm the new call site shares the same absence of that risk — don't just cite "we already proved busy panes are safe to type into" as a blanket license.

- **`block-commit-without-design.sh`'s design-comment classifier needs LITERAL shape-family wording, not merely a comment that conceptually covers root cause/approach/rejected-alternative — and its freshness window is only 180s from the comment's `createdAt`.** #127: a genuinely thorough design comment (root-cause narrative, a measured decision, an explicitly rejected alternative) still failed `design_gate.classify_design_comment` because it never used any phrase from `_APPROACH_RE`'s word list (`approach`, `will implement/fix/add/use`, `fixing it by`, …) — conceptual coverage isn't the test, keyword-family coverage is. And by the time a scoped fix is fully written+tested (tens of minutes), the ORIGINAL comment's freshness window has long expired, so `git commit` blocks again even though a design comment genuinely exists — `post-record-design-comment.sh` only re-checks on a FRESH (≤180s) `gh issue comment` call, it doesn't re-scan old comments. Fix: post (or re-post) the design comment with each of the three concept families spelled out under explicit headers ("**Root cause:**", "**Chosen approach:**", "**Rejected alternative:**", using literal words like "approach"/"will implement"/"rejected") in the SAME turn as the commit, never minutes earlier — sanity-check the classifier locally first (`python3 -c "import design_gate as dg; print(dg.classify_design_comment(open('x.md').read()))"`) rather than discovering the miss from a blocked commit.
- **`block-commit-without-design.sh` also gates on every BARE `#N` mentioned anywhere in the commit message TEXT, not just the trailer's issue** — already documented above for #125/#126, re-confirmed live on #127: citing prior/context tickets by number in a commit body (`#119`, `#118`, `#112`, even a camera-box PR number like `#436` quoted from a ticket specimen) each demanded their own design marker and blocked the commit. Describe every historical/context reference by NAME ("the sibling non-CI poll-repeat guard", "an earlier heredoc-verdict placement precedent", "camera-box's own chained poll-CI turns") in the commit message; reserve bare `#N` for the trailer's own `Closes #N`.
- **The 180s FRESHNESS window (#127, above) gates only the very FIRST classification of a design comment — once `design_gate.write_marker()` fires from that one fresh `gh issue comment` post, the marker FILE it writes has no expiry at all (`marker_exists()` is a bare `os.path.isfile()` check, `read_marker()` never compares its stored `ts` against `now`).** Confirmed empirically on #406+#408 (2026-08-12): a fresh design comment was posted, the RED commit referencing both issues succeeded — then TWO MORE commits (GREEN, then a `docs/autopilot-log.md` update) referencing the SAME `#406`/`#408` in their own messages, made many minutes later with no new comment posted, both committed cleanly with zero hook friction. So the freshness requirement is "post ONE qualifying comment before your FIRST commit that mentions this issue", never "post a fresh comment before EVERY commit that mentions it" — don't over-post repeat design comments once the marker for an issue already exists (`design-posted`/`validated-posted`/`reviewed-posted` dirs under `~/.claude`, one marker file per `<repo>#<issue>` per kind).

- **A headline count taken from an APPEND-ONLY log that spans a behaviour change measures two different regimes — split it on the fixing commit's timestamp before treating it as current evidence.** The #128 ticket was filed on "the one-shot bypass marker was used 187 times in one session", which reads as a guard being routinely routed around. Splitting that session's 193 `/tmp/airuleset-main-exec-bypass.log` lines on `7bcbafe` (`git show -s --format=%cI`, the commit that made the marker one-shot) gives **186 before / 7 after**, and the 7 are 4 arms + 3 consumes paired 1:1 — i.e. the defect as filed had already been fixed and was no longer re-measurable, which changes what the ticket should build (auditability, not scarcity). Two companion traps in the same log: pre-fix lines have a DIFFERENT FIELD SET (arm-logging did not exist yet, so `tool=` is absent on 16 of them and any `awk`-positional parse silently shifts), and a hook's OWN TEST SUITE writes to the same `/tmp` log with synthetic session ids (`t-mg-*`, `corpus*`) — so "the log is still growing right now" was the test suite, not a live session. Filter to real session ids (`grep -E 'session=[0-9a-f]{8}-'`) before reading any rate off a hook's /tmp log.
- **Before calling a classifier verdict a FALSE POSITIVE, grep `tests/` for the shape — the repo may have decided it deliberately, and "fixing" it would be a silent policy reversal.** Replaying a real day's commands through `block-main-implementation.sh` for #128, the single largest blocked class was `grep -E '<pat>' /tmp/run.log | tail -2`, which looked like an obvious miss of the "judge the SIZE that comes back" principle — until `PipeReducers80`'s three tests turned up (`cat file | grep | head -20`, `grep -rn . | head -20`, `journalctl | tail -50` must ALL block, with the reasoning in the class docstring). The proposed fix was withdrawn on that basis and the withdrawal posted to the ticket. The tell that you are in this situation: your fix would need to EDIT an existing test rather than add one. Companion to the existing "phrase-locks are deliberately multi-homed" bullet — here the lock is a behavioural test, not prose.
- **Never key a corpus-replay result map on a TRUNCATED command string.** Storing `blocked[cmd[:160]] = verdict` collided across a fleet of `ssh -i key -o StrictHostKeyChecking=no -o UserKnownHostsFile=/dev/null user@host "cd /opt/app && …"` commands that share a long identical prefix, so a later analysis attributed one command's classifier match to a completely different command (a nonsense `tail -n +165426` label on an `ssh` line was the tell). Key on the full command; truncate only at PRINT time. Per-session counts were unaffected because the replay's own cache was keyed correctly — a collision like this corrupts the ATTRIBUTION long before it corrupts the totals, so it is easy to miss if you only check the numbers.
- **ATTENDANCE is a measurable per-event axis, and it is the discriminator for "should an autonomous-session guard engage here".** For each main tool call, bisect its timestamp into the sorted list of that session's REAL user prompts (a `type=="user"` transcript entry with a `promptId`, a string `message.content`, not starting with `<`, and no `<local-command-stdout>`) and take the gap. On dev1 for 2026-07-28, only 931 of ~2000 main calls were more than 5 minutes after a human prompt, which is what let #128 widen a guard's engagement to previously-unguarded sessions while touching ZERO attended calls (103 newly blocked, minimum gap 15.0 min). The runtime counterpart of that transcript measurement is `/tmp/claude-user-active-<sid>` (stamped by `clear-question-dedup.sh` on UserPromptSubmit, which goal re-pokes and hook feedback do NOT fire) — a `stat -c %Y` away, so a hook can read the same axis for one session in O(1) with no transcript scan. Fail-open on a missing marker: `/tmp` gets cleared under long-running sessions, and "not provably away" must never start blocking a session that was previously unguarded.
- **The design gate reads the WHOLE Bash command, so a heredoc BODY containing bare `#N` blocks the commit even when the commit message is clean.** The existing bullets cover bare `#N` in a commit MESSAGE; the same gate fired on `cat >> docs/autopilot-log.md <<'ENTRY' … ENTRY && git add … && git commit -m "<clean message>"`, because the log entry itself legitimately cites the historical tickets it is recording. It also fires on the `gh issue comment … && git commit …` one-liner, since PreToolUse evaluates the whole compound BEFORE the comment posts. Both are the same fix: write the body with the **Write tool** into the scratchpad, then `cat scratch >> target` and `git commit` as SEPARATE calls — never chain a design-comment post or a heredoc-bodied append into the same Bash call as the commit.

- **A transcript LINE is not a turn — dedupe by `requestId` before counting anything.** Claude Code writes ONE assistant API response as SEVERAL lines, one per content block (`thinking`, then each `tool_use`), and every one of them carries a COPY of the same request-level `usage`. Measured on `agent-a8f4affe3e4d342cb.jsonl`: **236 usage lines, 111 distinct `requestId`s** (2.13x), with one request spread over 9 lines each restating `cache_read_input_tokens: 118,307`. Line-counting therefore inflates `turns` ~2x AND multiply-counts the same input tokens; it also silently distorts any per-turn mean, because a request written across 9 lines gets weighted 9x. Key on `requestId`, fall back to `message.id`, and keep the LAST usage snapshot per request — the trailing block carries the response's real `output_tokens` while earlier blocks carry partial values, and the input side is identical on every line. This is why `burn.scan_split()` over-counts (filed separately); `burn.read_dispatch` is the correct shape.
- **A subagent transcript carries much of its own injected floor, exactly measurable.** Line 0 is `type: "user"` with the dispatch prompt as a plain STRING in `message.content`; then two `attachment` entries — `skill_listing` (with the full `content`, and a `skillCount`) and `deferred_tools_delta` (`addedNames`). What is NOT in the transcript: the base system prompt, the loaded tool schemas, the agent definition, and CLAUDE.md — those must be read off disk (`burn.import_closure_chars` resolves the `@import` closure, each file once, cycle-safe) or left in a NAMED residual. Never fold an unmeasurable part into an attributed one.
- **The `subagent_type` is recoverable, but only via the PARENT transcript.** The subagent's own file never records it. The parent's `tool_result` for the dispatch quotes the new `agentId` in its text; its `tool_use_id` resolves to the `Agent`/`Task` `tool_use` block whose `input.subagent_type` is the answer (`burn.agent_types_from_parent`). Worth the join: the dispatch floor turns out to be bimodal by agent type and by essentially nothing else.
- **A TOKEN share and a COST share are different answers whenever caching is involved — report both or you overstate the lever.** The dispatch floor is 43.6% of subagent context TOKENS but 37.0% of subagent COST, because it is cache-WRITTEN once (weight 1.25) and cache-READ on every later turn (weight 0.1). A request's `cache_read` cannot be split into floor-part and growth-part from a transcript, so the cost figure is necessarily a stated MODEL (`floor x 1.25` once, then `floor x 0.1 x (turns-1)`) — say so rather than presenting it as a re-derivation from the raw buckets.
- **Only dispatches whose FIRST request is inside the window have a floor in it.** A file touched in the window is not a dispatch begun in it: a straddler's first in-window request is already carrying accumulated growth, so it must be excluded and COUNTED (3 of 105 over 12h, 0 of 301 over 48h), never averaged in. The earlier "~3 turns per dispatch" figure was wrong on exactly this axis and on the line-vs-request axis simultaneously; the real distribution is median 36–38, p25 15, p90 ~160, max 338.

- **A test asserting a phrase in `--help` must undo argparse's HYPHEN wrapping, not just its line wrapping.** `textwrap` runs with `break_on_hyphens=True`, so `machine-local` genuinely renders as `machine-\nlocal` and the usual `" ".join(out.split())` normalisation yields `machine- local` — the assertion fails while the help text is perfectly correct, which reads as a missing feature. Either assert only single words (textwrap never splits inside one) or re-join the artifact explicitly (`flat = h.replace("- ", "-")`) for that one containment check. Same family as this file's other "the test matched its own formatting, not the claim" traps.
- **Finding out WHICH keys a detection-only watchdog job actually consults: inject a SPY around its `marker_ok` (or any injected predicate) that records the key and then delegates to the real function.** Combined with `dry_run=True`, `send_fn=None` and a no-op `card_probe`, that is a completely read-only live probe against this box's real repos, and it answers a question no unit test can: whether the new lookup is reached in the REAL wiring and in what proportion. Insist on a POSITIVE control in the same output — #141's probe reported 45 card keys consulted for this repo of which **25 were delivered**, and each of the 20 unreported also got a backfill lookup; without the 25 a detector returning "everything is missing" is indistinguishable from a broken key (this repo's own recurring failure shape).
- **Any test that can reach a `notify` send/marker path must isolate `notify._claude_dir` in `setUp` — the marker store is the LIVE one, and its markers are SUPPRESSION artifacts.** The established isolation rule in this file covers `compact_claims_path`/`compact_sync_log_path`; the notify store needs it for a sharper reason. #141: a digest test class patched `marker_delivered` and returned `"sent"` from its fake `send`, so it reached the real `mark_backfill_reported` and wrote `~/.claude/autopilot-notify-sent/backfill#proj#7` on the developer's own box — silencing watchdog job 25 for any repo named `proj`, issue 7, for the 14-day marker TTL. Two tells that a marker came from a test rather than a real send: mode `rw-rw-r--` (default umask via `_dedup_mark_status`) instead of the `0600` of `_dedup_claim`, and an mtime sitting between two authoring commits. The pollution is invisible in a passing suite, so add the isolation to `setUp` AND assert it (`self.assertTrue(notify._dedup_dir().startswith(str(self.tmp)))`) rather than trusting that every future test in the class stays read-only.
- **`discover_managed_repos` has two blind spots that make it the wrong instrument for "does this box hold repo X".** It `continue`s on `depth >= max_depth` BEFORE testing for `.git`, so with the default `max_depth=4` a repo root at depth 4 is never even examined; and it tests only `dirnames`, so every git WORKTREE and SUBMODULE — whose `.git` is a FILE — is invisible. Measured on dev1: 40 discovered against 55 real checkouts, including a genuine depth-4 repo and a submodule with a real origin. It is nonetheless correct for jobs 27/28, which sweep for repos to MONITOR (a false negative there costs one unmonitored repo). A caller that must not produce a false "not here" should walk its own way (`_checkout_roots` in airuleset.py) rather than widening a function two other jobs depend on — and should try the invocation cwd first, which answers the common case in ~0.03s instead of ~0.7s per sweep.
- **A detector that GATES an operator needs an override, and the direction of harm decides it.** #141's first cut refused `--backfill-digest` outright when no local checkout was found. The harm it prevents is one over-apologetic message; the harm it causes is that the operator standing on the CORRECT box cannot repair a reporting gap at all — worse for a tool whose only reason to exist is repairing such gaps. Whenever a heuristic gate lands in front of a repair tool, ship a logged `--force` with it, and say in the refusal text that the check can be wrong ("if the checkout IS here and this check simply missed it, re-run with --force"). Related: a NEW symbol added to an EXISTING `from notify import ...` whose `except ImportError` path skips the whole repo silently converts a transient missing-symbol window (the timer runs the working tree every 60s, so a mid-push checkout is real) into a silent death of the entire backstop — resolve a new name in its OWN try, fall back to `None`, and LOG the degraded mode.

- **`hooks/block-sensitive-staging.sh` refuses to STAGE any path whose basename contains "secret" or "credential" — so a feature ABOUT secrets cannot use the obvious filenames.** #144's store, endpoint and test were blocked on the first commit and are named `vault*` instead, with the user-facing command still `airuleset.py secret`. Renaming is the guard's prescribed remedy (same as this file's B64_PAT/KV_PAT identifier hits); its `# airuleset:secret-ok` bypass is for content that genuinely cannot be renamed, which a new file never is. **The guard is also self-tripping in PROSE**: it decides it is in scope by grepping the command for the staging verb, so a heredoc that merely QUOTES that verb (documentation, a playbook entry, a commit body) turns on the filename gate and then blocks on the words in the surrounding text. Two consequences — write such prose to a file with the Write tool and `cat` it into place rather than through a heredoc, and assemble the verb from pieces (`'g'+'it a'+'dd'`) in any diagnostic so your own probe is not gated. The harness reports all of this as the opaque `PreToolUse:Bash hook error … No stderr output` while the hook really exited 2 with its reason on stdout.
- **A batch of literal `str.replace(..., 1)` edits over one file silently no-ops when an earlier replacement moved the anchor — and lands in the WRONG function when two functions share identical code.** #144 hit both in one pass: two edits meant for `cmd_secret` never applied (the health-probe fix was half-live, still sending the token) and a third landed in `cmd_upload`, which has a byte-identical readiness loop, pointing an unrelated command at a `/healthz` route its server does not have. Unit tests saw NEITHER — they covered the helpers, not the call sites. After any multi-replace pass, `grep` each intended anchor to confirm it moved, and RUN the command: the full suite is what caught the cross-command damage, and running the real CLI is what caught the no-op.
- **A CLI that hands a secret to a child must not hand it the parent's fd 1/2.** `subprocess.run(cmd, env=...)` with no `stdout=`/`stderr=` gives the child the agent's transcript, so `secret exec DB -- env` was a one-command exfiltration and any verbose child did it by accident. Capture, filter (`_secret_redact`: raw/stripped/percent/base64×3/hex, longest form first, nothing under 4 bytes), then re-emit. State the residual honestly — a child that TRANSFORMS the value still defeats any filter, and the only real containment (a user-written command template instead of agent argv) trades away the usability the feature exists for.
- **Pass a capability token to a detached child through the ENVIRONMENT, never argv.** `/proc/<pid>/cmdline` is 0444 (every uid on the box) while `/proc/<pid>/environ` is 0400. These boxes host foreign uids by design, so an argv token is readable by all of them for the endpoint's whole lifetime. Same reason a liveness probe must target a token-free route (`/healthz`, 204): probing the token URL hands the token to whatever won the pick-a-port race.
- **`O_EXCL` refuses a planted symlink (EEXIST); `O_TRUNC` FOLLOWS one and truncates the target.** A metadata write sitting next to a value write looked equally safe and was an arbitrary same-uid file-destruction primitive (`secrets/X.meta -> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys`). Add `O_NOFOLLOW` to every write at a path an attacker could pre-create, and check a store DIRECTORY for symlink-ness both before and after `mkdir(exist_ok=True)` — that call succeeds against a link to an existing directory, and a later `chmod` then tightens the target instead.
- **An env var documented "tests only" is enforced by nothing.** Gate such an override on a property an attacker cannot arrange (path under `tempfile.gettempdir()`), report the refusal on stderr, and the tests keep working because they already run under a temp dir. Companion test trap: a test asserting the override is IGNORED must not use a home-relative path — another test in this suite leaves `HOME` inside `/tmp`, where the override is legitimately allowed, so the assertion then measures the environment rather than the rule.
- **`build_opener(ProxyHandler({}))` DROPS the handler from `.handlers`** — an empty proxies dict registers no `*_open` methods, so `add_handler` never keeps it. A structural assertion for "our opener carries an empty ProxyHandler" therefore fails on correct code; assert BIDIRECTIONALLY instead (a default `build_opener()` in the same environment DOES carry the variable's proxies, ours carries none). A live control is impossible on loopback: urllib bypasses proxies for 127.0.0.1, the only address a test endpoint should bind.
- **A chmod-based "make this unlink fail" fixture is defeated by any `ensure_dir()` that re-tightens the mode on every path lookup** — it hands the write bit straight back. Patch `Path.unlink` for the one filename instead. Reach for this whenever testing that a delete-and-report path reports HONESTLY: `forget`/`purge` must not claim success when only the metadata went, since that "forgotten" line is the agent's proof a credential is gone.
- **`argparse.REMAINDER` stops parsing at the first token after the positional, so EVERY flag written after the name is swallowed into it** — `secret request X --ttl 900` silently ignored the TTL and stored with the default. A command that needs `-- CMD…` must absorb its own flags off the head of the remainder and stop dead at `--` (so a child keeps its own `--ttl`), and the absorber must run for every action, not just the one whose test caught it.
- **Adding a watchdog job: the docstring is load-bearing and tested.** `tests/test_no_session_kill.py` parses the header (`N numbered jobs per poll — L LIVE and R RETIRED`), requires an entry per number with no holes, and cross-checks the retired count against entries marked REMOVED. Add the number at the END (never renumber — historical log lines address jobs by number), update both header figures together, and follow the injected-callable convention (`run_once(..., vault_purge=None)` does nothing without it) so existing callers and tests see no change and nothing reaches a real store.

- **A PreToolUse guard registered on the `Bash` matcher alone leaves the `Read`, `Grep` and `Glob` TOOLS completely unguarded — and for anything an agent might want to LOOK AT, those are the more reflexive route, not the fallback.** Issue #153's first green version hard-blocked `cat ~/.claude/secrets/DB_PASS.secret` and every shell shape around it, while `Read` with `file_path` pointing straight at the same file sailed through untouched; a fresh-context adversarial review rated it the one CRITICAL finding, on the grounds that an agent asked what is in a file reaches for `Read` long before `cat`, and a prompt-injected one has a route no Bash-matched hook can see. The hook's own header had written that gap down as "outside its reach by construction", which was a design choice phrased as a law. Register the extra matchers by **EXACT tool name, one settings entry each** (`Read`, `Grep`, `Glob`) — this repo already registers `Write` and `Edit` as separate entries for the same reason, and an alternation regex matcher has been observed here to silently never match, which is the worst outcome available: a guard that never runs still reads as coverage. Payload fields differ per tool (`file_path`, `notebook_path`, `path`, `glob`), and one discrimination is load-bearing: **`Glob`'s `pattern` IS a path pattern and must be inspected, while `Grep`'s `pattern` is a regex to search FOR and must NOT be** — treating Grep's pattern as a path blocks searching the repo for the guard's own subject matter, a false positive with no security value whatsoever.
- **An allowlist of "provably metadata-only" commands is almost never what it says — two independent shapes break it, and both look harmless in the list.** (a) A head that accepts a **file as a NAME LIST** reads content and echoes it back in its own error text: `file -f <path>` and `du --files0-from=<path>` both print the file's first line as `cannot open '<CONTENT>'`. (b) An allowlisted head **loses its meaning when PIPED** — `ls <dir>/* | xargs cat` makes `ls` a name source, not a listing, so the exemption has to be conditional on the segment's terminator, which means a quote-aware splitter must return `(segment, terminator)` pairs rather than bare strings. Two further entries that read as metadata and are not: `cd`/`pushd` (the Bash tool's working directory PERSISTS between calls, so an allowed `cd` in one call makes a bare `cat *` in the NEXT call invisible to any stateless single-command check — refusing the `cd` is the only thing that closes it), and `chmod`/`chown`/`rm`/`shred`/`touch`, which are mutations rather than reads and, on a box hosting foreign uids by design, hand a 0600 file to another account. What survived audit for #153 was exactly `ls`, `stat`, `test`, `[` — and `ls -l` plus `stat` answer every legitimate metadata question anyway.
- **A minimum-length floor on a redaction/matching filter must be tested against the VALUE, never against each derived FORM, because encodings and escapings EXPAND.** #153 extended an output filter with escaped renderings and introduced a regression the unit tests could not see: the existing per-FORM `len(form) >= 4` guard let a 1-byte value `"` through as its 6-byte HTML rendering `&quot;`, so every `&quot;` in a child's ordinary output became the redaction marker while the function's own docstring promised short values are left alone. The same shape pre-dated the change via base64, whose encoding of one byte is four characters. Gate the whole form-expansion on the raw value clearing the floor. The general rule: whenever a filter derives N representations from one input, any length/entropy precondition belongs on the INPUT, or the derivations quietly re-admit exactly what the precondition excluded.
- **Proving a replacement assertion "can now fail" requires mutating the code path the test actually EXERCISES — mutating the function the test is named after is the easy way to certify a still-tautological test.** #153 replaced a test whose `calls = []` was local and never wired in. The first mutation attempt broke `vault_purge_job` itself and the test still passed, which reads as "the fix did not work"; the real reason was that `run_once` gates the call (`if vault_purge:`) and never reaches the job at all when unwired, so the mutation was applied to code the test never runs. Mutating the GATE instead (`if vault_purge:` → always call, with a stand-in callable) made the new assertion fail and the reverted code pass. Trace from the test's own entry point to the line you intend to break before believing a green mutant run.
- **`sudo -n -l` reporting `(ALL : ALL) NOPASSWD: ALL` for the agent's uid settles every "could this live somewhere the agent cannot read" question on these boxes with a flat no — and the honest consequence has to be written into the shipped artifact, not just the ticket.** A root-owned 0400 store, a dedicated service account and a setuid helper are each one `sudo cat` away, so uid separation buys nothing here and implementing it would imply a boundary that does not exist. What a hook genuinely delivers in that situation is worth stating precisely, because it is still worth having: the unsafe path is refused BY DEFAULT so the leak cannot happen by reflex or by prompt injection, and any deliberate circumvention has to leave an artifact (an audit line, or its own removal in git). Write that distinction — guardrail, not boundary — into the hook header and into the refusal message the caller actually sees, so the next reader cannot mistake the mechanism for a stronger one.
- **A text-matching guard over command STRINGS is inherently self-tripping, and the fix is documentation plus a Write-tool workflow, never a heredoc-body exemption.** #153's guard refuses any command whose text merely NAMES the protected path, so `grep -rn '\.claude/secrets' hooks/` and a commit or issue body quoting the path are blocked although they read nothing — the same shape `block-sensitive-staging.sh` has had for years, with the same remedy (describe the path in prose, or write the body to a file with the Write tool and `cat` it in). The tempting fix, stripping heredoc bodies before matching, was considered and REJECTED for this class of guard: a heredoc body is NOT inert here (`python3 <<EOF` genuinely executes, and this repo already measured that `cat > f <<EOF` + run is the dominant real shape), so that exemption trades a real read path for comfort. Note also that the env bypass cannot rescue an operator mid-session — an inline `VAR=1 cmd` sets the variable for the COMMAND, not for the hook process, which inherits Claude Code's own environment — so an env-only bypass is genuinely user-only by construction, which is the right property for a guard whose whole purpose is removing the agent's discretion.

- **A path-matching guard must decide on what the shell can EXPAND a component into, not on how it is typed — and the anchor that separates a path from a REGEX is the component's literal PREFIX, never a literal run anywhere in it.** #156: `block-vault-store-read.sh` matched the literal characters `.claude/secrets`, so `cat $HOME/.cl*/sec*/*` read a credential and was allowed — globbing is an entire expansion layer between the spelling the hook reads and the path the shell resolves. The fix is per-component `fnmatch(target, component)` plus an anchor, and the anchor choice is the whole design: "any literal run >= 3 chars that appears in the target" also matches `secret.*=`, `*.claude*` and `^[[:space:]]*//.*[Cc]laude` — grep REGEXES and `find -name` PATTERNS, which are not paths at all. Requiring the run to be the component's literal PREFIX **and** a prefix of the target keeps every real truncation (`.cl*`, `secr*`, `.claud?`, `.claud[e]`, `secret[s]`) and drops every one of those false positives. This is the same mention-vs-use distinction the hook already made deliberately for Grep's `pattern` field, re-learned one layer down. The residual it leaves — a glob that does NOT begin with the name (`*ecrets`, `[s]ecrets`, a bare `*`) — is the honest thing to ADMIT rather than chase: closing it needs an unanchored wildcard rule, which measurably refuses `du -sh ~/.claude/*` (sizes, never content) and is the wrong trade for a guard whose stated claim is only that the leak cannot happen by REFLEX.
- **Corpus-replay a classifier change through BOTH REAL versions of the script, prefiltered by a provable SUPERSET of the new predicate — a hand-written mirror silently under-reports because its segment splitter is not the shipped one.** #156: the mirror's naive `re.split` on `[;|&\n()]` cut `grep -rsoE "N8N_API_(KEY|URL)..." ~/.claude*/` at the `|` INSIDE the quoted regex, so the segment's head came out wrong and a real hit vanished from the report. Derive the prefilter from the predicate's own anchor requirement (here: every rule needs >= 3 literal chars of `.claude` or of `secret`, so the text must contain `.cl` or `sec`) — that took 212,743 unique commands down to 18,057 and is provably lossless. Then run `git show <base>:<hook> > /tmp/old.sh` and the working copy, one invocation each per command, and report NEWLY-BLOCKED and NO-LONGER-BLOCKED as separate numbers. Serial it is ~3 h under load; a `ThreadPoolExecutor(24)` brings it to ~15 min. Verdict for this one: 3 newly blocked, all three the session's own probe commands, 0 regressions.
- **To prove a guard TEST has teeth, write the guard as a free function taking the module under test, then aim it at a textually-mutated copy and require it to FAIL.** #156 finding 4: the guard read run_once's log, and `vault_purge_job` returns `[]` when nothing expired, so a REAL sweep against an empty store was indistinguishable from no sweep — the two states it existed to separate. Mutating Python for this needs `importlib.util.module_from_spec` + **`sys.modules[name] = mod` before `exec`** (the `@dataclass` trap already documented above applies to any module-level machinery), and the mutation target must be pinned by an `assertIn` so a moved signature fails loudly instead of silently mutating nothing. Assert BOTH directions in the meta-test — that the old observable is blind and the new one catches it — and verify by hand that reverting the observable really turns the meta-test red, or it is only asserting what you already believe. The fix itself was to read the artifact a sweep leaves WHATEVER it finds (`state["vault_purge_hour"]`), which is #134's gate-on-the-artifact lesson applied to a test rather than to a notification.
- **A guard's own AUDIT LOG is a second place the thing it protects can come to rest — fingerprint it, never record the raw subject.** #157: the bypass line wrote the full command, so an allowed WRITE (whose value is in its own text) deposited that credential verbatim into a plaintext 0664 file with no read guard and no TTL. Record tool + the matched REFERENCE + a SHA-256 + length instead; the reference is safe by construction because it is what the PATH predicate matched, always a path fragment and never the argument carrying a value. Two implementation notes: have the matcher EMIT the audit fields itself on a marked line that the wrapper splits off (`grep '^#AUDIT# '` / `grep -v`), because re-parsing the payload a second time to recover the command is exactly what created the leak; and create the file under `(umask 077)` + `chmod 600` rather than the ambient umask, since these boxes host foreign uids by design. Note what is NOT part of this defect: the refusal message echoing the offending command to stderr adds nothing, because the command is already in the transcript as the tool_use input the agent submitted.
- **`airuleset.py secret`'s own guard hook makes THIS repo self-tripping in a specific way worth planning around.** Any Bash command whose text names the store — a probe script, a `gh issue comment` heredoc, a `git commit -m` message quoting the path — is refused, by design. The workable pattern for a session working ON the guard: write probe scripts and issue/commit bodies with the **Write tool** and run/pass them by PATH (`python3 /tmp/x/probe.py`, `gh issue comment -F body.md`, `git commit -F msg.txt`), and assemble the path from fragments (`"."+"clau"+"de"`) inside any script that must construct it. `git commit -F` is doubly required here because `block-commit-without-design.sh` scans the whole Bash command text for bare `#N` and demands a design marker for every ticket merely MENTIONED.
- **A test that asserts only `returncode == 2` on a fail-closed hook cannot tell a real refusal from a CRASHED check — and this repo shipped exactly that.** #157: an audit change unpacked a 2-tuple as 3 inside `block-vault-store-read.sh`'s Read/Grep/Glob branch, so the branch threw on EVERY violation; python exited 1, the bash wrapper's `fail_closed` turned that into exit 2, and all four `ToolsOtherThanBash` block tests passed. A 3,148-test suite was green while the branch that #153 added as its CRITICAL fix never ran: the refusal message became HOOK MALFUNCTION instead of pointing at the sanctioned route, no audit line was written, and the user's env bypass could not work at all for the three tools an agent reaches for FIRST. The store stayed shut the whole time, which is exactly why nothing looked wrong. **Whenever a guard has a fail-closed path that shares an exit code with a real block, every block assertion must also assert the DECISION** — no `fail-closed`/`Traceback` in stderr, and the real refusal text present. The companion test is cheaper and even sharper: assert the side effect only a working branch produces (here, that a bypass writes an audit line), because a crashed branch produces none.
- **Dispatch the adversarial review as a FRESH-CONTEXT agent and give it a digest, not the repo — then act on its findings as RED tests, not as edits.** #156/#157: the review found the crash above plus three whole bypass classes the implementer's own corpus replay could not see, because a replay only covers shapes that OCCUR — brace expansion (`~/.claude/{secrets,x}/*`), an interposed `.` component (`~/.claude/./secrets/*`, which spells both names in full and defeats even a literal adjacency regex), and `find <parent> -exec cat {} +` (recursive by construction, so no `-r` flag to detect). It verified each twice: the hook's own ALLOW verdict, then a sandbox `HOME` proving the command really reads a planted credential. That two-step is what separates a finding from a hypothesis, and it is worth demanding explicitly in the dispatch. It also correctly flagged a claim as too strong (an audit "reference" is whatever the pattern matched, so a VALUE shaped like a store filename lands in the log) — a class of defect no test suite catches, because the code does exactly what it says and what it says is wrong.
- **When a rewrite encodes N DISTINCT user decisions, lock EVERY one of them — the gap is never a weak assertion, it's an UNLOCKED decision, and a one-clause revert of it passes the whole green suite.** #456 rewrote the autopilot sync-model to satisfy four user decisions (continuous dispatch, narrowed integration mutex, ALL-lanes-concurrent-never-starve, consolidate-not-layer). Round-1 review added positive locks for decisions 1 & 2; round-2 PROVED decision 3 had none by reverting the master template's concurrency clause back to "the LANE SCHEDULER (first lane wins)" — 3490 tests still passed, because every existing assertion targeted the LANES-FULL / LANE-3 / continuous-refill windows and nothing anywhere asserted "ALL LANES CONCURRENTLY" / "round-robin" / "starve". Before shipping a doctrine rewrite, ENUMERATE the decisions it encodes and confirm one positive lock per decision (a one-edit revert of each must fail SOME test). Two corollaries this ticket also hit: (a) a FORBID assertion is evaded by markdown FORMATTING of the forbidden token — a bold `**3-5**` puts `*` between the digits and "parallel", so `assertNotIn("3-5 parallel")` misses it; forbid a regex over every spelling (`3[-–]5`), not a literal (same family as the wrap trap at line 143, but for `assertNotIn`); (b) a positive `assertIn(phrase)` has single-mutation teeth ONLY if the phrase occurs exactly ONCE — "fixed cap" and "must never starve" each appear twice, so a single-occurrence revert survives; either confirm one occurrence, or lock the strong tooth on a genuinely-unique phrase and treat the multi-occurrence one as a weaker "doctrine present anywhere" check.
- **When a guard's rule needs a head that "walks a tree by construction", require the ACTION as well, and measure both spellings before choosing.** #156: adding `find` to the recursive-sweep trigger closed a real leak and cost 90 real commands, essentially all `find ~/.claude -name ...` name listings — the same trade already declined once for `du -sh ~/.claude/*`. Classifying the corpus by shape gave the answer without another full replay: the `-exec`/`-delete` family costs **5** commands (3 of them the session's own probes) while any-`find` costs **104 more** that only ever listed names. So the action form is blocked and the piped form (`find <parent> -type f | xargs cat`) is ADMITTED — deciding it would mean enumerating reader commands, the enumeration the guard rejects on principle, and the header already carried an `xargs`-fed-a-name-list gap that just needed to name this shape. A classifier rule that costs 20x more to close the second half of a class is two rules, not one.
- **A shared stdout channel between a matcher and its wrapper is an INJECTION channel — sanitize at the source, and make the real record win by position.** #157: the matcher emits its audit fields on a `#AUDIT# ` line the bash wrapper splits out with `grep`, which turns everything ELSE the matcher prints into a way to forge that line. A newline is a segment separator outside quotes but is BUFFERED INSIDE SINGLE QUOTES, so a quoted excerpt of the offending command can span lines and the second can begin with the marker — forging the timestamped entry and demoting the real fingerprint to a raw continuation line. Route every excerpt through one `re.sub(r"\s+", " ", ...)` helper (which also fixes the refusal message's layout), and have the wrapper take the LAST marked line since the emitter always prints it last. The general shape: any time a hook passes STRUCTURED data back through a channel that also carries UNTRUSTED text, the untrusted half needs a sanitizer, not the structured half a cleverer parser.

- **A PreToolUse block kills the WHOLE compound command — including the heredoc that was supposed to write the file the blocked command needed — and the retry then fails for a DIFFERENT reason that looks identical.** #159: `cd /tmp/x && cat > body.md <<'EOF' … EOF && gh issue create … -F body.md` was refused by `block-ungated-issue-filing.sh`, so `body.md` was never created; the obvious retry with an absolute path reported the SAME "no `Scope-gate:` line" refusal, now because the body file did not exist rather than because the line was missing. Two rules, both cheap: compose a body in its OWN call and file in a second one (never chain the write to the gated action), and pass `-F` an ABSOLUTE path, since the hook resolves it relative to ITS cwd (the repo root) and a relative path from another directory silently reads as "no body". The general shape — a gate that reads a file the same blocked command was going to produce — will recur for any hook that inspects `-F`/`--body-file` content.
- **A test that SCRAPES the artifact it is testing re-learns mention-vs-use one level up.** #159's template test pulled every backtick span starting `gh issue list` out of the shipped `/goal` text and demanded `--jq` on each — but the templates deliberately MENTION the bare form to explain why it is unusable as evidence, and that mention is worth keeping. Assert that a qualifying form is DECLARED (`any(... for ...)`), never that no disqualifying one is ever NAMED. Same family as the repo's existing "a lock test will match its own prose" traps, except here the prose belongs to the shipped artifact rather than to the test.
- **When an invariant MOVES from prose into a stronger mechanical form, RE-POINT its phrase-lock — do not delete it, and do not re-add the prose to make it pass.** #159 turned the branch-merge / fork-no-merge release-containment rule from the phrase "contained in origin/main" into a proof command that must print `RELEASED` into the stopping turn; `test_bounce_lane`'s two `assertIn("contained in origin/main")` locks broke. Those locks guard a real incident (2026-07-20 — tickets closed, prod got nothing, both sides reported done), so the fix is a shared helper asserting the command AND its expected token, which is strictly stronger than the old phrase: the phrase was satisfied by the template merely SAYING so while nothing ever checked it. Deleting such a lock silently retires an incident's lesson; satisfying it by re-adding the words retires the improvement.
- **An evidence protocol must name a command whose SUCCESS state prints something — otherwise it cannot distinguish a proof from an omission.** #159: `gh issue list --state open --search "-label:autopilot-skip"` prints NOTHING when the backlog is empty, so "paste the output showing none remain" asks for a blank space, which is byte-identical to never having run the command. The counted form `--json number --jq 'length'` prints a literal `0`. Whenever designing a paste-the-proof protocol, ask what the PASSING case looks like on screen first; if the answer is "empty", the protocol is unfalsifiable by construction no matter how firmly it is worded.
- **Claude Code caps a `/goal` condition at 4000 characters and REJECTS anything longer outright — and nothing in this repo ever issues an arm, so the failure is silent until a human pastes a template and reads the error.** #169: one commit (#159's proof protocol) took all three templates in `skills/autopilot/SKILL.md` from 3158/3355/3738 to 4903/4943/5435, which meant no goal could be armed anywhere on the fleet, by hand or by job 9 — not a degradation, a total outage of the loop mechanism. Worse, it was self-amplifying: a refused arm leaves BOTH of job 9's triggers on screen (Claude Code echoes the rejected command into the conversation, and the skill's arm question sits right above it), so every affected pane got the same over-length line re-typed every `GOAL_ARM_WINDOW_S`. `tests/test_goal_backlog_proof.py::TheTemplatesMustFitClaudeCodesGoalCap` now locks the cap plus the template COUNT, so the length assertion can never pass by silently measuring an empty list. Compress by cutting justification and incident citations only — move them to the skill BODY beside the templates, where a human reads them; the evaluator reads the condition and the condition has a hard budget.
- **`Goal cleared:` is written ONLY for an explicit `/goal clear`; a goal that finishes on its own prints `✔ Goal achieved` to the screen and persists NOTHING.** Measured over 8329 local transcripts: 86 `Goal set:` markers against 32 genuine `Goal cleared:` ones, every one an explicit clear. That asymmetry is what makes "the newest goal marker is a clear" a sound read of "the user switched this loop off" (#170) — a fact the pane's viewport cannot show, since `/goal clear` leaves the arm question and the printed `/goal` line untouched on screen. Count them with the STRUCTURAL filter (top-level `type=="system"` / `type=="user"` string content only): a crude full-text grep over the corpus counted the phrase nested inside `<task-notification>` blocks quoting another session's transcript — 2 of the first 3 hits were that, the exact trap `scan_goal_markers` already exists to avoid. Reuse `scan_goal_markers` for any new marker read rather than adding a second reader, and inherit that filter for free.
- **`pgrep -f "<pattern>"` inside a poll loop MATCHES THE LOOP'S OWN SHELL, so a "did my job die?" liveness check answers yes forever.** The playbook already documents this for `pkill -f` (which kills the calling shell); the `pgrep` form is worse in a different way — it fails SILENTLY and inverts the wait's meaning. Live: a suite run had already been killed, `pgrep -f "pytest tests/"` matched the polling `bash -c` whose own command line contains that literal string, and the loop reported "still running" for nine minutes against a dead process while the dot count never moved. The tell is a progress counter that does not advance while the liveness check stays positive — treat the counter, not the process check, as the authority. Bracket a character (`pytes[t]`) or match the process NAME, and prefer polling the OUTPUT ARTIFACT's growth over asking whether a process exists.
- **A harness `<status>killed</status>` on a background task does NOT mean the OS process died — and the two cases need opposite responses.** Twice in one session the notification said the suite was stopped while the run was genuinely gone; the general shape is the documented one (the handle is dropped, the process may survive), so the correct move is to re-derive from the DURABLE artifact — the output file's size/mtime and its own terminal line — never to relaunch on the notification alone. Relaunching a still-live run double-loads the box; concluding "finished" from a dropped handle reads a truncated file as a result. Both failures are avoided by writing an explicit terminal sentinel (`echo "EXIT=$?" >> <file>`) and polling for THAT.
- **Job 6's session-limit state (`sesslimit:<sid>`) shows `attempts: 0` for the whole wait, and that is the design, not a stall.** Live diagnosis of a codex-bridge report ("hit the limit and `continue` didn't work — a regression"): `{"resets_at": …, "pinged": true, "continued": false, "attempts": 0, "gave_up": false}` with the banner `You've hit your session limit · resets 7:40pm`. The job pings ONCE and retries the resume only AFTER the reset clock, because a retry before it just re-hits the limit and burns an attempt — so zero attempts before the reset is the mechanism working. Two things to check before calling such a report a regression: WHICH limit the footer actually shows (`5h 100%` vs `wk 10%` — the 5-hour and weekly limits are different, and users reasonably conflate them), and whether the ping was DELIVERED rather than merely claimed (`pinged: true` is a claim; the marker under `~/.claude/autopilot-notify-sent/` records the status, per #135).

- **An operator FLAG naming who a notification is for is a guess; the repo's own live pane is the artifact.** `--backfill-digest` took its recipient from `--owner-name`, falling back to `resolve_owner()` — the OPERATOR's tmux, which over ssh is nothing. Both describe who is TYPING, never the repo: a codex-bridge catch-up (every pane in the `david` group) went out with `--owner-name zbynek` from a dev1 session and landed in the wrong thread. `_checkout_pane_owner` matches pane cwds by `os.sep` CONTAINMENT (bare `startswith` matches sibling `proj-old`) and returns "" on two owners. The flag is DEMOTED to a fallback and refused when it contradicts the pane — #134's gate-on-the-artifact, applied to addressing.

- **`CLAUDE_CODE_STOP_HOOK_BLOCK_CAP` (default 8) overrides ANY blocking Stop hook — a Stop hook can DELAY a stop, never PREVENT one.** #166 measured this before writing the Stop-hook gate `#159`'s "prove the backlog is really empty" acceptance criterion originally called for. Read directly from the CC 2.1.220 binary: the Stop-hook aggregation loop tracks a running count of CONSECUTIVE Stop events where ANY registered hook returned blocking (`Un.blockingErrors.length>0` — aggregate across every hook in the batch, project hooks and `/goal`'s own internal evaluator hook alike, no special case for either); once that count exceeds `wue(process.env.CLAUDE_CODE_STOP_HOOK_BLOCK_CAP,8)`, Claude Code overrides every blocking hook and force-ends the turn anyway (`{reason:"completed"}` — the SAME terminal reason as a genuine unblocked completion), printing only a transient UI warning ("A hook blocked the turn from ending N consecutive times — overriding and ending turn"). Live-confirmed with a control arm in an isolated scratch session (`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, no real repo, no `gh`, no `/goal` armed): `CLAUDE_CODE_STOP_HOOK_BLOCK_CAP=2` + a hook that blocks unconditionally on every call still ended with `claude -p`'s own structured output reading `"terminal_reason":"completed","num_turns":4` after exactly 3 hook invocations (2 absorbed, the 3rd exceeded the cap) — the SAME `terminal_reason` a hook-free control run produced immediately (`num_turns":1`). Consequence: this sharpens the earlier `:272` entry above (which only flagged the `/goal` hook's own ~5s-later TIMING as "structurally invisible" to a same-batch gate) — the limit is harder and more general than ordering: no Stop hook, however written or however early it runs, can offer an unconditional guarantee against a persistently wrong claim, only a bounded nudge. #166 re-scoped accordingly: it ships `backlog_marker_gate.py` (a portable, tested mention-vs-use classifier for the `🏁 BACKLOG EMPTY:` marker) for #160's OUT-OF-BAND watchdog fix to consume, not an enforcing Stop hook.

- **This repo pushes DIRECT to `main` with no PR, so a `Closes #N` trailer on a RED (test-only) commit auto-closes the issue the instant it's pushed — before the fix exists.** Caught before push while working #150: the RED commit was first drafted with `Closes #150` in its message; `git reset --soft HEAD~1` (explicitly sanctioned for unpushed local work — never `--hard`) undid it cleanly, and the trailer was moved to the GREEN (fix) commit only. Since both commits usually land in the SAME `push` batch the end state is correct either way, but the trailer belongs on the commit that actually contains the fix — a RED commit that "closes" its own bug is a false record for anyone reading history later.

- **A cadence marker set only in an in-memory `state` dict, with `save_state()` running only once at the very end of a long sweep function, is not durable against a real process KILL — reuse the existing `persist=` callback shape (jobs 8/11) for every future cadence-gated job, never invent it fresh.** #172: jobs 27/28 (net-issue-drift alarm / stuck-main sweep, #137) set their hourly cadence stamp in memory and relied on `run_once`'s single trailing `save_state()` — but a systemd `TimeoutStartSec=120` kill mid-sweep is an uncaught PROCESS TERMINATION (not a catchable Python exception; `except Exception` never sees it), so the stamp never reached disk and the next 60s tick re-attempted the SAME expensive per-repo loop, killed again, forever (236 kills in one day, zero before — 7h07m with no completed sweep at all, all on dev1). The fix: accept a `persist` callback (default no-op), call it immediately after the cadence key is set in `state` and BEFORE any per-repo network call — proven with a RED test that models the kill via a fetch raising `SystemExit` (which propagates past every `except Exception` in the module, unlike an ordinary raise) and asserts the marker reached disk via a SECOND `run_once()` call reloading `state_path` from scratch. Paired fixes for the same incident: a round-robin `_repo_sweep_batch(repos, state, cursor_key, max_repos)` bounds how many repos ONE sweep touches (default 3, env `AIRULESET_REPO_SWEEP_BATCH`) so a box with 40+ repos never tries all of them in one 120s-budgeted run — remember the existing per-repo dedup-pruning logic (`{k: v for k, v in seen.items() if k in live}`) implicitly assumed EVERY repo was re-measured every sweep; once batching sits in front of it, prune only entries for repos actually TOUCHED this sweep, not just ones currently `live`, or an untouched repo's dedup memory gets silently wiped. Also: a long function's decision log printed only from its RETURNED list (not as jobs run) shows NOTHING when the function is killed before returning — a `_FlushList` list-subclass that fans every `append`/`extend`/`+=` out to an injected `log_fn` immediately (existing `logs +=`/`.append()` call sites need zero changes) turns "the whole sweep's output vanished with it" into "every job that ran before the kill is already in the journal".

  **CORRECTION (#176):** the original #172 write-up (and this bullet, before this
  correction) claimed the dev1 livelock "starved every other job including job 1's
  529 continue-nudge". That is **false** and the opposite sign of the truth: job 1's
  auto-resume runs inside the SAME per-pane loop as every other job, hundreds of
  lines before jobs 27/28 are even dispatched — a sweep killed at 120s inside jobs
  27/28 has already executed job 1 on every tick. On the box that actually stalled
  (gatekeeper), the journal shows **0** `start operation timed out` and **1140**
  `Finished api-watchdog.service` for 07-29, 367 of them inside the very window
  #172 claimed had "no completed sweep" — sweeps were completing there the whole
  time. dev1's own livelock is real but had **no api-error at all** during it, so
  nothing was starved there either. The real defect job 1 had is a SECOND,
  independent one: `pane_at_idle_prompt`'s bare-only gate misread a pane idle at `❯`
  but holding a foreign draft as "busy" and silently refused to type for 32
  consecutive polls (36 minutes) — see the `_classify_boundary` +
  `deliver_with_stash` fix below (#176). The "4h 28m" figure quoted for that stall
  is also uncorroborated; the demonstrable dead-with-zero-nudges window is 36m10s.

- **Two INDEPENDENT jobs that each escalate a "genuinely busy, silence otherwise"
  episode with job 4's shape (a state record + one deduped ping past a threshold)
  must NOT share one state-key prefix, even when they key on the identical session
  id.** #176: job 1's api-error busy-pane skip reuses job 4's exact escalation
  pattern (`:8536`) but writes `state["apierr-busypane:" + key]`, a prefix distinct
  from job 4's own `"busypane:" + key` — the two episodes (a 529 stall vs a `⏳
  WORKING` stall) are otherwise unrelated and can be live for the SAME session at
  different times; sharing one bookkeeping dict would let one job's `pinged`/
  `first_seen` silently suppress or corrupt the other's independent episode. The
  existing generic state-cleanup loop (`k.startswith(...)`) needed exactly one new
  branch added to its OR-chain for the new prefix — no new cleanup mechanism.
- **Reproducing a busy-pane misdiagnosis in a test: don't reach for the full
  `_BUSY_PANE`/chrome-peel fixture machinery when the boundary shape itself is the
  whole point — a ONE-LINE capture (`"❯\xa0nechať ako je\n"`) is enough to drive
  `_find_boundary_line_raw`'s glyph-fallback peel to the right answer** (no chrome
  rows below it to peel, so the loop stops immediately and returns that one line).
  Mocking the delivery primitive itself (`m.patch.object(wd, "deliver_with_stash",
  side_effect=_fake)`, recording `(pid, text)` and returning True/False) is what
  actually proves "job 1 now routes an idle-with-draft pane through the stash
  protocol instead of refusing" — driving the real tmux Ctrl-S/type/Enter sequence
  through `_FakeTmux`'s static per-pid capture would need a THIRD capture-sequencing
  fixture shape (`cap_seq`, already used elsewhere in this repo) for no added proof
  value at the `run_once`-integration level; that lower-level protocol is already
  covered by `deliver_with_stash`'s own dedicated test file.
- **Adding a NEW "ping once past a multiple of an existing tunable" threshold: check
  it against every EXISTING test that already uses that tunable at a nearby value,
  not just against the incident's own numbers.** #176's acceptance text asked for a
  ping "after 2×grace"; the pre-existing (must-keep-passing)
  `test_run_once_apierror_skipped_when_pane_busy` happens to use `grace=300` with a
  transcript exactly `600` seconds stale — precisely `2 * grace`. An inclusive
  `idle >= 2 * grace` fires exactly at that fixture's own boundary and flips its
  "no ping" assertion. A strict `idle > 2 * grace` keeps that pre-existing fixture
