  a no-op unmodified while still guaranteeing the ping fires the moment a REAL
  stall runs even one tick past the threshold — cheaper and safer than editing an
  existing locked test to accommodate a new one when a one-character `>`-vs-`>=`
  choice resolves the collision outright.

  **CORRECTION (#176 REOPENED):** "guaranteeing the ping fires the moment a REAL
  stall runs even one tick past the threshold" was true only for the ONE branch
  this bullet is about (the genuinely-busy skip) — it read, alongside the
  `run_once` docstring and this same ticket's own autopilot-log entry, as a
  SYSTEM-WIDE promise that job 1 could never again go silent. An independent
  adversarial review found a direct counterexample: the SEPARATE aborted-stash
  branch (`deliver_with_stash` returning False) had no state, no ping, and no
  bound at all — the exact silent-unbounded-skip this ticket was commissioned
  to remove, just relocated from the busy branch to the draft branch (finding
  F1). Fixed by giving that branch the identical escalation shape under its OWN
  dedicated state prefix (`apierr-stashabort:`, never `apierr-busypane:` or job
  4's own `busypane:` — three independent episodes on the same session id must
  never share bookkeeping). The busy branch ALSO had a real gap: its own
  threshold read live `idle` (`now - transcript mtime`), the one signal job 1's
  documented grace deliberately avoids elsewhere, so an unrelated write
  touching the transcript could hold it artificially low for as long as the
  busy stretch lasted (finding F2) — fixed by anchoring on the episode's own
  `first_seen` (wall clock) instead. **CORRECTION (#176 R1):** the sentence
  that used to stand here claimed these two fixes together made the guarantee
  system-wide — false, and disproved from two directions in the very next
  reopened pass: F3's own new "skip raced" branch (job 1 re-verifying against
  a fresh capture immediately before delivery) was ITSELF a third stateless,
  unbounded skip until that pass gave it the identical `apierr-stashabort:`
  escalation shape (R2); and `skip in-mode` (pane in tmux copy-mode, #175's
  own scope) remains a genuinely silent path this ticket never touches —
  empirically 0 of 201 in-mode skips in a 7-day journal were job 1's, but
  "never observed" is not the same claim as "cannot happen". State exactly
  which branches a threshold bullet like this one covers, not just which
  incident motivated it, and never claim a guarantee is system-wide without
  naming every branch it would have to cover.
- **A "poll once, abort" verify inside a keystroke-sending helper needs a
  bounded render-SETTLE poll before it gives up, whenever the SAME repo has
  already measured that the target's render can lag the keystroke landing.**
  #176 REOPENED F4: `deliver_with_stash`'s step-4 abort (the post-`C-s`
  verify that the box went bare-with-`STASH_MARKER`) took exactly ONE
  immediate capture — but this file already documents (see the `_await_typed`
  bullet above, and its own comment) that a render can lag a `send-keys`
  toggle actually landing. A raced immediate capture reported "stash failed"
  for a toggle that DID take, silently stranding the user's draft in the
  invisible single-slot stash with no delivered turn ever started to trigger
  its auto-restore — the worst possible outcome for a helper whose whole job
  is protecting a draft. Fix: `_await_stash_bare`, the same bounded-poll
  shape `_await_typed` already uses (never a blind timeout — returns the
  instant the box agrees), THEN a best-effort restoring `C-s` (outcome
  unchecked) only if it's still unverified after the settle window. Test
  teeth for this shape specifically require TWO fixtures in the mocked
  capture queue, not one: the first capture still showing the stale
  (unsettled) state, the second showing the real post-toggle state — a
  single-capture fixture can only ever test the "genuinely never settles"
  path, never the "settles on retry" path the fix actually adds.
- **A keystroke-sending decision computed from a TOP-OF-SWEEP capture can be
  stale by the time it actually acts, even within the SAME `run_once` call —
  re-verify against a FRESH capture immediately before the send, not once at
  the top of the loop.** #176 REOPENED F3: job 1 classified `captured` (the
  once-per-sweep capture taken before ANY job runs) as idle-with-a-draft, but
  job 10 (`prompt_wedge_check`, gated on the SAME once-per-sweep `captured`
  it was handed as a parameter) runs earlier in the sweep and CAN send real
  keystrokes for a recognized MACHINE draft — submitting the exact draft job
  1 was about to stash-deliver into. By the time job 1 reaches its own send
  point, the pane has already moved. `_goal_template_drift` (job 20) already
  had the right pattern for this exact race (`:6676-6679`, "by now the
  sweep's own capture is several tmux round-trips old") — job 1 just hadn't
  adopted it. The general rule: ANY job whose keystroke decision spans more
  than the single capture-then-immediately-act step must re-verify
  immediately before the send, not trust a capture taken earlier in the same
  sweep, however recent that earlier capture felt.
- **THREE independent "genuinely busy/failed, silence otherwise" escalation
  episodes for the SAME session id need THREE independent state-key
  prefixes, not two.** Extends the existing "two independent jobs... must use
  DISTINCT state-key prefixes" bullet above: #176 REOPENED added a THIRD
  escalation (the aborted-stash branch, `apierr-stashabort:`) alongside job
  1's own busy-branch (`apierr-busypane:`) and job 4's working-stall branch
  (`busypane:`) — all three can be live for the identical session id at
  different times, and all three must own a completely separate `pinged`/
  `first_seen` pair or one episode's bookkeeping silently corrupts another's.
  The cleanup OR-chain (the generic `k.startswith(...)` loop) needs exactly
  one new branch per new prefix, same as before — no new cleanup mechanism,
  and (per F7 below) a test that actually seeds and prunes each prefix, since
  a missing OR-chain branch leaves every PRE-EXISTING test green (nothing
  else in the suite ever reads that key).
- **An aborted verified-delivery attempt (`deliver_with_stash` returning False) must
  not be allowed to silently advance a decide()-style nudge/backoff counter that was
  already computed before the delivery was attempted.** The pre-existing shape here
  unconditionally wrote `state[key] = entry` immediately after calling `decide()`,
  before knowing whether any keystroke would actually be typed — harmless while the
  only delivery method (`send_continue`) was fire-and-forget with no verification.
  Once one branch (idle-with-a-draft) gained a VERIFIED delivery, the write had to
  move to AFTER that verification succeeds (still unconditional for the
  unverified `send_continue` path, since that never changed) — every OTHER branch
  (the usage-cap ping, the plain `wait` action) still writes `state[key]` exactly
  where it always did, so the only behavioral change is confined to the one new
  verified-and-fallible path.

- **`cmd_push`'s own fail-closed test-suite step (`subprocess.run([...], cwd=...)`
  with NO `capture_output`) makes the merged `stdout+stderr` log LOOK
  chronologically scrambled when redirected to a file (`> log 2>&1`) — this is
  a buffering artifact, not evidence of a bug.** unittest's dot-progress and its
  final `Ran N tests ... OK/FAILED` summary go to **stderr** (effectively
  unbuffered here), while every `print()` from application/test-fixture code
  under test (the SAME noise the existing "gk-request `o/r`, notify-card
  renders" bullet above documents) goes to **stdout**, which is FULLY
  block-buffered once it's a file rather than a tty — so a big chunk of that
  fixture noise can visually appear to print AFTER the real "Ran N tests"
  summary line even though it was produced earlier in wall-clock time, because
  the OS only flushes the stdout buffer in irregular chunks (partial mid-run,
  the rest at interpreter shutdown). Live-hit verifying #151's deploy: a genuine
  transient failure (the #179 wall-clock-timestamp-collision flake in
  `test_vault_channel.py`) correctly aborted the real push at
  `"  TESTS FAILED — refusing to push untested code."`, but that exact line sat
  in the log SANDWICHED inside a huge trailing blob of unrelated
  `cmd_push`/`gk-request`/notify fixture noise that had simply not been
  flushed yet — reading the file top-to-bottom naively made it look like the
  script kept running long after it had actually exited. Trust `grep -n "^Ran \|
  ^OK$\|^FAILED\|TESTS FAILED — refusing\|Deploying to\|deployments complete"`
  over the raw tail, and treat the REAL summary line (`Ran N tests ... OK`, then
  `Deploying to <host>` lines actually appearing) as the only trustworthy
  verdict — never the last few printed lines by position alone.

- **`airuleset.py push` races a concurrently-running TDD worker.** push runs
  the full test suite from the WORKING TREE fail-closed before pushing; a
  subagent doing RED→GREEN TDD in the same checkout will have its committed
  `[red]` failing test picked up by push's suite run, failing the push
  spuriously (live incident 2026-08-01: push failed with 1 failure that was
  the in-flight worker's `[red]` commit; second cause the same day: a
  background push task got killed mid-suite). Never launch `airuleset.py push`
  while an implementation worker is mid-flight in this repo — dispatch workers
  first, push ONCE after they finish; and launch push detached (`setsid
  nohup ... &` + a separate bounded waiter) so a killed background wrapper
  can't abort the deploy mid-run.

- **A single `stalled.add(key)` (or any per-session "still alive" marker) sitting AFTER several early-`continue` safety gates inside job 1's per-session pane loop is a RECURRING shape, not a one-off bug (#175 F1, reopened after #176 had already added TWO more early-continues below the old marker position without anyone noticing the marker was in the wrong place).** The end-of-sweep cleanup pass deletes any bare-UUID episode key not in `stalled`, so ANY gate that can `continue` before the marker silently resets that session's accumulated nudge/backoff/`escalated` state on the very next sweep — and because the marker only needs to mean "this session is still stalled right now" (which a truthy `err_text` already establishes), the fix is always the same: move the ONE existing `stalled.add(key)` call to the very first statement inside the enclosing `if err_text:`, above every gate, rather than adding a second marker call per gate. **When adding a new early-continue gate to this loop in the future, add it BELOW the marker, never re-introduce one above it.**
- **Isolating a mutant test PER FIX when several unrelated fixes land in the SAME commit and the SAME file: back up the whole file to `/tmp` (`cp watchdog/__init__.py /tmp/wd_backup.py`) before mutating, restore with a plain `cp` afterward — do NOT reach for `git stash`.** `git stash` would revert every uncommitted change in the file at once, which is fine when proving ONE fix against a clean-HEAD baseline (the existing RED/GREEN `git stash` techniques), but is the wrong tool for proving fix A's mutant dies while fix B and fix C's code stays in place (#175: F1's mutant needed F2/F4 still applied, and vice versa). A `python3 -c`/heredoc script doing a targeted `str.replace` with an `assert src2 != src` guard on each substitution, followed by a plain `cp` restore, isolates exactly one fix per mutation run.

- **A "persist before the expensive work" fix applied to a cadence-gated sweep job is easy to ship INCOMPLETE unless checked against EVERY step preceding the loop, not just the network calls inside it — and against per-ITEM dedup memory, not just the once-per-sweep cadence stamp.** #172 REOPENED: the first #172 fix moved `persist()` ahead of the per-repo `gh`/`git fetch` loop (jobs 27/28) but left it AFTER `repo_roots()` — an `os.walk($HOME)`, not free, and still capable of losing the marker to a kill — and left the per-repo DEDUP memory (`state['net_drift']`/`state['stuck_main']`, duplicate-ping suppression) written only at the very END of the loop, copying only the CADENCE half of jobs 8/11's own "dedup memory BEFORE the ping" shape, not the per-repo DEDUP half. Checklist for any future "persist before it can be lost" fix: (1) does the persist happen before EVERY step that could itself be interrupted, including local/non-network ones; (2) does EVERY piece of state a kill could lose (not just the cadence stamp) get written+persisted at the moment it changes, mirroring jobs 8/11's `seen[label] = {...}; persist()` shape verbatim rather than inventing a new one.
- **Proving a `print()`-doesn't-flush regression needs a REAL subprocess with a REAL OS pipe + SIGTERM — `unittest.mock`'s captured kwargs never touch an OS buffer, so an identity assertion (`log_fn is print`) can lock in exactly the bug it should have caught.** #172 REOPENED finding 1: the original fix's own test asserted `captured.get("log_fn") is print`, which passes for the UNFIXED code and would FAIL against the correct fix (`lambda line: print(line, flush=True)`) — a green test asserting the wrong thing. The real proof: spawn `[sys.executable, "-c", script]` with `stdout=subprocess.PIPE` (a real OS pipe is what makes stdout non-tty and triggers CPython's 8 KiB block-buffering, exactly like systemd's own `ExecStart`), have the child call the wired `log_fn` once then sleep long enough to still be alive, `send_signal(signal.SIGTERM)` from the parent after a short fixed wait (imports are ~70ms here — no need to poll), then assert the marker string IS in the captured stdout. Reuse this shape for any future "does this actually reach the journal before a kill" claim; a mocked capture can only ever prove the value was PASSED, never that it survives a real kill.
- **A best-effort network refresh preceding a LOCAL measurement must SKIP the measurement on failure, never fall through to measuring on data that might be stale — this is the OPPOSITE of the "never block on don't-know" contract used elsewhere in this file for an UNMEASURABLE result.** #172 REOPENED finding 5: `stuck_main_sweep`'s `git_fetch` failure was logged but the sweep still read `delivery_state()` off whatever refs were already on disk — those can be arbitrarily stale, which *inflates* `delivery_age`/`undelivered` rather than making them absent, manufacturing a false stuck-main positive for a repo merely behind a slow link (job 27's sibling contract is already correct by contrast: a failed `gh` fetch returns `None`, which IS "unmeasurable" and is silently skipped). The discriminator: a `None`/absent result is unmeasurable and safe to skip-as-unknown; a REFRESH FAILURE ahead of a read of EXISTING local data is a "the data I'm about to read may be wrong" signal and must `continue` (skip the item this sweep), never be treated as "proceed with what's on disk".

- **Historical "did the loop stop falsely N times before" measurement cannot be answered by grepping local transcripts for the stop marker text — a genuine hit needs the CLAIM to be ASSISTANT-AUTHORED, and even then it may not exist.** Investigating #181's blast radius: searching montalu/marek/simap's full local transcript corpora (1266/6/53 files) for `🏁 BACKLOG EMPTY:`/`SLICE EMPTY` found the string ONLY inside `system`/`attachment`/`user` entries (the `/goal` ARM text itself contains that wording as part of the condition, and re-injected rule content repeats it) — filtering to `type=="assistant"` (the only entry type that would represent the agent actually CLAIMING the marker) found **zero** occurrences on all three boxes, including on montalu even though its own airuleset checkout had #159's marker mechanism live 19 hours before the ticket's cited incident. This reconfirms the earlier finding in this file that Claude Code's own "✔ Goal achieved" stop announcement is PANE-ONLY and never persisted — so a definitive historical count of past false stops is NOT recoverable from transcript archaeology alone, and a ticket's own cited live-evidence quotes may have been gathered by a DIFFERENT session/box (e.g. an investigator running `gh` queries against the repo directly) than the one that actually experienced the stop, which is why the quotes don't always turn up verbatim in the affected box's own retained transcript files.

- **A "cross-check" that only requires its response to PARSE asserts nothing — it must ASSERT NON-EMPTY on a query that cannot legitimately be empty.** #181 I-1: the shared-account zero was validated by `involves:@me` returning parseable JSON, and `[]` parses — but "search returns nothing everywhere" IS `[]`, so the state the check claimed to detect was the state it accepted. Build the probe from a query with NO filtering qualifier (`--search "sort:created-desc"` — a sort-only search matches every open issue; verified live) and disambiguate its empty case with a STRUCTURALLY DIFFERENT code path (`gh issue list` with no `--search` uses the repository-issues connection, not the search index). Non-empty on the probe = the mechanism works; probe empty + other path non-empty = the mechanism is broken, refuse; both empty = there is genuinely nothing, and the zero is trivially correct. The general shape: a health check needs a POSITIVE control, and the control has to travel a different route than the thing it is vouching for.
- **A failure value that is indistinguishable from a legitimate answer is a wrong-answer generator wherever the caller BRANCHES on it — return `None` and make the caller decide.** #181 I-2, the same class as the earlier `view.get("labels")` bullet, one function away: `_gh_login`'s `except: return ""` made "gh api user failed" read as "not the maintainer", and the one caller treats those oppositely, so a box whose `gh` is simply unauthenticated silently got the own-account qual union — skipping a validation gate and, on a shared tracker, turning `author:@me` into the whole maintainer backlog. Resolution shape worth copying: the resolver returns `None`, the derived helper RAISES a named exception (`SliceUnresolved`), and every caller handles it in the way it ALREADY handles a query failure (the CLI refuses, the footer and the card keep `None`) — no caller invents a new policy, and no caller can silently keep the old guess.
- **A "refresh the live thing" write must never CREATE the thing.** #164 M-1: the run-window heartbeat wrote unconditionally, so with no progress file (or one already outside the window, which zeroes the counter) it created `{"done": 0, "ts": now}` and the footer asserted an active run with nothing achieved for six hours. Round 2 had fixed a heartbeat that stopped too early by shipping one that started too eagerly. Gate the heartbeat on the thing already being live; nothing is lost, because the state it exists to keep alive always exists by the time it fires.
- **A template under a hard character cap can usually pay for new content out of its own divergence from its siblings.** #181 round 3 added the obligation-set scope sentence and a whole REVIEW-WATCH clause to the FULL `/goal` template and it came out SHORTER (3724 → 3663 of 4000), because the same paragraphs — the (A) clause, the question/sleep-window policy — had already been tightened in the two reduced templates and never back-ported. Diff the three variants before touching the cap or its test: the cheapest characters are the ones a sibling already proved unnecessary.
- **GitHub's issue SEARCH index does NOT follow a repo rename; the REST/repository-listing path does — so `gh issue list --search ...` returns `[]` (rc 0) while `gh issue list` on the same repo returns everything.** Measured 2026-07-30 with a checkout whose `origin` still pointed at `zbynekdrlik/odoo-slovnormal` (renamed to `odoo-erp`): REST 110, every `--search` 0, `gh repo view` correctly resolving the NEW name. This is the cheapest reproduction of the whole false-empty class and needs no mocking — `mkdir /tmp/x && git init && git remote add origin <OLD-name>.git` and every search-backed command in that directory answers empty with exit 0. Any state where search answers empty while REST does not (index lag, a partial outage, a permissions edge) reaches the same line; the rename is just the one you can create on demand. The disambiguation that makes a health probe sound is that the two paths are structurally different gh code paths: a sort-only search (`--search "sort:created-desc"`, no filtering qualifier, matches every open issue) versus a bare `gh issue list -L 1`.
- **A docstring or comment that CORRECTS a false claim must not restate the false sentence verbatim — its own lock test will fire, and that is the lock working.** Round 4's `_write_autopilot_progress` docstring said "It said the run … always has an in-window file already", while the test forbade exactly that literal in the docstring; the test failed on the correction, not on the bug. Reword the retraction ("it used to justify the guard by asserting that …") rather than weakening the assertion. This is the third shape of this repo's recurring self-reference trap, and the one the existing entries do not cover: the earlier two are a TEST matching its own prose and a test forbidding a token the correct fix must contain — this is the ARTIFACT UNDER TEST quoting the forbidden literal while explaining why it is wrong.
- **A lock test asserting a canonical SENTENCE across several homes must normalise comment markers and wrapping, or it fails on formatting and locks nothing.** Round 4 required one settled `prio:bounce` definition in three places (a `#`-comment block in `airuleset.py` and two markdown bullets). The needle `not the never-stops failure` wrapped as `not the\n# never-stops failure` in the code comment and `not the\n   never-stops failure` in the skill — both correct statements, both failing. `re.sub(r"(?m)^\s*#\s?", "", window)` then `" ".join(window.split()).lower()` makes the assertion about the CLAIM. Prefer this over the alternative the playbook already records (writing the receiving home unwrapped), because a code comment cannot be kept unwrapped without fighting the line-length lint.

- **A FakeTmux that replays a FROZEN capture makes every `C-s` look like a no-op, so any test asserting "a pane holding text gets NO keystroke" passes for the wrong reason — and four of them did, across four files, for as long as the stash mechanism has existed.** #189: `test_stash_delivery`, `test_bounce_backstop`, `test_goal_autoarm` and `test_goal_rearm` each encoded "a user draft is never typed into" as *zero literal keystrokes*. Against a REAL pane `deliver_with_stash` PARKS the draft and types the payload into the box the park emptied — that is issue 35's entire purpose — but with a static capture the post-toggle verify can never see the marker light, so the delivery always aborted before typing and the assertion held by accident. The damage is not the four green tests: it is that a design invariant looked ENFORCED when nothing tested it, so the first change to that path failed five tests at once and each failure had to be re-adjudicated from scratch. Fix pattern, now in all four files: an opt-in `model_stash=True` fake holding a real input buffer plus a single stash slot, whose `C-s` parks/pops, `-l` appends, and `Enter` submits — roughly 30 lines, keyed off the fixture's own `❯` line so existing fixtures work unchanged, and `model_stash` defaults False so no other test in those files moves. Then assert the invariant that actually matters (`stash == "<the draft>"`, `submitted == [payload]`) instead of a keystroke count. **The general tell: a test asserting the ABSENCE of an action, driven by a fake whose state never changes, is asserting nothing** — the same shape as this file's `blocked()`-helper and `count_hook_blocks` traps, one layer out.
- **When a fix's correctness would hinge on an UNMEASURED behaviour of the thing you are automating, design the path so the behaviour cannot matter — do not encode your guess in a fixture.** #189: whether Claude Code's Ctrl+S dismisses a rendered prompt suggestion is not established, and the tempting design ("type only when the box reads bare after the toggle") delivers or refuses depending entirely on that answer. A fixture would then have proved whichever pane model it encoded — the precise mechanism that hid the space-vs-U+00A0 separator bug for the whole life of the stash. The shipped design types after the toggle in EVERY non-bare case and lets the type-verify discriminate (a suggestion is REPLACED by the keystroke, a real draft is APPENDED to), so the ghost case delivers under both possible answers, and the test asserts exactly that with `for survives in (True, False)`. When a ticket's own history says "fixtures encoding the wrong assumption is what hid this", the response is to remove the dependency on the assumption, not to guess it more carefully.
- **`gh issue view <N> --comments` is dead on this install — it fails with `GraphQL: Projects (classic) is being deprecated ... (repository.issue.projectCards)` and prints NO comments at all.** The `--json` form of the same command works fine, because only the human-readable view requests `projectCards`. For comments use the REST endpoint instead: `gh api repos/<owner>/<repo>/issues/<N>/comments --jq '.[] | "=== \(.user.login) \(.created_at) ===\n\(.body)"'`. Worth knowing before reading any ticket whose real content is in its comment thread — the failure looks like an empty discussion rather than an error, so the first read of a ticket can silently miss the correction that inverts its diagnosis, which is exactly what #189's comment thread contained.
- **Read-only probe for "would this pane-classifier change actually decide differently?" — evaluate BOTH predicates against real live captures, zero keystrokes.** `tmux list-panes -a -F '#{pane_id}\t#{pane_current_command}'`, filter to `claude`, `capture-pane -p -S -30` each, then print the old and new predicate side by side and count the rows where they disagree. For #189 that gave the honest verification the journal could not: 6 of 8 real panes on dev1 were bare boxes the old precondition refused and the new one accepts. Prefer this over reading a journal for the ABSENCE of an error signature — absence is equally consistent with the triggering condition simply not occurring right now (the gatekeeper 529 had resolved by then), whereas a live disagreement count is direct evidence about the change itself.
- **`git checkout HEAD -- <impl>` DESTROYS an UNCOMMITTED fix — the documented RED-proof recipe only works once GREEN is committed, and this file's own wording hid that.** The existing entry says `git checkout <pre-fix-sha> -- <impl files>` -> run the new tests -> `git checkout HEAD -- <impl files>`, which is correct for its stated case (proving RED for a fix that is *already* committed). Applied to a fix still sitting in the working tree it is a silent delete: HEAD *is* the pre-fix commit at that moment (the `[red]` test-only commit), so the "restore" step checks the pre-fix file back out and the fix is gone with no warning and no stash to recover from. Hit live on #190 — the whole 47-site rewrite had to be reapplied. The tell is `git status --porcelain` showing NO modification to the impl file after the restore, when it should show exactly one. **Correct order: commit GREEN first, THEN `git checkout <red-sha> -- <impl>` -> run -> `git checkout HEAD -- <impl>`** (now genuinely a restore, because HEAD carries the fix). If a RED proof is genuinely needed before committing, `cp` the file aside first and restore with `cp`, never with git. Both #190 and #188 reported their RED from the post-GREEN order, which is also the order that makes the proof reproducible by a reviewer from the SHAs in the commit message.
- **`set -o pipefail` + `grep -q` is a RACE, not a test — and every hook in this repo is written that way.** `VAR=$(echo "$MSG" | grep -q<flags> PAT && echo 1 || echo 0)`: `grep -q` exits at its FIRST match without draining stdin, so the `echo` writer is killed by SIGPIPE, pipefail reports the WRITER's **141** rather than grep's verdict, and `&& echo 1 || echo 0` collapses 141 into `0` — the value meaning "absent". So the gate's answer depends on process scheduling. Measured on dev1 (GNU grep 3.11): `rc=141` captured directly on a ~350-byte message under CPU saturation, and the real hook blocked its own fixture at iteration 44 of 400. **The deterministic forcing condition is SIZE** — past the 64 KiB pipe buffer the writer cannot finish in one `write()` and the race becomes certain (0 false verdicts at 360 B / 4.5 KB / 17 KB, 100% at 70 KB and 140 KB). Both polarities bite and the ticket only saw one: fail-CLOSED a byte-correct 140 KB completion report drew FIVE false violations (including "missing canonical heading" on a message whose first line IS that heading); fail-OPEN `merge despite the failing check` stopped being blocked at all, i.e. the guard silently does not guard. Fix shape (`msg_has` in `stop-check-prose-violations.sh`, #190): feed grep from a **here-string** — no concurrent writer process, so the race cannot exist — and keep the `$(… && echo 1 || echo 0)` wrapper so the conversion stays a pure substitution. Two companions: a `grep … | head -1` has the same defect one stage along (use `grep -m1`), and a grep exit **>= 2** is an ERROR the old idiom also could not tell from exit 1, so record it (to a FILE — every caller runs inside `$( )`, where a variable would not survive) and decline to assert a violation built on it. **#192 tracks the same idiom in 15 other hooks (100 sites); `block-sensitive-staging.sh` and `block-commit-without-design.sh` matter most there, because both inspect arbitrarily large content and a `block-*` hook failing this way fails OPEN.**
- **A flake needs a DETERMINISTIC forcing condition before it gets a test — a load-triggered reproduction proves the mechanism, it must not become the assertion.** #190's honest sequence: reproduce under CPU saturation (16 spinners, the real hook, the ticket's own fixture) only to CAPTURE the exit code and prove the mechanism, then find the property that forces it every time (message size vs pipe buffer) and write the test on THAT. Shipping the load-based repro as a test would have added a second flaky gate to the fail-closed pre-push suite, which is precisely what the ticket was filed about. Two traps met on the way: a candidate test that PASSED under RED (`test_the_heading_is_never_reported_missing`) turned out to be load-dependent itself — which check flakes varies run to run — and was deleted rather than kept as a weaker restatement of the deterministic one; and the structural lock's first regex bounded the span between `grep` and `head` with `[^|]*`, which cannot match a grep whose own PATTERN contains a literal `|`, so it saw one of the two offending lines and silently missed the other.
- **An error is not a verdict — resolve the unknown WHERE IT ARISES, and let the CALLER pick the direction by picking which question it asks.** #194: the #190 fix made `msg_has` return **1** on a grep exit >= 2, i.e. *"the pattern is not in the message"*. That is a fabricated verdict of exactly the same class as the `141 -> 0` collapse it was fixing, and it fails OPEN for every PRESENCE gate (no violation is added, `HARD_VIOLATIONS` stays empty, the hook exits clean while `merge --admin` ships). A downstream suppression tried to undo it and was keyed on *"did ANY check error"*, so one grep's failure discarded verdicts from checks that used a different pattern on a different regex engine and never errored. The shape that holds is two named questions — `msg_has` ("does it CONTAIN this?") and `msg_missing` ("does it LACK this?") — where **an undeterminable check answers YES to whatever it was asked**. The verb at each call site then IS that site's fail-direction declaration, and the two polarities get OPPOSITE outcomes from ONE rule: an INCRIMINATING pattern fires (fail closed), an EXONERATING pattern is denied so the established violation stands (fail closed), and a REQUIRED-FIELD probe reads as present so no unfixable accusation is manufactured (fail open, deliberately). Nothing downstream needs to ask about other checks, so the suppression is deleted rather than scoped — and the `mktemp`-failure hazard disappears with it, because nothing gates on the diagnostic file any more. **Reuse this verbatim for the 15 hooks in #192**; a per-call-site resolution is what makes a 16-hook conversion reviewable, whereas provenance plumbing through every violation is not. Companion detail: a non-blocking Stop hook's stderr never reaches the model, so a caveat about an unevaluable check has to travel on the block REASON.
- **Catastrophic PCRE backtracking in an `.*A.*B.*C` chain needs the FIRST segment's literal to have MANY candidate positions — pick the filler from that, or a repro silently proves nothing.** #194: the ticket's own reproducer padded with `"0 RED"` and never tripped anything (rc=1 at 600 KB), because `.*0 🔴` fails immediately at every `0 ` and the scan stays linear. Repeating the literal `0 🔴` instead is rc=2 (`exceeded PCRE's backtracking limit`) from ~5000 repeats / 30 KB, in 0.11 s — *refused, not slow*, so a timing-based probe misses it too. Fix by BOUNDING each gap rather than dropping `-P`: it is the tighter reading of the rule anyway (an audit line is a short fixed template), and it preserves matching semantics — whereas re-expressing a `(?<!code-)` lookbehind in ERE as `(^|[^-])` is strictly STRONGER and would newly reject legitimate lines. Verify a bounded pattern in both directions: the attack input answers cleanly at 1.2 MB AND every legitimate spelling still matches AND the string the lookbehind exists to exclude still does not.
- **SIZE such a bound by REAL DATA, never by worst-case speed — a bound picked for safety margin becomes a NEW false-accusation path, and its unbounded twin makes the result undiagnosable.** #194's first cut used `.{0,120}`, reasoned from step counts (121^3 against a 10M limit) and shipped. An adversarial review then measured what real audit lines look like: the gap between `/review[: ]` and the first counter is **169** chars for a verbose standards sentence and **161** for a found-and-fixed summary, so both were rejected and a byte-correct report was hard-blocked with *"Missing ✅ /review audit line"* — an accusation the agent cannot act on, because nothing in the message names LENGTH. Speed was never the constraint in the usable range: with grep's required-literal pre-filter defeated (place decoy literals BEFORE the anchor, or the engine never runs and every candidate looks free), 120 through 1000 all answer a 1 MB line in well under a second, and only `.{0,1000}` degrades badly (15.75 s at 200 KB, unfinished at 3.2 MB). So measure the real population first, then take the widest bound that stays flat — `.{0,300}` here, ~1.8x the longest real shape. The aggravator worth remembering: the twin `requesting-code-review` probe is **unbounded ERE** (a DFA, no backtracking limit to hit), so the identical gap passed there — the report was blocked on one audit line and passed on its twin, which makes the block unreadable from its own reason. When you bound one member of a matched pair, say in the comment why the other is not bounded.
- **A grep probe has THREE roles, not two, and each has a different fail direction: SELECTOR, PATTERN, and TRIGGER.** #194 shipped the has/missing rule for patterns and then got a selector wrong in the same commit. A **SELECTOR** only chooses what the real pattern reads (stage 1 of `grep 🌐 | grep localhost`, or the `/goal` line filter): it is neither incriminating nor exonerating, so an unanswerable selector must **WIDEN the scope** and let the pattern decide — resolving it as a verdict accused a message containing no 🌐 line at all, printed an EMPTY "offending line(s)" block, and attached a note blaming message size that pointed at the wrong repair. The same commit already had this right for the `/goal` filter (falls back to the full MSG) and wrong for the 🌐 one, which is the tell: **grep every sibling of a shape you just fixed.** A **TRIGGER** — a probe deciding whether a whole BLOCK of other gates applies — has no settled answer yet and is filed separately (#197): resolving it "in scope" runs required-field gates against a message that may not be a report and manufactures the exact unfixable accusations the field family deliberately fails open on, while "out of scope" silently disables a hard gate, and the selector's widen-the-scope trick does not transfer because there is no downstream pattern left to decide.
- **Dispatch the adversarial review as a FRESH-CONTEXT agent even when the suite is green and you have re-read your own diff — it is the step that catches YOUR regression, not the ticket's.** #194: 3406 tests passing, ruff clean, a deliberate RED/GREEN differential and a careful self-read all missed `.{0,120}`, because every fixture in the suite used the canonical short audit line. The review found it in one pass by asking what REAL reports look like instead of what the tests contain, and it separated its own findings honestly into content-reachable (needs no shim) versus robustness-only (exercised with a single-pattern PATH shim returning exit 2, `exec`ing the real grep otherwise — a reusable technique for driving exactly ONE check undeterminable while the rest return real verdicts). Demand that split in the dispatch: a finding whose trigger is simulated is a design finding, not an exploit, and conflating them mis-ranks the work. Give it a DIGEST plus the invariant to attack, never the repo.
- **A reproducer that does not reproduce may be blocked by an ENCLOSING GATE, not by a wrong mechanism — prove the code path is REACHED before concluding the ticket is wrong.** #194's fixture used `## Work Complete` without the ✅, so `IS_COMPLETION` stayed 0 and the entire audit block holding the failing check never ran; combined with the wrong filler above, the "verbatim" repro returned `BLOCKED: True` at every size and looked like a stale ticket. Both faults were in the fixture and the defect was real. Cheapest discrimination: run the hook under `bash -x` against one real payload — the trace shows exactly which branch was skipped, and it also localises a `set -u` "unbound variable" in one call (moving a variable's definition later in the file broke a derived one two lines above it).
- **A differential is the right test shape for "X must not affect the verdict" — but check it is not VACUOUS at RED.** #194 asserts the same message + same grep behaviour under a writable and an unwritable `TMPDIR` reach an identical decision. Under a total grep stub the pre-fix code produced NO violations at all, so both arms agreed and the test passed against the bug; it only bites in the mode where exactly ONE check errors while the rest return real verdicts, which is the shape that reaches a suppression with a non-empty violation list. Run both modes as subTests. Related: when replacing an assertion that turns out to encode the fail-OPEN half of the defect (here, "an all-errors run must not block AT ALL"), SPLIT it by polarity and keep both halves — deleting it weakens the suite, and keeping it blocks the fix.
- **Strip quoted spans PER PURPOSE in a source-scanning lock regex — for FLAG detection yes, for CONSUMER detection never.** #194: stripping quotes stops a grep whose PATTERN contains `-q` from reading as a quiet grep, but the same strip erases the early-exit signal of `sed -n '1p;q'` and `awk 'NR==1{print;exit}'`, whose `q`/`exit` live INSIDE the quotes — so the widened consumer lock silently matched nothing. Keep quotes for the consumer scan and instead require the sed `q` to be the script's LAST command (`\bq\b['\"]?\s*(?:\||\)|$)`), which excludes a substitution that merely contains the letter (`sed 's/q/x/'`). And pair every widened lock with a TEETH test asserting each evading spelling is caught AND each safe shape is not — a lock that matches nothing passes forever, and this one is about to certify 15 more hooks.

- **A live CC 2.1.220 input box is BORDERED, its separator after `❯` is U+00A0, and a WRAPPED draft puts the glyph on the box's FIRST row — so any check that tests the BOUNDARY row for `❯` is blind to every payload long enough to wrap.** Read off three real panes 2026-07-30 with `tmux capture-pane -p -t <pane> | cat -A` (read-only, zero `send-keys`): the shape is `────…` / `❯\xa0<draft>` / `────…`, so `_find_boundary_line_raw`'s STRUCTURAL separator-pair strategy is the one every real capture resolves through, and it already had the box's whole row list in hand (`content = lines[earlier[-1]+1 : idx_b]`) before discarding all but the last. That is why #193's fix needed no new scanning: the head row was already there, bounded by the box's own borders, reachable without any walk into the transcript (which would be the #233 scar). The borderless glyph-fallback deliberately still returns ONE row and is therefore silently NOT fixed — most of this file's older fixtures are borderless, so that is exactly where a future regression will hide. **Verify a pane-classifier change against real panes with a read-only differential**: load the pre-fix module alongside the working tree (`git show <sha>:watchdog/__init__.py` into a temp file, import both) and print every consumer predicate old-vs-new for each live capture. Zero disagreement on real panes plus a deliberate disagreement on the target shape is the evidence; a unit suite cannot produce it.
- **Making a previously-unreadable value readable hands every SUFFIX and SUBSTRING test in the file a one-character needle — audit each one before shipping the read.** #193: while a wrapped box returned `None`, three tests were structurally unreachable. The moment it returned its TAIL row (which can be a single character), all three became trivially satisfiable and each would have typed into or submitted a genuine user draft: job 7's `prompt.endswith(itext)` → a bare `Enter` on a foreign draft ending in `"."` (every reply prompt ends with the same fixed sentence), plus the answer marked delivered though never typed; `_is_dreply_machine_text`'s `txt in tail` → job 10 classifying a user draft as MACHINE, which SKIPS its at-rest guards and auto-submits; and `deliver_with_stash` newly ADMITTING a pane whose wrapped draft it can neither verify nor undo, leaving the payload glued to that draft — the same defect the ticket was about, relocated one branch along. The fix shape in all three: **require BOTH ENDS of the box to agree** (head row AND tail row), and where the recorder only stored one end, store the other (`_record_dreply_typed` gained a `head`; a pre-change record then matches nothing, so job 10 PINGS instead of typing — the safe direction, and the 48h TTL retires it). The grep that finds the population: every `endswith(` / ` in ` / `startswith(` whose needle is now the newly-readable value.
- **`C-s` into a NON-EMPTY box PARKS it (single slot, SILENT overwrite), so any recovery that toggles while our own text is still in the box destroys the user's parked draft.** `deliver_with_stash`'s verify-failure path did exactly that: `if parked: run(C-s)  # pop the parked draft back` fired with our payload sitting in the box, because the verify failed for INABILITY TO READ, not because the text was absent. The correct order is backspace-then-toggle, and the backspace is only provable when the box was VERIFIED BARE immediately before typing (PARKED/NOOP) — then every character in it is ours and a surplus backspace lands on an empty box. For an UNRESOLVED box (content the toggle did not park) the same reasoning does not hold, so that branch refuses unless the box provably ENDS with our text. Also: read the pop back rather than logging it (`_log(... if _input_line_text(capture_pane(...)) else ...)`) — a log line asserting a delivery it never checked is the #134 mistake in miniature.
- **A test fixture that hand-builds a record the production code WRITES will drift the moment the writer grows a field — seed through the writer.** `MachineTailRecognition` hand-wrote `{"tail": …, "ts": …}`; adding a `head` half broke it, and the failure read like a bug in the fix. `wd._record_dreply_typed(state, pid, text, now)` cannot drift from itself. Companion, for any fixture that must WRAP: build it by asking the renderer, never by counting columns — `draft_with_tail()` loops `n` and accepts only when `_input_box_rows_raw(render_box(d))[-1] == tail`. Two hand-counted attempts silently produced a single-row box and a 200-char unbreakable word, so the tests passed while exercising nothing; the self-verifying builder failed loudly instead. Use single-character filler words (`"a a a …"`, 2 columns each) so any target fill length is reachable.
- **A regression test for a fix must drive the JOB, never the helper the fix touched — a helper-level assertion can only fail on a changed signature, which proves nothing.** #193's first attempt at the review-finding tests called `_box_holds_our_own_text` and a re-arity'd `_is_dreply_machine_text`; against the pre-fix build they raised `AttributeError`/`TypeError`, i.e. five failures that demonstrated nothing about behaviour (the same class this file already flags for `AttributeError`-only REDs). Rewritten to drive `deliver_discord_replies` and `prompt_wedge_check`, all four fail as `AssertionError`s printing the harm itself — `'Enter' unexpectedly found in [...]` against a foreign draft. Pair each with a POSITIVE control in the same class (our OWN stuck delivery is still recognised, still auto-submitted), or a "never do X" test is indistinguishable from having deleted the feature.
- **Dispatch the fresh-context adversarial review BEFORE `push`, not after — for this repo `push` IS the deploy, and a keystroke path reaches six live user sessions the moment it lands.** #193: 3390 tests green, ruff clean, a deliberate RED/GREEN differential, a live read-only pane check and a careful self-read — and the review still found two CRITICALs and a MAJOR, all of them regressions the fix itself introduced. Neither the suite nor a corpus replay could have seen them: a fake that models ONE rendering cannot model the tails a real renderer produces, and the newly-reachable branches had no corpus because they were unreachable until this change. Give the reviewer a DIGEST plus the invariants to attack (not the repo), and demand it separate findings it could TRIGGER from theoretical ones — that split is what let three findings be fixed immediately and the rest be recorded honestly as residual.

- **In a `set -euo pipefail` hook, EMIT THE VERDICT FIRST and do bookkeeping after — position alone makes a state write able to delete a decision it has nothing to do with.** #196: `stop-check-prose-violations.sh` wrote its retry counter on the line above the `jq` that prints `{"decision":"block"}`, so a failed redirect (unwritable file, unbuildable path) exited the shell before any verdict existed — rc 1, empty stdout, and a quality-bypass offer shipped. `|| true` on the write is necessary and not sufficient: the ORDER is what stops the next statement added there from re-opening it, which is how this defect class has now moved seven times. State the rule as "bookkeeping decides only the counter", and check it by reading each condition out loud — a guard whose enclosing `if` names a CALLER, a QUERY SHAPE, or a BOOKKEEPING FACT rather than the thing being trusted is the next round's CRITICAL.
- **Three bash traps that make a hook fail SILENTLY, all hit in one ticket (#196/#198).** (a) `echo x > "$F" 2>/dev/null` does NOT suppress a REDIRECTION failure — redirections are processed left to right, so the shell reports `Permission denied` on the still-live stderr before `2>` takes effect; the message then reads like a hook malfunction in the turn. Use `{ echo x > "$F"; } 2>/dev/null || true`. (b) `[ "$X" -lt "$Y" ]` with a NON-NUMERIC `$X` exits **2**, and inside an `&&` condition that is indistinguishable from "false" — so a state file holding garbage made a whole block branch unreachable at rc 0 with no complaint at all, the quietest of the three variants. Normalise with `case "$X" in "" | *[!0-9]*) X=0 ;; esac` before any arithmetic test. (c) a `case` whose matched branch ENDS in a failing `[ … ] && assignment` returns non-zero, and `set -e` exits on a compound command — so guard length/shape checks in `if` blocks, or make sure the failing test precedes the final `&&` (the shape at the foot of that hook, which is why it survived for years).
- **A key derived from input that may be absent or hostile is VALIDATED AND REFUSED, never mangled.** #198: `session_id // "unknown"` turned every unidentifiable invocation into ONE per-BOX `/tmp` bucket that nothing expired, so five such calls disarmed a Stop gate for the life of the box's `/tmp` — and it really was sitting at the cap on dev1, which is how an independent verification reported a shipped, correct, deployed fix as broken at every payload size. The tempting repair, sanitising the id (`tr -c 'A-Za-z0-9' _`), is MANY-TO-ONE: two distinct garbage ids collapse onto one key, i.e. the same shared bucket with a new spelling. Accept an id only if it is ALREADY a safe path component (`case "$X" in .* | *[!A-Za-z0-9._-]*)` → refuse) and give a refused id NO state; that also keeps `/`, `..` and every shell metacharacter out of the path by construction rather than by escaping. Fail direction for throttle/bookkeeping state, settled: unresolvable state must never SUPPRESS a verdict, so "no state" means the gate fires.
- **A per-session key does NOT die with the session — `claude -c` reuses the session id across a restart — so any per-session state file still needs a TTL.** Both halves are already in this file separately (the id survives `/exit` + `claude -c`; a stale counter disarmed a later session live). Pair them whenever keying state by session: the key stops COLLISIONS, only the TTL stops RESURRECTION. Size the TTL from the real spacing of the events it bounds (consecutive Stop blocks are one message rewrite apart, seconds to minutes → an hour is 20-60x headroom and cannot cut a live loop short).
- **`systemMessage` is the decision-free channel for a Stop hook that must SAY something without blocking.** Present in the installed 2.1.220 bundle (30 occurrences, incl. the hook-output parse site and `systemMessageChars` telemetry) and documented as a common field for every hook event. #198's acceptance was that exhausting a retry cap must not be an empty stdout indistinguishable from a clean pass — `{"systemMessage": …}` warns the user and any caller while carrying no `decision`, so it cannot re-block (which would be the exact runaway the cap exists to stop). Prefer it to a bare non-JSON line: Stop-hook stdout is parsed as a decision when it is JSON, so an ad-hoc line risks a parse warning where a documented field does not. Test it by asserting `"decision" not in json.loads(stdout)`, never just that stdout is non-empty.
- **Before trusting ANY manual hook invocation, check the state files that invocation reads — a poisoned shared file makes the probe lie in whichever direction the state happens to point.** This is the #198 lesson turned into a diagnostic habit: the verification that was fooled ran a correct reproducer against a correct fix and got `rc=0 stdout-empty BLOCKED=False` at every payload size, purely because `/tmp/airuleset-stop-block-unknown` was at the cap. Two cheap habits: pass a UNIQUE key (`uuid4().hex`) per manual call so no shared state can be reached, and put a POSITIVE CONTROL in the same run (a clean message, and a known-violating one) so "nothing happened" is distinguishable from "the mechanism is dead". When a `/tmp` namespace has grown, `ls -1 <pat> | sed 's/<prefix>//' | sed 's/[0-9a-f]\{6,\}.*//' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn` gives the per-prefix histogram that names the GENERATOR in one command (2418 files → one test module responsible for 2000 of them).
- **A "is it a number?" guard written as `case "$X" in *[!0-9]*)` is LOCALE-COLLATED and does not reject a fullwidth digit — and even a correct digit test does not mean `[` can parse the value.** Both were live in the first cut of #196's fix and both reached `[ "$X" -lt "$Y" ]`, which exits **2**, which inside an `&&`/`if` condition is indistinguishable from "false" — i.e. the identical silent fail-open the fix existed to remove, reached by a different byte sequence. Measured on dev1: `case "３" in *[!0-9]*)` does NOT match, while `case "３" in 0|1|…|9)` correctly does not accept it. So write the guard as LITERAL alternatives, never a range or a character class, and bound the LENGTH as well (a 20-digit run is not "not digits", but `[` still cannot compare it). Third member of the same family: **bash strips a NUL out of a command substitution**, so a file holding `3\x004` arrives as `34` — two digits spliced into a value past a cap — with a `warning: command substitution: ignored null byte in input` printed into the turn. A byte-size bound on the file is what kills that one; the warning itself comes from the SHELL performing the substitution, so it needs `{ V=$(cat f); } 2>/dev/null`, not a `2>` inside the substitution.
- **A state file under `/tmp` whose name embeds a session id is ENUMERABLE, not merely guessable, and `-f` + `>` make it a same-uid write primitive.** `/tmp` is `drwxrwxrwt` and shared with foreign uids by design on these boxes, and live session ids are world-readable straight out of `/tmp` from this repo's OWN markers (`airuleset-bgtasks-<sid>-*`, `airuleset-runcard-gate-<sid>`). So a foreign uid can pre-create any such path. Three consequences, all reproduced against the real hook in #196/#198: `[ -f "$F" ]` alone FOLLOWS a symlink, and a bare `> "$F"` writes THROUGH it (truncating any file our uid can write); a **FIFO** at the path blocks `cat` until the harness kills the hook, so a Stop gate never prints its verdict at all — the sharpest fail-open available; and a planted file with a disarming value is simply believed. The shape that closes all three: read only when `[ -f "$F" ] && [ ! -L "$F" ] && [ -O "$F" ]`, and write via `mktemp` + `mv -f` (rename replaces the NAME, so a planted symlink or foreign file is DISPLACED by our own 0600 file instead of being written through — it self-heals the key). This is the `O_NOFOLLOW`/`O_EXCL` lesson already recorded for the vault store, in its shell form.
- **A TTL on state must be bounded in BOTH directions — an age in the FUTURE passes every `-le` test.** `[ "$((now - mtime))" -le "$TTL" ]` is true for every negative difference, so a file dated ahead of the clock is honoured forever: exactly the immortal counter #198 removed, rebuilt by one backward clock step (an NTP correction, a wrong RTC at boot, a restored VM snapshot). Require `mtime <= now` as its own check before the age comparison, and treat an unestablished age as no state.
- **Dispatch the fresh-context adversarial review BEFORE `push`, and give it the INVARIANTS to attack rather than the repo.** For this repo `push` IS the deploy, and a Stop hook reaches every turn of every session on seven boxes the moment it lands. On #196/#198 the suite was green, ruff was clean, a deliberate RED/GREEN differential had been captured, the original reproducer passed in all six variants, and a careful self-read had been done — and a single Opus review pass still found two CRITICALs and four MAJORs, three of which were failures of invariants the fix itself had just claimed in its own comments. Ask it explicitly to separate findings it TRIGGERED against the real script from THEORETICAL ones, and to prove test teeth by mutation; that split is what makes the report actionable instead of a list of worries. Budget note: the escalation ladder wanted Fable for an adversarial review of a safety-critical guard, `airuleset.py fable-gate` reported CLOSED at 84% of the weekly window, and Opus was the correct rung — run the gate, do not skip the review.
- **A `set -e` hook's own accountability/bookkeeping HELPER needs a guard at TWO independent levels, not one.** #381 (`block-tier0-local-build.sh`'s new audit log): guarding only the helper's internals (`mkdir -p ... 2>/dev/null || true`, the `echo >>` in a `{ ; } 2>/dev/null || true` group) is not enough by itself to trust — the review's point was that a FUTURE edit removing one internal guard silently reopens the exact `set -e` fail-open #118/#196 already catalogued (a non-writable dir or ENOSPC — the very disk-full scenario the ticket exists to fix — aborts the whole hook before its own `exit 2` runs, and Claude Code treats a non-0/non-2 hook exit as a HOOK ERROR and runs the blocked command anyway). The defense-in-depth form is to ALSO wrap every CALL SITE in `|| true` (`_log_tier0_event "blocked" || true`) — two independent guards mean one future edit dropping either one still cannot reopen the hole, verified by a mutation test that removes only the helper's internal guards and confirms the call-site `|| true` alone still holds the hook's own exit code.
- **A direct-exec regex needs a real COMMAND-POSITION anchor, not bare whitespace — the same "mention vs use" distinction #156 already made for `block-vault-store-read.sh`'s path matching, one layer up at the shell-syntax level.** #381: `(?:^|[;&|(]|\s)(?:sudo\s+)?(\S+\.sh)\b` accepted a script path preceded by ANY whitespace, so `cat ./install.sh` / `grep cargo ./install.sh` — reading or inspecting a script that is never actually invoked — false-positive blocked as if it were a direct exec. Fixed by requiring an explicit separator (`;`/`&`/`|`/`(`/newline) or start-of-string, optionally followed by `sudo `/`time `/an env assignment, before the path. Positive-control test alongside the negative one is mandatory here — tightening a boundary can silently lose real invocation shapes the review already validated (`sudo ./x.sh`, `make; ./x.sh`, `cd .. && ./x.sh`), so both directions need their own test.
- **A test assertion on an audit-log LINE cannot use a raw substring COUNT of a field name once one field legitimately holds arbitrary user text.** #381: a `cmd=${CMD}` field can legitimately contain the literal text `project=` (e.g. `git commit -m "fix project= handling"`, or — caught live — the ticket's OWN adversarial test payload), so `line.count("project=") == 1` is unachievable for any such command and is not a sound invariant, even though it reads like a natural strengthening of "no forged second line". Caught only by running the FULL repo suite as final verification (a single-file run during development doesn't exercise the same command text every time); the achievable, still-real invariant is a POSITIONAL/anchored check on the REAL field — `re.match(r"^\S+\s+project=<expected>\s", line)` — never a whole-line count. The `len(lines) == 1` sibling assertion (no forged SEPARATE physical line) is the one that actually carries the security property, and mutation-tests clean: reverting the flattening fix reproduces the exact forged line and fails it.

- **A suppression whose EXIT CONDITION is the action it suppresses can never release — and it reads as a correct, narrowly-scoped guard while you write it.** This is the sharper twin of #134's "a suppression that defers to an UNENFORCED action is a silence generator": there the guard trusted something nothing enforced; here the guard's own release depends on the very thing it is blocking, so the state is terminal by construction. #170 shipped `_goal_was_cleared_by_user` = "the newest goal marker is a clear" — true for the whole remaining life of the session, because a clear STAYS the newest marker until something arms again and the only thing that would arm it is job 9, which the clear is blocking. Live on montalu@subdev six days later: the user cleared a goal, came back, asked the session to print the arm question again, and job 9 refused every sweep for hours (`skip cleared (goal-autoarm) %0 (odoo)`) with a fresh `/goal` line sitting unarmed on screen — reported by the user as the watchdog no longer arming anything. **The test to apply to any new guard: name the event that turns it OFF, and check that event is reachable WITHOUT the suppressed action.** If it is not, the guard needs a second signal that is — here, whether the session printed a NEW arm question AFTER the clear, which both cases already carried and which separates them exactly (#170's arm question PREDATES its clear and merely survives on screen untouched, which is precisely why the viewport could not decide it; a session asking again does so after it). Verify such a fix against the REAL transcript with both builds side by side (copy the module to the box over ssh stdin, import `wd_old`/`wd_new`, print `state`/`arm_after`/verdict for each) — a unit suite cannot tell you the live session is actually in the state you think it is, and this one was.

- **Recalibrating an alert/threshold constant: the population that matters is what the CALLER feeds the comparison, never what your measurement script happened to bucket.** The burn-scan re-baseline measured a 1.39x old-to-new ratio on dev1's OWN hourly buckets and scaled the ABS threshold from it — but watchdog job 19 feeds `hourly_burn_alert` the FLEET-WIDE merged `total_usd` (7 hosts), a population whose median hour was already $73 against the old $20 threshold (83% of rows over it — the alert had been chronically firing for days and nobody could act on it). The recalibration that holds: read the real consumer-side history (`fleet.jsonl`), take p95 of the population the threshold actually gates, scale by the measured migration ratio, and write population + method + date + sample count into the constant's comment. A threshold whose comment does not NAME its population will be recalibrated against the wrong one again.

- **Extending a single-purpose artifact-gate (design markers, #136) to MULTIPLE kinds from the SAME comment-classification pass needs cross-kind exclusivity, not just per-kind classification — a rich enough comment plausibly satisfies several shapes at once, which defeats the premise that each kind proves a DIFFERENT step happened.** #213/#214 (validated/reviewed markers): a comment carrying root-cause/approach/alternative language ALSO tends to carry validation-sounding words ("confirming this still happens") and review-sounding words ("reviewing the retry path", plus an incidental sha) — confirmed live, one such comment classified `True` against all three classifiers simultaneously, and the naive per-kind loop happily wrote three markers from one comment posted before any code existed. The fix: track `claimed_urls()` (every comment url already used by ANY existing marker for that issue) and refuse a SECOND kind from the same url; and even within one fresh classification pass, stop at the FIRST still-missing kind that matches rather than granting every kind that happens to match. Reuse this shape for any future "one artifact must prove exactly one claim" gate that grows a second classified kind.
- **A hook that fires on `gh issue comment <N>` only classifies the FIRST such invocation's target when TWO calls to the same command shape land in ONE Bash tool call — `re.search` finds the first match, not the last, and PostToolUse fires once per TOOL CALL, not once per shell command inside it.** Hit live posting design-comment evidence for four sibling tickets in one Bash call (`gh issue comment 213 ...; gh issue comment 214 ...`): only #213 got a marker, because the hook's regex-extracted `target` was `213` (the first match in the whole compound command text) even though the SECOND command's own `gh issue comment` call genuinely posted a real, classifiable comment to #214. The recovery (not a bug fix — this is how the hook is DESIGNED, one command per invocation) is to re-invoke the hook directly against the real posted comment: build the exact PostToolUse payload (`{"tool_input":{"command": "gh issue comment 214 -F ..."},"cwd": ...}`) and pipe it to `bash hooks/post-record-design-comment.sh` — it re-reads the REAL GitHub state, so this is a legitimate real classification, not a workaround. Lesson for any future multi-ticket batch posting comments via this hook family: post ONE `gh issue comment` per Bash tool call, never chain several in one compound command, or budget for a manual re-trigger per missed target.
- **A test detector built around one exact historical claim-phrase goes VACUOUS the moment the surrounding prose stops using that phrase — and the fix (widen to a mention+claim two-stage detector) can itself mis-flag an HONEST non-claim if the claim-keyword set is too broad.** #216 hardening: `_ENFORCED_BY_RE` anchored on literal "enforced by ... `hook.sh`" found ZERO real matches in `agents/autopilot-worker.md` the moment its own sibling fix started saying "checked by" instead — confirmed empirically (`old_re.findall(text) == []`) even though six genuine enforcement claims existed in the file. Widening to a two-stage detector (find every `` `hook.sh` `` mention, THEN judge whether a nearby keyword window contains an enforcement claim) fixed the vacuousness but a first, naive claim-keyword set including a bare `checks?` immediately mis-flagged the CORRECTED, honest #215 text ("...it is THAT report `stop-check-playbook-review.sh` actually checks — never your own SubagentStop turn") as a false claim, because that sentence's whole POINT is a non-enforcement disclaimer that happens to contain the word "checks". Narrowing to specific claim-shapes (`enforc`, `HOOK-ENFORCED`, `blocks your stop`, `blocks the commit`, `checked by`) fixed it — and the same hardening pass caught a genuinely missing entry in `SUBAGENT_REACHABLE_EVENTS` (`PostToolUse` was absent, even though `post-record-subagent-bg-launch.sh`'s own docstring already documents that event firing for subagent-launched tool calls). Any future "does the current content still make this claim" detector needs BOTH a non-vacuousness self-test against real current content AND an honest-non-claim regression test — a detector proven only against its own hardcoded sample proves nothing about the file it is supposed to guard.
- **A statusline/shared-render label rename needs a repo-WIDE grep, never a grep scoped to the files that "own" the segment.** #223 renamed `gk-req` → `gkq` in `statusbar.py`; the label-migration sweep correctly caught every assertion in `tests/test_statusbar.py`/`tests/test_caveman.py`/`tests/test_usage_cache.py` (the files that define/exercise the segment) but missed `tests/test_gk_request.py::TestStatuslineBadge`, which lives in a completely different feature's test file (the gk-request stream→supervisor channel) yet directly asserts the shared statusline segment's rendered text (`self.assertIn("gk-req 3", seg)`). It was caught only by the FULL `airuleset.py push` pre-push suite (3603 tests), not by the narrower scoped runs used while iterating — confirming the repo's own established discipline that only the full suite is the real gate. Before renaming ANY rendered label, `grep -rn '"<old label>' tests/` across the WHOLE `tests/` tree, not just the files whose docstring claims ownership of the segment.
- **Capturing a PER-COMMIT timestamp alongside a regex match over a multi-commit `git log` blob: use a record separator (`%x1e`) at the start of each commit's format AND a field separator (`%x1f`) between the timestamp and the message — never scan the whole flattened blob with one regex.** #224's original `merged_closes` did exactly that (`--format=%s%n%b` over the whole window, one `finditer` pass) and so could only ever return bare issue numbers — nothing tied a `Closes #N` match back to WHICH commit, and therefore WHEN, it came from. `--format=%x1e%ct%x1f%s%n%b` + `text.split("\x1e")` + `rec.partition("\x1f")` gives one clean `(commit_ts, message)` record per commit for free, with zero ambiguity about which commit a match belongs to — reuse this shape for any future "which commit, and when" question over a `git log` window, instead of reaching for `--format=%H%n%ct%n%s` + a second `git show` per hash (N+1 calls) or trying to regex-split on blank lines (commit bodies routinely contain blank lines themselves).
- **Proving a per-ticket dedup fix "no longer re-pings a day later" needs the SAME real data run through OLD and NEW code side by side — a synthetic fixture proves the mechanism exists, not that it fires on the actual stuck tickets.** #224's live differential: load `git show <pre-fix-sha>:watchdog/__init__.py` and the current file as two separately-`sys.modules`-registered modules (the `@dataclass` `exec_module` trap already documented above applies here too — `sys.modules[name] = mod` BEFORE `exec_module`), call the REAL `_watchdog_closed_fetch` (a genuine, READ-ONLY `gh issue list --state closed` — safe, no writes) as `closed_fetch=` for both, run each with a FRESH empty `state` at `now` and again at `now + 86500` (a day + a bit later) against the SAME real repo, and diff the `send_fn` call counts. First attempt returned nothing sent at all for either — the tell was an EMPTY `logs` list, meaning `merged_closes` alone (no `closed_fetch` wired) found zero local `Closes #N` trailers for camera-box/forestshop-app, because those repos' issues close from the PR body (the exact tvdole-shaped signature `_watchdog_closed_fetch`'s fallback exists for) — wiring `closed_fetch=` in the differential script itself was required before the comparison meant anything. Result: OLD code sent 2 pings (one per sweep, both real, one day apart) for the identical still-unreported ticket set; NEW code sent 1 (silent on the second sweep) — this is stronger evidence than any unit test, because it is the box's own real backlog, not a constructed fixture.
- **A scratch Claude Code session (`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` recipe) proves keystroke/tmux MECHANICS with ZERO working API auth — do not treat "Not logged in" as a blocker for this class of live proof.** #225: a copied `.credentials.json`/`.claude.json` still left the scratch session reading "Not logged in · Please run /login" (root cause never chased down — irrelevant to what was being proven), yet `tmux send-keys -l '/compact'` still landed as a REAL slash command the real CC binary executed ("Not enough messages to compact." — a genuine response, not a simulation), because `list_claude_panes`/`resolve_self_pane`/`send_continue` only need a pane whose `pane_current_command == "claude"` and a MATCHING TRANSCRIPT FILE for the resolution step — nothing about them touches the model/API layer at all. When the thing under test is tmux/pane/transcript plumbing (not model behavior), a manually-written synthetic transcript entry (mirroring `_write_ctx_transcript`/`_write_marker_transcript`'s own shape) dropped into the scratch `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`'s `projects/` dir is sufficient and honest — it is the SAME technique this repo's own unit tests already use, just pointed at a REAL pane instead of a `DeliverCompactNowFakeTmux`. This is strictly stronger proof than a unit test alone (real `tmux list-panes`/`display-message`/`capture-pane`/`send-keys` round-trips, real CC binary parsing the keystrokes) while costing nothing in auth setup — don't burn time debugging scratch-session login before checking whether the proof you need even requires it.
- **`_stop_already_rejected`'s premise ("an earlier hook in THIS SAME Stop-hook batch already rejected THIS turn") only holds for a caller that genuinely runs INSIDE a Stop-hook batch — a synchronous delivery path invoked as an ordinary MID-TURN Bash tool call is not inside any batch at all, and the check will misfire against it.** #225: `compact-request --self` is meant to be called by the session itself, as its own tool call, before its turn's text even ends — at that instant there is no Stop event in progress, yet the function scans backward from the last real assistant message for a `Stop hook feedback:` entry regardless, and under an active `/goal` loop the IMMEDIATELY PRIOR turn almost always carries exactly that entry (it's what keeps the loop continuing) — so every retry in a bounded hold loop would be refused for the WHOLE window, misreading "the previous turn's stop was rejected" as "this boundary was already invalidated". The fix is a NARROW, NAMED exemption (scoped to the one origin whose caller is provably mid-turn, not the whole proven-boundary set) rather than a blanket skip — a sibling proven origin (`subagent-stop`) that genuinely IS derived from within a real hook-fired chain keeps the check, pinned by its own regression-control test. General lesson: before reusing an EXISTING gate for a NEW caller, re-read the gate's own docstring for what STRUCTURAL CONTEXT it assumes ("runs inside X"), and verify the new caller actually satisfies it — a gate that is CORRECT for its original caller can be silently wrong for a caller with different timing.
- **A "preserve trusted state instead of letting it be silently overwritten" fix needs a BOUNDED freshness window from day one, or it inverts into the opposite defect: an indefinitely-resurrectable proof that defeats the very expiry the surrounding system relies on.** #225's first cut of `record_compact_request`'s origin-preservation (a blank-origin call must not downgrade an already-recorded PROVEN origin) had no age check at all — an adversarial review found that ANY later blank-origin call for the same session would re-preserve the origin AND refresh `ts` to the new call's value, so a session producing repeated boundary reports could keep a stale proof alive forever (defeating `COMPACT_REQUEST_MAX_AGE_S`'s whole 30-minute ceiling) and could attach an OLD proof to a materially later, unrelated boundary (even a different `cwd`). The fix bounds preservation to a SHORT window (120s — sized from the one real gap it exists to cover, not a round number) measured from the PRESERVED entry's OWN `ts`, re-checked on every call rather than accumulated. Test the failure shape explicitly, not just the happy path: within-window preserves, one-second-past-window does not, a MULTI-HOP chain of blank calls each individually fresh relative to the prior hop is legitimate continuous freshness (not resurrection) and must be told apart from a single stale entry surviving via periodic refresh, and a malformed/legacy entry with a proven origin but no readable `ts` must never be treated as fresh (unmeasurable age is not zero age).
- **Before adding a NEW instruction to one of the `/goal STOP CONDITIONS` template strings in `skills/autopilot/SKILL.md`, MEASURE each of the three variants' current length against Claude Code's hard 4000-char cap FIRST — never estimate, and never assume headroom is shared across the three.** #225: `python3 -c "..."` measuring `len(line)` per template took under a second and showed the three variants sit at wildly different distances from the cap (337 / 83 / 54 chars of headroom for full / branch-merge / fork-no-merge respectively) — a change that fits comfortably in one can silently break arming FLEET-WIDE in another (#169's own incident: exceeding the cap in ANY of the three disabled `/goal` arming everywhere, discovered only when a human tried to arm one by hand). When headroom is asymmetric like this, a PARTIAL, explicitly-scoped rollout (wire the template with room, file a dedicated follow-up for the tight ones rather than guessing at trims under time pressure) beats either forcing an inconsistent trim into a fragile, already-optimized string or silently shipping the change to only one tier without saying so. Re-run `tests/test_goal_backlog_proof.py::TheTemplatesMustFitClaudeCodesGoalCap` (the existing lock) after ANY edit to these strings, for ALL three variants, not just the one touched — and add a companion test that pins the NEW instruction is actually present (a positive assertion), plus one that pins the deliberately-deferred variants are HONESTLY still absent (so the follow-up ticket's own scope can't silently rot into "forgotten" — it starts failing, in a GOOD way, the moment the follow-up lands).

- **A dedup/throttle KEY must include every field the CLAIM it makes actually depends on — narrowing the key to "the entity" alone (never "the entity + the fact")  silently merges genuinely different facts into one.** #146's first cut keyed the not-autopilot-worker DECLINE dedup on `(session_id, reason)` alone, on the stated premise "agent_type never changes mid-session" — which the SAME file's own header three paragraphs above already disproved (SubagentStop fires once per parallel tool-call branch AND per dispatched subagent, so one session routinely runs several different `agent_type` values over its life: Explore, general-purpose, ticket-validator, fork). A fresh-context review measured it on dev1's real corpus: 6 of 8 real sessions logged more than one distinct type, one logged five. The fix folds `agent_type` into the key too — the genuinely invariant thing is the (agent_type, reason) FACT once observed, never the session's non-worker status as a whole. Before shipping any "log/notify once per X" dedup, ask "what is the actual CLAIM this suppression makes, and does the key name every field that claim depends on" — a key one field too narrow reads as a working fix (it does cut the volume) while quietly deleting real diagnostic diversity.
- **`find -mtime +N` matches an age STRICTLY GREATER than N+1 FULL days, not N — a "prune after N days" comment/constant paired with a bare `-mtime +N` is off by one day, every time.** Measured directly (#146 review): files aged 13.0/13.9/14.0/14.5/14.99 days were KEPT under `-mtime +14`, and only 15.0+ was pruned — i.e. the real threshold was 15 days for a comment promising 14. `find`'s day-counting truncates to whole days from "now" and then requires the count to EXCEED N, so enforcing a genuine N-day TTL needs `-mtime "+$((N-1))"`. Cosmetic when the mismatch is one day on a multi-day TTL, but state it in the comment either way — a future reader tuning the constant will otherwise tune the wrong number.
- **A cheap `[ -e file ]` existence check followed by a separate `touch`/write is NOT atomic, and "concurrent stops can't corrupt anything, worst case is two writes" understates the real risk when the write ALSO gates a one-time LOG LINE.** #146 review finding 4: verified directly against the real hook with genuinely concurrent `subprocess.Popen` racers (feed+close every process's stdin BEFORE waiting on any of them, or they run serially and never race at all) — 12 racers for the identical dedup key produced up to 4 duplicate log lines, not the "at most 2, harmless" the original comment claimed. Fix pattern, reusable for any future "create this marker file exactly once" gate: `( set -o noclobber; : > "$seen_file" ) 2>/dev/null || return 0` — the subshell's `noclobber` doesn't leak into the caller, `>` on an existing file fails under `noclobber` (silently, via `2>/dev/null`), so at most one racer's write ever succeeds; keep the original cheap `[ -e ]` check IN FRONT of it as a fast path for the overwhelmingly common already-seen case (no subshell/fork needed when the marker already exists) and let the atomic form resolve only the genuine race. `return 0` inside an `A || return 0` top-level statement is exempt from `set -e` the same way `A && return 0` is (bash's AND/OR-list exemption, verified empirically — see the earlier `set -e` bullet in this file) — the pattern is safe to drop into any `set -euo pipefail` hook unchanged.
