- **A "closed via a merged PR" signal can be WRONG two layers deep on the SAME repo, and `merged_closes` (the LOCAL git-trailer read used by BOTH job 24 and job 25) is not immune just because it's "just reading git log" — it trusts two separate assumptions that can each be false.** Discovered live-verifying #230's deploy on odoo-erp (a genuine 3-branch repo: develop→staging→main). (a) `_git_base_ref` resolves the "delivery branch" from the CHECKOUT's own `origin/HEAD` symref, which can diverge from GitHub's actual configured default branch (`gh repo view --json defaultBranchRef`) — one sub-dev stream's checkout had `origin/HEAD` pointed at `develop` while GitHub's real default was `main`, silently making job 24/25 watch the wrong branch on that one box only (verified per-box: 2 of 3 subdev checkouts were correct, 1 was stale). (b) EVEN on the correct branch, `merged_closes`'s regex (`close(s|d)?|fix(es|ed)?|resolve(s|d)?\s+#N`) matches any commit message containing that phrasing, with NO verification that the match was GitHub's actual auto-close trigger — live-demonstrated: a commit "[green] Fix #2857 — ..." landed on `origin/main` ~1h40m BEFORE issue #2857 actually closed on GitHub (`closer: null`, hand-closed), meaning GitHub never auto-closed from that commit at all (most likely explanation: a `GITHUB_TOKEN`-authenticated CI push, which GitHub deliberately excludes from auto-close to prevent circular loops — a documented GitHub behavior, not an airuleset bug). Consequence: a repo whose commit-message convention routinely documents an already-closed ticket ("Fix #N — description", written for traceability, not to trigger a close) will make `merged_closes` claim a "delivered but unreported" state for tickets that were never merged at all — and since `merged_closes` is the PRIMARY path (job 25's own GraphQL fallback only runs when it returns EMPTY), a correct fallback fix can still fail to change the OBSERVED symptom on a repo where the primary path itself misfires. Before trusting ANY "local git says X was closed by a merge" signal on a repo you haven't personally verified, cross-check both: `git symbolic-ref refs/remotes/origin/HEAD` against `gh repo view --json defaultBranchRef`, and spot-check one matched commit's timestamp against the issue's real `closedAt`/`closer` via `gh api graphql`.
- **Post-deploy live verification on the ACTUAL box the ticket names can reveal that a correctly-implemented, correctly-tested fix does not change the user-observed symptom — because the fix's own scope was narrower than the symptom's actual cause.** #230's `_watchdog_closed_fetch` fix is provably correct in isolation (returns `{}` for odoo-erp, 52 genuinely-merged tickets with correct timestamps for camera-box, mutation-tested) — and the phone nag STILL didn't stop on the two live boxes the ticket named, because `merged_closes` (a different function, out of the ticket's stated scope, which the ticket's own root-cause section explicitly said was "NOT affected") turned out to have its own independent false-positive causes on that specific repo. The honest response was NOT to silently expand #230's scope to "fix everything" (that function is shared by job 24 too, changing it needs a real cost/correctness trade-off decision — GraphQL-verify every local match, defeating the whole "free, local-only" design, or accept a narrower syntactic match unverified against the fleet's real commit conventions) — it was to report the actual before/after journal lines VERBATIM (even though they show no visible change), file a clearly-evidenced follow-up decision ticket, and close #230 on its own correctly-narrow merits. Don't let "the ticket's own established evidence said X was fine" stop you from re-verifying X live when your own post-deploy check contradicts it — the dispatch instruction to "treat given evidence as given" is about not re-deriving what's ALREADY proven, never a license to skip verifying your OWN fix's real-world effect.
- **#232 (the follow-up #230's own retrospective predicted): a CANDIDATE producer stays cheap; VERIFICATION is applied only to the subset that would actually act.** The general shape, reusable for any future "cheap local heuristic, expensive authoritative check" job design: keep the cheap producer (`merged_closes`) completely unchanged, compute the job's OWN existing gates first (grace period, forever-dedup — here, `pingable`), and only THEN spend the expensive call, on that already-narrowed set, reusing the SAME verified seam (`closed_fetch`/`_watchdog_closed_fetch`) a sibling fallback path already built rather than writing a second query. A single per-iteration-local flag (`verified_source`) is enough to keep the fallback path (which already IS verified) and the new verify path (which ISN'T yet) mutually exclusive within one sweep — no shared state, no cross-repo leakage, because the loop body resets it every iteration. On a verification FAILURE, the safe direction (mirrored from the pre-existing sibling fallback-failure branch) is: drop every currently-pingable candidate for THAT SWEEP ONLY, add nothing to the forever-dedup set — a genuine finding gets retried next sweep rather than silently swallowed forever, and an UNVERIFIABLE-forever repo just costs one bounded API call per sweep rather than either nagging wrongly or going permanently silent.
- **A self-review that catches a real bug 3 minutes after committing GREEN is not a shortcut around the fresh-context adversarial review — dispatch it anyway, and expect it to independently reproduce what you already fixed.** #232: mutation-testing the shipped diff BEFORE dispatching a review (mentally trying "what if the `pingable` guard is missing") caught a genuine gap — the FIRST `[green]` commit (`e9826d9`) omitted `pingable and` from the verify gate, so it would have cost one wasted `gh api graphql` call every ~60s sweep for any repo with local trailer matches, even with nothing pingable. Fixed it immediately with its own RED (`12a8515`, proven by `git stash push -- <impl file>` against the ALREADY-COMMITTED buggy version, not the working tree) → GREEN (`002aa88`) pair. The dispatched fresh-context review, working from a stale copy of the diff, INDEPENDENTLY reproduced the identical gap via its own mutation testing and only then discovered (mid-review) that the implementer had already shipped the fix — which is the review doing its job correctly, not a wasted dispatch: it confirmed the self-caught fix was the RIGHT fix (not just "a" fix) and went on to find 3 more real 🔵 gaps (`dry_run` untested, missing multi-repo isolation test, a pre-existing sibling gap in #230's own fallback) that the self-review missed entirely. Never skip the adversarial-review dispatch because a self-review already found something — the two catch structurally different classes of gap.
- **`git commit` followed minutes later by `git diff HEAD -- <file>` showing an UNEXPECTED non-empty diff is proof a prior `git add && git commit` staged a DIFFERENT byte-content than what a later `Edit`/inspection shows on disk — investigate before assuming the working tree is simply "ahead" of the commit.** #232: `git show <sha>:watchdog/__init__.py` at one point genuinely lacked a one-line guard (`pingable and`) that a `grep` on the on-disk file confirmed WAS present — i.e. the commit captured an earlier state of the file than what the immediately-preceding `git stash pop` had just restored. Root cause not fully isolated (a `git stash push -- <file>` / `git stash pop` cycle interposed between two Edit-tool sessions is the leading suspect), but the RECOVERY pattern is what matters and is reusable: treat the discrepancy as a genuine correctness question (never assume it's cosmetic), write a NEW regression test that would fail against the COMMITTED (not the in-memory-assumed) code, confirm true RED via `git stash push -- <impl file>` (proving the CURRENT git history is buggy, not just a hypothesis), then commit a normal RED→GREEN pair fixing it. Never silently `git add` the working tree "to be safe" without first proving — via a real failing test against the actual commit — that there is a real behavioral gap to fix.
- **Pruning a shared JSON state file that a systemd `--user` TIMER writes every ~60s from a SEPARATE ssh session is a genuine TOCTOU race, not a "low-stakes, why bother locking" edge case — it bit on the very first attempt.** #232: a plain read-modify-write prune (read the file, drop one key, write back atomically via `mktemp`+`os.replace`) reported success (`PRUNED ... -- entry removed`), and re-reading the file MINUTES later showed the EXACT pre-prune bytes restored (same nested dict, same float timestamps to the 13th decimal) — proof a concurrent sweep had already loaded the OLD state into memory before the prune landed, and its own end-of-run unconditional `state["card_unreported"] = {...}` write silently clobbered the prune on its next 60s tick. The fix that actually held: `systemctl --user stop api-watchdog.timer` → prune → **read the file back immediately, in the SAME ssh session, before declaring success** → `systemctl --user start api-watchdog.timer`. A stop/prune/verify/restart window of a few seconds against a 60s-cadence timer is well within the "the work itself" exemption (`no-destructive-remote-actions.md`) — it is not an unrelated-service stop needing separate approval. Generalize: any one-off external edit to a state file a live poller owns needs either this stop-verify-restart bracket, or an immediate same-session read-back proving the write survived at least one tick — a write that merely reports its own success is not evidence it stuck.
- **An idempotent MARKER-BLOCK function's own "does a valid block already exist" check must be a STRUCTURAL span scan, never two independent substring membership tests — the failure mode is not just a stale no-op, it can be actual DATA LOSS one run later.** #235 (`apply_tmux_history_limit`, mirroring `apply_ultracode_launcher` #77's `if MARK_START in existing and MARK_END in existing:` shape): a naive whole-file `re.compile(START + r".*?" + END, re.S)` search, on a conf file externally corrupted with END appearing BEFORE START, correctly finds nothing on the FIRST run (so it self-heals by appending a fresh clean block) — but on the SECOND run, the lazy `.*?` now matches from the stray leftover START all the way to the FRESH block's END (the nearest END after that stray START in file order), silently deleting every real directive sitting between them. The fix (`_clean_tmux_block_spans`) is a left-to-right positional scan (`str.find`, not regex) that only accepts a START...END pair as "clean" when NO other marker literal falls between them — an unpaired/crossed marker is skipped and left as inert literal text forever, never merged with anything else, so the function is safe across ANY number of runs regardless of how it got corrupted. Found only by a fresh-context adversarial review dispatched BEFORE `push` (this repo's own live deploy) — a synthetic "does it look about right" self-read of the diff missed it, same as #189/#193/#194/#196's own dispatch-before-push lesson. **`apply_ultracode_launcher` (#77) has the IDENTICAL latent bug and was deliberately left unfixed** (out of #235's named scope — filed as #237 instead of silently expanding scope, per the bundling/follow-up gate).
- **Waiting for a dispatched async `Agent` task from INSIDE a subagent: a bare `sleep N; <command>` chain gets pre-emptively BLOCKED by the harness ("use Monitor... or run_in_background"), but `Monitor` is the WRONG fix here — it registers as a SECOND in-flight background task the SubagentStop gate now makes you manage too, not a way to satisfy the "wait foreground" requirement.** Live-hit waiting on #235's own review dispatch: `sleep 240; echo checkpoint` was blocked outright; dispatching a `Monitor` heartbeat loop to work around it "succeeded" but the VERY NEXT Stop-hook check listed BOTH the original `Agent` task id AND the new `Monitor` task id as in-flight work still owned by this subagent — net negative, not a fix. The actual correct pattern (confirmed working): `TaskStop` the `Monitor` task immediately (it adds nothing `ci-monitoring.md`'s own foreground poll-loop shape doesn't already give you), then issue ONE real blocking Bash call containing a `for`/`sleep` loop with a `SECONDS`-based deadline INSIDE it — e.g. `DEADLINE=$((SECONDS+540)); for i in $(seq 1 18); do sleep 30; [ "$SECONDS" -ge "$DEADLINE" ] && break; done` — which the harness does NOT treat as a "blind wait" needing Monitor/run_in_background (unlike a bare `sleep N` with no loop structure), and which genuinely blocks the subagent's own turn until it returns, keeping the subagent ALIVE for the async task-notification to land on. Repeat the single blocking call as many times as needed; each return is a natural checkpoint to see whether the Stop-hook gate still lists the task as in-flight.
- **Flipping tmux's `window-size` policy to `manual` against a RUNNING server is ITSELF a disruptive resize event — independent of `resize-window` and independent of any `#{...}` format-expansion query.** #236's original design (extend #235's already-shipped live-apply to also live-apply `window-size manual` + `default-size 176x50` against a running server) looked safe: no per-window resize call, no live format query — exactly the two mechanisms the ticket's own prior incidents had already been traced to. A fresh-context adversarial review, and an independent follow-up repro against this box's own live tmux binary, both proved a THIRD pathway: the moment `window-size` flips from `latest` to `manual`, tmux recalculates every existing window's size against its STORED/created size (or `default-size`), discarding whatever size the currently attached client is actually rendering at — a session created 176x50, attached from a 200x60 client (auto-tracked to 200x59 under `latest`), snapped straight back to 176x50 the instant `manual` was set. **Reproducing this needs a REAL attached pty client — a bare detached session (`tmux -L <sock> new-session -d -x W -y H`, no attach) shows NO resize at all**, because `recalculate_sizes()` has nothing to reconcile against without a live client; a quick sanity check without an attached client will silently miss the bug. Minimal repro recipe (throwaway socket, `python3`'s `pty.fork()` + `fcntl.ioctl(fd, termios.TIOCSWINSZ, ...)` to drive a real attached client to a specific size, then `tmux -L <sock> set-option -g window-size manual` from a separate control connection and re-read `list-windows -F '#{window_width}x#{window_height}'`). The fix, and the general lesson: an option whose SEMANTICS govern how tmux computes a LIVE, already-rendered value (window geometry, in this case) can be exactly as disruptive to live-apply as the operation it was meant to replace — "no resize-window call, no format query" is necessary but not sufficient proof of safety; the option's own effect on already-open windows has to be checked too, ideally with a real attached client, before trusting a live `set-option` is inert. #236 landed with `window-size`/`default-size` CONF-ONLY (written to `~/.tmux.conf`, taking effect at the next server/session/window start) while `history-limit` stays live-applied (proven safe, unaffected by this finding — it does not change how any already-rendered value is computed). **CORRECTION (#241): `window-size manual` is no longer shipped at all, conf-only or otherwise.** It turned out to have a SECOND, unrelated failure mode beyond the live-apply resize this bullet documents: reading it from a conf file CRASHES tmux 3.4's server outright at startup (`server exited unexpectedly`) — confirmed live against the real 3.4 binary every managed box runs, the only version Ubuntu 24.04 noble ships. Unlike the live-apply finding above (a live `set-option` against a running server was the trigger), this is a conf-READ-time failure with no safe way to keep the option at all, so it was removed from `render_tmux_history_block` at the source. `default-size 176x50` is unaffected and still ships conf-only, starts cleanly on 3.4, and is what delivers the fixed-geometry goal for NEW windows — see #236's own comment thread for the accepted trade-off (without `manual`, an existing window's live size is no longer pinned against later attach/detach cycles). `history-limit` stays live-applied, unaffected either way.
- **"Safe to LIVE-APPLY" and "safe to WRITE TO A CONF FILE" are two independent claims about the same tmux option — proving one does NOT prove the other, and #236/#241 found a genuinely different failure mode for each half of `window-size manual`.** #236's adversarial review proved the LIVE-APPLY half unsafe (flipping it against a running server snaps every window back to its stored size — a disruptive resize) and responded by making the option CONF-ONLY, which is the right fix for that specific failure. #241 then found the SEPARATE, conf-READ-time half was ALSO unsafe: tmux 3.4 crashes its own server outright the moment it reads `window-size manual` from a conf file at startup, regardless of whether anything ever live-applies it. Conf-only was a necessary mitigation for the first finding and a complete non-mitigation for the second — the option had to be removed entirely. General lesson for evaluating whether ANY tmux/systemd/config-driven option is safe to ship: check its effect at APPLY time (a live `set-option`/reload) and at PARSE/STARTUP time (a fresh server/process reading the file cold) as two SEPARATE questions, each needing its own real reproduction against the real binary/version in use — a real attached pty client for the live case (`recalculate_sizes()` has nothing to reconcile against without one, so a detached-only test misses it entirely), a real cold `-f <conf> new-session -d` start against the real deployed version for the parse case (this box's default `tmux` resolves to a NEWER 3.7b at `/usr/local/bin/tmux`, which does NOT reproduce the crash — only `/usr/bin/tmux` 3.4, the actual fleet version, does).
- **A root-owned, boot-time provisioning unit that flips a live-server-sensitive resource (here: the `/usr/local/bin/tmux` symlink) must be INSTALLED + ENABLED unconditionally by `install`, but NEVER STARTED — the actual action only ever fires at the box's own next reboot, when the precondition ("nothing is using the old resource yet") is structurally guaranteed rather than merely checked.** #242: `cmd_install()` runs on every `push`, far more often than any box reboots, so a provisioning function that both installs AND starts such a unit would repeat the exact #240/#241 live-server-mismatch failure on every single push. `DefaultDependencies=no` + `Before=sysinit.target ssh.service ssh.socket` (the earliest possible boot hook) is what makes "next reboot" provably before any login/client/server can exist — reuse this ordering + never-start split for any future "flip a shared resource that a live process depends on" provisioning ticket.
- **`[ -e "$FILE" ]` (existence) is the wrong guard for "is this build usable" — use `[ -x "$FILE" ]` (executable) whenever the file in question is a binary a script is about to symlink/exec.** #242's adversarial review caught this: a truncated or interrupted copy of a locally-built binary (`tmux-3.7b`) passes `-e` but should never become the live target. Reusable for any future "does the newer build exist yet" provisioning check.
- **`Type=oneshot` defaults `TimeoutStartSec` to INFINITY, and a unit ordered ahead of `sysinit.target`/`ssh.service` for early-boot correctness can therefore block boot — and therefore ssh — fleet-wide if its script ever hangs.** #242's adversarial review flagged the missing bound; `TimeoutStartSec=30` (or any sane cap) converts an unbounded-hang failure mode into the benign one, since a failed/timed-out oneshot does NOT block boot on its own when it's `Wants`-pulled (not `Requires`-pulled) — but ONLY if the bound is actually set. Any future early-boot custom unit needs this checked explicitly; it's easy to write a correct-looking unit that omits it.
- **A test asserting "this dangerous flag/action is never issued" via `"start" in argv_list` checks LIST MEMBERSHIP, not substring/family membership — it silently misses sibling shapes that mean the same thing** (`enable --now`, a bare `restart` element) — and this repo's OWN dominant pattern for its other two managed units (file-drop, api-watchdog) IS `enable --now`, so a future editor pattern-matching the codebase would plausibly "fix" a new unit to match it, defeating exactly the invariant the test exists to protect. #242's adversarial review caught it; fix is a shared assertion helper checking ALL known-equivalent shapes as separate `assertNotIn` calls, not one substring/membership check. Same family as this file's other "presence is not the claim" test-quality traps (`blocked()` helper, `count_hook_blocks`).
- **A "does nothing when already correct" no-op test is BEHAVIORALLY TAUTOLOGICAL against an "always re-apply unconditionally" mutant whenever the applied value would be identical either way — a plain writable sandbox cannot distinguish "skipped the write" from "re-wrote the same bytes".** #242's adversarial review: `chmod 0555` the sandbox DIRECTORY before running the already-correct case — the correct script (genuine compare-then-skip) never attempts a write and still exits 0; a mutant that always `ln -sfn`s hits a real permission-denied write failure against the read-only dir and fails the test. Reusable pattern for any future "prove a comparison gate is real, not just present in the source text" test.
- **The gatekeeper `~/.ssh/subdev_admin` root-hop pattern (already documented above for `apt-get install` per #171) generalizes cleanly to installing/enabling a systemd unit on the shared subdev box too** — same `ssh -i ~/.ssh/subdev_admin root@subdev '<remote command>'` shape, fired from a NEW function that `cmd_install()` calls unconditionally (true no-op via identity-file-presence detection on every box except gatekeeper, per #68's own trust discriminator). #242's version writes root-owned files (`sudo`-equivalent write via `tee`, since the ssh session is already root) instead of `apt-get install`, and layers the SAME "install+enable, never start" boot-safety split on top. Reuse this shape for any future "one root-level operation on subdev covers all four stream accounts" ticket, rather than inventing a new subdev-reaching mechanism.
- **CC renders the session's EFFORT MODE into the input box's own TOP border (`──── ultracode ─`) — `_is_separator_line`'s deliberate strictness rejects it, so a separator-pair box scan must accept the labelled `_is_border_rule` form for the TOP edge while the BOTTOM edge stays strict (it is the anchor, always pure on a real pane).** And the companion trap from the same incident: any chrome detector anchored on which segment LEADS the managed statusline breaks at the next reorder — the #223 reorder silently killed `startswith("ctx ")` fleet-wide, blinding the glyph fallback for a day; match the segment VOCABULARY with >=2 co-occurring shapes (`_statusline_hits`), and when REORDERING the statusline (any #223-class change), grep watchdog's chrome/boundary detectors for anchored assumptions BEFORE shipping. Both faults had to coincide to be visible at all (only the one ultracode pane failed both paths), which is why the regression ran a day undetected (#243).
- **A transcript that QUOTES an input box defeats an anywhere-in-capture structural scan — three guards hold it (adversarial review of the #243 fix): a candidate pair is rejected when a non-chrome row BELOW its bottom edge starts with the prompt glyph or carries "esc to interrupt" (the real prompt/turn is elsewhere); the box HEAD is found by walking up past non-glyph content rows (so a pasted table row `│ a │ b │ c │` inside a wrapped draft is never taken as the top border) but never past a strict separator; and the row immediately above the head must be a border.** Residual quoted-box shape documented in #245. The review found all three against a green 3772-test suite with mutation-checked guards — the dispatch-before-push adversarial review remains the only thing that catches this class.
- **The Nth recurrence of "an EMPTY result from a `run`-fed helper (`list_claude_panes`, `_default_run`) means a genuine negative, not 'could not measure'" — this file has now documented the confusion at least three times (#88's line-continuation splicing, #172's kill-mid-sweep, and now #199) and it keeps re-appearing because a plain `if not X:` reads identically whether X is empty-because-nothing-exists or empty-because-the-READ itself failed.** #199's own fix (`live_pane_ids`, built from `list_claude_panes(run)`) initially pruned every `pwedge:`/`pwedge-ping:` entry the moment that set was empty — but `_default_run` degrades to `""` on ANY tmux read failure (timeout, tmux not running, an ssh blip), which makes `list_claude_panes` return `[]` regardless of whether panes are genuinely gone or the sweep simply couldn't see them. An adversarial review caught it with a one-line reproduction: feed a `run` callable that returns `""` unconditionally (mirroring `_default_run`'s real failure shape, not a hand-picked edge case) and watch a LIVE pane's own state get wiped alongside the dead one. The fix is the same shape this file already uses elsewhere (`_watchdog_delivery_probe`'s `fetch_error` sentinel, job 27/28's `persist=` degrade-to-local-only): **when a signal you are about to treat as a negative could ALSO be produced by measurement failure, check for the failure signature explicitly and skip acting rather than guessing** — here, `if not live_pane_ids: continue` before ever comparing a pane id against the set. Before writing `if not <something built from a run()/fetch() call>: <treat as genuine negative>` anywhere in this file, ask what that same emptiness looks like when the underlying READ itself failed, and whether the two are distinguishable at all from the caller's side.
- **A fix landing NEAR existing code must be checked against every EARLY-EXIT (`continue`/`return`) that sits BEFORE it in the same loop or function — placement, not just correctness of the new logic itself, decides whether the fix actually fires.** #182's reopen-clear step was written as a new block correctly reasoning about markers/reopened-issues in isolation, and was inserted textually next to the existing "two ways a ticket can already be reported" comment — which happened to be AFTER `if not closed: seen.pop(root, None); continue`. Any repo where nothing closed in THIS sweep's 48h window (the exact shape of a ticket whose own first close aged out, then reopened, with no sibling ticket closing to keep `closed` non-empty) never reached the new block at all — silently reproducing the very bug the ticket was filed to fix. An adversarial review live-reproduced it by building that precise scenario against the real, unmutated function. The general check, worth running on every "add a step to an existing per-item loop" ticket: list every `continue`/`return`/`break` already in that loop BEFORE the insertion point, and ask whether the new step's own precondition is a strict SUBSET of what each earlier exit already required — if the new step needs to fire under conditions an earlier `continue` would already have skipped past, it has to move ahead of that exit, not sit wherever was textually convenient.
- **Defer/suppression exit conditions must anchor on the FIRST-OBSERVED time, never on a field a later write overwrites.** #250: the `COMPACT_DEFER_GRACE_S` window was first keyed on the request's own `ts`, which every re-record refreshes — a session that completes tickets faster than the grace window NEVER reached delivery at all (the adversarial review's MAJOR finding). The fix anchors on `deferred_since`, stamped once at the FIRST observed defer and preserved unconditionally across re-records. Companion to this file's #134 lesson (a suppression needs a reachable exit condition): the exit condition ALSO needs to live on a clock nothing else resets. An env override of a safety window (here `AIRULESET_COMPACT_DEFER_GRACE_S`, clamped to `[1, TTL)`) should be bounded the same way, so the override can neither disable the mechanism nor recreate the starvation.
- **A flaky test fix must remove the wall-clock dependence, then get PROVEN by repetition — not just re-read as plausible.** #179 (strip ISO timestamps before the length-leak assertion, so a coincidental digit match can no longer trip it) and #226 (real TTL headroom instead of a bare comparison, closing a TTL-vs-subprocess-overhead race) both shipped only after ~30 and 15 consecutive green runs respectively, #226's proof additionally forced under deliberate CPU saturation to stress the exact race window. A flaky test "fixed" without a repetition proof is still a guess.
- **An env-tunable loop/sweep budget MUST clamp non-positive values, or the "tunable" becomes an off-switch.** #172's adversarial review: `AIRULESET_SWEEP_BUDGET_S` had no `<= 0` guard, so a misconfigured (or maliciously set) non-positive value would disable the whole per-transcript wall-clock self-bound instead of shortening it — the exact livelock the ticket existed to fix, reachable through the override meant to prevent it. Any future env-tunable timing guard needs the same floor.
- **A helper that accepts `now=` for testability must have `now` threaded at EVERY production call site — a single missed site silently reads real wall-clock and the test suite stays green throughout.** #172's adversarial review caught `_human_clock`'s production call not passing `now=`, so it diverged from `run_once`'s own timeline in production while every test (which always passes `now=`) kept passing. When adding a `now=` seam, grep for ALL call sites of the function, not just the ones in the diff under review.
- **An `authorized_keys` line's trailing COMMENT field is pure cosmetic metadata — never trust it as identity, especially during a cross-system access review.** #258: a genuine, correct, security-motivated access cleanup on a DIFFERENT repo's session (gatekeeper, working odoo-erp#2961) stripped dev1's own push-automation SSH key from montalu/montalu2/montalu3's `authorized_keys`, because the key's comment field happened to read `david grena mac` — misattributing it to the real, unrelated `david@subdev` sub-dev account (cross-company access, correctly not sanctioned there). The key was in fact byte-identical to dev1's own default `~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub` (confirmed via direct base64-blob comparison, not by trusting either comment string). Fixed by restoring the SAME key bytes under a corrected, unambiguous comment (`dev1-airuleset-push (NOT david@subdev — see airuleset#258)`) via the sanctioned `root@subdev` hop. Lesson for any future SSH-access audit touching a shared box: diff the actual base64 key material against every account's OWN expected identity before removing a line on the strength of its comment alone — a `.bak-<date>` backup of the pre-change file (which gatekeeper's own cleanup script correctly left behind) is what made this root-cause traceable at all.
- **`hooks/block-subdev-ssh-misuse.sh` inspects the LITERAL argv text of the Bash tool call — it never sees shell variable expansion.** A `for u in montalu2 montalu3 montalu4; do ssh "$u@subdev" ...; done` loop is blocked outright with `ssh as unauthorized user '$u'@subdev` (the literal two-character token `$u`, never expanded), even when every individual expansion would be on the allow-list. This is not a hook bug — the hook receives the exact command STRING Claude Code is about to run, before any shell executes it, so it structurally cannot see what a variable will expand to. When investigating multiple subdev accounts (verifying montalu2/montalu3/montalu4 in #251/#258), issue one command per literal username instead of looping — the same discipline the hook's own header already documents for `sudo su - <user>` / wrapper-script indirection ("invisible to argv-level matching").
- **Refactoring `cmd | while read` into a split/process-substitution form drops the pipeline's `set -e` coverage for the producer side — the read loop keeps running past a failed producer instead of the whole pipeline failing.** #180's adversarial review: splitting the `jq`-piped read this way let a marker-file read failure crash the hook uncaught instead of hitting the fail-closed path, reproduced via `chmod 000` on the marker file. Any future `cmd | while read` → split rewrite needs its own explicit failure check on the producer, not an assumption that `set -e` still covers it.
- **A "scan the whole command text for a boundary character preceded by `cd`" heuristic is a genuine injection surface, and quote-stripping is the wrong fix for it — ANCHOR to the command's own literal START instead.** #187/#220: the first draft of `notify.resolve_work_cwd` (repo resolution for `block-commit-without-design.sh` when a worker's session cwd differs from the repo a `git commit` actually lands in) scanned for a `cd <path>` preceded by ANY `;`/`&`/`|` anywhere in the command text. A fresh-context adversarial review found this CRITICAL: a `cd /path && ...` literal sitting inside a HEREDOC BODY (not a quoted span, so `_strip_quoted_spans` — my own follow-up hardening for a DIFFERENT self-found injection, `;cd /path` inside a quoted echo argument — could not see it) was misread as a real statement boundary, reachable via ordinary commit-message prose quoting a runbook recipe, and because `design_gate.required_refs(missing, work_cwd)` ALSO used the resolved (spoofable) directory for the #206 closed-issue `gh` exemption, this could silently DISABLE the whole design gate. The quote-stripping hardening itself was ALSO a regression: it blanked a legitimate `cd "/path with spaces"` along with the injection vector it defended against. The fix that closes BOTH at once: require `cd` to be the command's own FIRST token (`^\s*cd\s+...`, `re.match` not `re.search`) — nothing (a heredoc body, a quoted argument, an earlier statement) can ever appear before position 0, so no scan-anywhere heuristic or quote-stripping is needed at all, and a quoted `cd` ARGUMENT can be parsed directly (`"([^"]*)"|'([^']*)'|(\S+)`) instead of destroyed. This also tracks real bash semantics exactly: if the command genuinely begins with `cd X &&`, bash really does execute that first. General lesson: when a "does this text merely MENTION shell syntax vs. really contain it" question can't be answered by quote-stripping alone (heredoc bodies are the counterexample — they DO really execute), prefer ANCHORING the match to a structural position the injected text structurally cannot reach, over trying to enumerate every place the injected text might hide.
- **A "does the guard matter" test is TAUTOLOGICAL under mutation whenever the test's OWN setup makes the correct-code path and the broken-mutant path land on the SAME observable outcome for DIFFERENT reasons.** #187's adversarial review: `test_cd_to_a_non_repo_path_falls_back_to_payload_cwd` placed a marker under the payload repo BEFORE testing a `cd` to a non-existent path — with the "is this a real repo" guard present, resolution correctly falls back to the payload repo, where the marker exists → pass(0); with the guard REMOVED (a mutant that trusts any `cd` target unconditionally), the invalid path is passed straight through, the OUTER caller's own `repo_name_for(work_cwd)` independently fails on the garbage path → also pass(0), via `if not repo_key: exit 0`. Same observable result, opposite reasons — the test caught nothing. Fix: remove the marker so the two paths diverge (correct code → falls back to a REAL repo with no marker → block(2); mutant → repo_key="" → pass(0)). General check: before trusting a "the guard matters" test, ask whether removing the guard could reach the SAME pass/fail outcome through a DIFFERENT code path (often a downstream "unmeasurable → never guess" exit that both the correct and broken paths funnel through) — if so, the test needs a scenario where only the correct path's SPECIFIC behavior (not just its fail-safe direction) produces the expected result.
- **A test fixture needing "does the fake `gh` see a DIFFERENT repo depending on which directory it's invoked from" (i.e. testing that a function correctly passes a RESOLVED `cwd=` to a `gh`/subprocess call, not the raw one) should be a PYTHON script, not bash with sed/awk** — sed/awk's `$`-inside-DOUBLE-quotes trap (`"s#\.git$##"` silently expands `$#` as bash's arg-count parameter before sed ever sees it, producing "unterminated `s' command" or silently wrong output) makes a bash one-liner doing `repo_name_for`-style URL parsing (strip `.git`, replace `:` with `/`, take the last path segment) fragile in exactly the way this repo's own `repo_name_for()` function already avoids by using Python. #187's M8 regression test (proving `design_gate.required_refs(missing, work_cwd)` queries the #206 closed-issue exemption against the RESOLVED work repo, not the payload cwd) needed a `gh` stub whose CLOSED/OPEN answer depends on `git remote get-url origin` read from ITS OWN process cwd (which IS the resolved `work_cwd`, since `_gh_issue_state` invokes `subprocess.run(argv, cwd=cwd, ...)`) — writing the stub as `#!/usr/bin/env python3` doing the exact same `url.replace(':', '/').split('/')[-1]` parse `repo_name_for` uses sidesteps the whole quoting class of bug and is what actually got the mutation-proof test working on the first correct attempt.
- **`tmux send-keys -l` does NOT auto-wrap its payload in ANSI bracketed-paste framing (`ESC[200~`/`ESC[201~`), even when the target pane's application has requested bracketed-paste mode — confirmed empirically, not from documentation alone.** #255: built an isolated throwaway tmux session (`tmux -L wd255test`) running a raw-mode Python reader that first emits `\x1b[?2004h` (exactly what Claude Code does to request the mode) then dumps every byte it receives; `send-keys -l "payload"` delivered the literal bytes with zero bracket framing either side. `man tmux` confirms this is correct, not a version quirk: only `paste-buffer -p` ("paste bracket control codes are inserted around the buffer if the application has requested bracketed paste mode") does the wrapping — `send-keys` documents no such behavior at all. This is why the #172 `sweep_budget_s` self-bound was NOT the cause of a real stuck-pane incident (a typed nudge sat unsubmitted, every retry Enter swallowed as literal text, only a manual `send-keys -H 1b 5b 32 30 31 7e` + Enter — the literal ESC[201~ paste-end marker — recovered it): the incident's own journal showed the sweep finished cleanly with no kill, and every delivery path in `watchdog/__init__.py` (`send_continue`, `deliver_with_stash`, `prompt_wedge_check`'s machine-submit) is `send-keys -l` for the payload, so nothing in this codebase was ever capable of emitting a real bracket-paste START at all — Claude Code's own paste-pending state has some OTHER trigger (most plausibly an internal heuristic on burst-arrival timing, since `send-keys -l` delivers a whole payload far faster than human typing), and once CC is in it, only the literal END marker recovers it; bare Enter retries (even with a leading Escape) never do. Reuse this finding before ever proposing "send-keys pastes, so X" reasoning for any future tmux/CC delivery bug — verify against a real session, the assumption is wrong.
- **A cheap sliding-window diff over a REAL `tmux capture-pane -p -S -<N>` dump is strong, concrete proof of Claude Code's own TUI renderer duplicating transcript bands into tmux's native scrollback — and this exact defect is an OPEN, actively-tracked upstream regression, not something airuleset's tmux config can fix.** #253: an 8-line sliding window over a live pane's full history, deduped for near-duplicate windows within a short line-distance of each other, found a real 25-line chunk of a prior completion report duplicated VERBATIM 40 lines apart — direct, reproducible evidence (not reasoning from docs) that #235's own "resize pushes a duplicate copy of the visible transcript into history" diagnosis is still live on CC 2.1.222, well past every version discussed upstream. `gh api search/issues -f q='repo:anthropics/claude-code <keywords>'` found the exact match already tracked and open: `anthropics/claude-code#84247` ("TUI resize duplicates bands of already-emitted lines into scrollback") and `#46834` ("TUI relayouts spill entire transcript into scrollback as duplicate copies", with an independent bisection pinning the regression to v2.1.101 and confirming a later "fixed in 2.1.116" changelog entry did NOT actually fix this variant). The one community-verified mitigation (`CLAUDE_CODE_NO_FLICKER=1`, switching to the alternate-screen TUI so CC never writes into native scrollback at all) is a genuine UX tradeoff (loses native tmux copy-mode / OS-level scrollback search), so it shipped as an OPT-IN launcher mode (`claude-fullscreen`) rather than a forced default — see the `apply_ultracode_launcher`/`CLAUDE_LAUNCH_SCRIPT_CONTENT` shape this repo already uses for `claude-ultracode`/`claude-new`/`claude-plain`. Reuse the sliding-window-diff technique for any future "is CC's renderer corrupting scrollback" investigation, and check `anthropics/claude-code` issue search FIRST before assuming a defect needs a new upstream report — this bug class already has multiple actively-discussed trackers.
- **A per-pane escalation counter that must persist ACROSS a delivery function's own `state.pop(key, None)` reset cycle needs its OWN independent `(hash, count)` pair — coupling it to the primary episode-tracking key's lifecycle silently zeroes it on every single retry.** #255's first draft popped the new `pwedge-submit-attempts:<pid>` counter in the SAME branch that resets `pwedge:<pid>` after a genuinely-new draft appears (`if st.get("hash") != h:`) — but that branch ALSO fires on the very NEXT sweep after a machine-submit attempt, because the attempt itself pops `pwedge:<pid>` as part of "still stuck → retries in 2 sweeps", making the SAME still-stuck draft look "new" from that check's point of view. Escalation could never be reached: attempt 1 set counter=1, then the next tracking cycle's "fresh draft" branch reset it to 0 before attempt 2 could increment it. Caught by a RED test that drove `PWEDGE_SUBMIT_UNSTICK_AFTER + 1` full attempt-cycles and asserted the unstick sequence fired — it didn't, revealing the coupling bug directly (not found by review, found by the test actually failing at 0 unstick calls after 3 attempts). Fix: store the escalation counter as its own `{"hash": h, "n": count}` dict, incremented only when the CURRENT draft's hash matches the STORED hash in that same key (never the sibling episode key), and reset it ONLY where the draft is provably gone (`if not txt:`) — never in a branch whose firing condition can be caused by the counter's own prior action.
- **Sending a proven manual recovery sequence AUTOMATICALLY must send EXACTLY that sequence — prepending an existing, seemingly-unrelated "safety" keystroke can silently defeat it or trigger a WORSE hazard.** #255's first draft prepended the ordinary #36 pre-Escape to every machine-submit attempt, including the escalation attempt that sends the bracketed-paste-END unstick (`send-keys -H 1b 5b 32 30 31 7e`) — but that sequence's own FIRST byte is ALSO `0x1b` (ESC), so the two back-to-back `send-keys` calls put TWO ESC bytes on the wire with nothing between them: `^[ ^[ [ 2 0 1 ~`. Verified empirically (an isolated throwaway tmux socket, `cat -v` reader) rather than assumed. This is precisely the rapid-double-escape shape this file already documents as PERMANENTLY DELETING a draft (issue #35), and separately risks the two ESCs being parsed as an Alt-modified CSI instead of a genuine bracketed-paste end marker — i.e. the "fix" could be simultaneously unsafe AND non-functional. A fresh-context adversarial review caught it by walking the exact byte sequence sent to the pty, not by reading the code in isolation. Lesson: when composing a proven-effective manual recovery into an automated retry path, reuse the EXACT sequence (nothing prepended, nothing appended) — if an existing "always send X first" step is already in the branch, it has to be REMOVED for the branch that sends a proven sequence whose own bytes could collide with it, not merely left in place because "it was always there."
- **A `sweep_deadline`/wall-clock self-bound must be scoped to the SPECIFIC loop it protects — reusing the SAME deadline for a job that runs strictly AFTER that loop silently zeroes the later job's own intended margin.** #255's first draft passed the per-transcript PANE LOOP's own `sweep_deadline` (`time_fn() + SWEEP_WALL_CLOCK_BUDGET_S`, 90s) straight into `bounce_backstop`/`goal_autoarm`, which both run strictly AFTER that loop finishes. The 90s-budget/120s-hard-kill split was always DESIGNED to leave a ~30s margin for "the remaining lightweight jobs" (the loop's own docstring already says so) — but whenever the pane loop legitimately used its FULL 90s (measured live: 26 of 3837 real sweeps over 3 days), jobs 8/9 received an ALREADY-EXPIRED deadline and broke at their very first item, doing zero work for the whole sweep — silently, with no error, exactly when a genuine backlog is most likely to exist (a busy box is both why the pane loop ran long AND why bounce tickets are piling up). For job 8 specifically this compounded with a PRE-EXISTING cadence-stamp-before-work design (`b["last_check"]` is set and persisted before the per-target loop, a deliberate #172 fix for a DIFFERENT problem — surviving a mid-sweep kill) — so the zero-work sweep also burned the full 30-minute `BOUNCE_INTERVAL` for nothing. Fix: give the LATER job its own EXTENDED deadline (`sweep_deadline + TAIL_BUDGET_S`, still comfortably under the hard kill), never the earlier loop's bare deadline. General lesson: before threading an existing deadline/budget value into a NEW caller, check whether that caller runs BEFORE or AFTER the code the deadline was originally sized for — "after" needs its own extended budget, not the same one.
- **A "regression-guard" test that measures the SAME quantity as the test it protects must literally SHARE that test's constants (a class attribute), never carry its own independent copy — an adversarial review proved this the hard way twice in one batch (#148, and see the earlier #226/#194 bound-sizing entries above for the sibling lesson on SIZING such a bound from real data).** #148 widened a flaky subprocess-timing bound and added a second test meant to guard it — but the new test hardcoded its OWN `ttl = 20` / bound, so a full REVERT of the widened test back to the exact pre-fix flaky values (`ttl=6`, `bound=ttl/3.0`) passed the whole class clean; the "guard" checked nothing. Fix: hoist `FAST_FAIL_TTL`/`FAST_FAIL_DIVISOR` to shared `TestCase` class attributes both tests read, plus a zero-wall-clock `assertGreater(bound, forced_delay_s * 2.0, ...)` margin check in the guard test itself — verified by mutating the shared constant and confirming the guard fails IMMEDIATELY, no real subprocess spawn needed. Generalizes past timing bounds: any second test whose whole point is "prove test A's threshold has real headroom" is meaningless unless it reads test A's ACTUAL threshold, not a copy of the number.
- **A bashrc `export VAR=value` fix reaches only shells STARTED after the write — the SAME "frozen in shell memory" defect this file already documents for a bashrc FUNCTION (#77) applies identically to a bare env-var export, and it is easy to miss because writing the file feels like "the fix landed."** #259: a first fix (`apply_notify_owner`, mirroring `apply_ultracode_launcher`'s idempotent marker-block shape) correctly wrote `export AIRULESET_NOTIFY_OWNER=zbynek` to simap's `~/.bashrc` and a FRESH `bash -ic` probe confirmed it worked — but an adversarial review pointed out the box's own ALREADY-RUNNING Claude Code session had inherited its environment from a shell that predated the write, so that live session kept misrouting, and restarting another user's live session is never done. The fix that actually closes the gap for an already-running process: move the decision into the PYTHON code path the fix's own consumer re-executes fresh on every invocation (here, `notify.resolve_owner()` itself, checked right after the existing `AIRULESET_NOTIFY_OWNER` env-override check) rather than into a shell config file a live process has already parsed once and will never re-read. Rule of thumb: whenever a fix's entire job is "make an ALREADY-LIVE session behave differently starting now," a bashrc/shell-config write is close to the wrong tool by construction — prefer the runtime code path that live process actually calls on its next real action.
- **Splitting one already-committed diff (BOTH tickets' work landed in the same working tree, in the same files) into two separate commits, one per ticket: write a Python script that parses `git diff <file>` into individual hunks by `^@@` boundaries, categorize each hunk by which function's section comment it falls under, write TWO patch files, and `git apply --cached` each in turn.** #238/#160 (batch 8): both tickets' round-2 review fixes landed in `watchdog/__init__.py` in the SAME session, in non-overlapping functions (compact-delivery machinery vs goal-rearm/wedge-check machinery) — `git add -p`'s interactive per-hunk y/n prompting would have needed 19 correct answers typed blind via scripted stdin, fragile to get right. The scripted split (`git diff > full.patch`, split on `@@`, bucket each hunk's index into two lists by eyeballing its `@@ ... @@ def <name>` context line, write two new patch files sharing the same `diff --git`/`index`/`---`/`+++` header, `git apply --cached --check` each to confirm before applying) worked cleanly because git's hunk boundaries already respected the function boundaries — no hand-editing of hunk content was needed. Verify the split BEFORE committing: `git show :<file>` the STAGED blob, confirm it parses (`ast.parse`), confirm ticket-A's marker strings are present and ticket-B's are ABSENT via grep, then temporarily `cp` the staged blob over the working file and run the affected test suite in isolation (proving ticket A's fix is genuinely self-contained, not accidentally depending on ticket B's uncommitted change) before restoring the full working-tree version and committing. This is a THIRD splitting technique alongside the existing `git add -p` (adjacent independent hunks) and hardcode-the-other-ticket's-condition-to-off (same lines, genuinely inseparable) bullets above — reach for this one when the hunks are already cleanly separated by the file's own structure and there are too many of them to safely drive interactively.
- **A SECOND fresh-context adversarial review of an ALREADY-reviewed-and-fixed diff is not redundant — the first round's own FIX is new code with its own new bugs, and a review that only re-checks the ORIGINAL three defects will never look at it.** #238/#160 (batch 8): both tickets got a first review (found real 🔴/🟡 findings, all fixed) and, on a hunch given the size of the changes, a SECOND review of the round-1 diff — which found genuinely new, real problems that were INTRODUCED by round 1's own fix, not present in the original code: a brand-new shared retry helper with ZERO test coverage (a mutant reverting the whole mechanism to a bare call passed all 349 existing tests, because none of them exercised the NEW code path specifically), a +102s real-sleep regression in the test suite (every hook-level test now blocks for the new retry's full hold, since the fake session never resolves), a latency regression on a path that used to be instant (removing an exemption added a mandatory multi-second block that a reused-but-mistuned retry constant made 3s worse than necessary), and an env-var coupling gap where raising ONE new config knob past another silently reproduces the exact bug round 1 just fixed. None of these are things round 1's own reviewer could have caught by re-checking the three named defects — they only exist because round 1's fix exists. Whenever a review-and-fix cycle produces a genuinely large diff (new functions, new shared helpers, new constants), budget for a SECOND review pass of that diff specifically, framed as "review this fix" rather than "review the ticket" — the two questions surface different bugs.
- **`watchdog.pane_owner()` and `notify.resolve_owner()` are TWO INDEPENDENT owner-resolution implementations, and only `resolve_owner()` ever knew about `STREAM_NOTIFY_OWNER` (#259) — any watchdog job that @mentions "the owner of a pane" via a raw `pane_owner(pid, run)` call bypasses the stream-persona redirect entirely.** #212 (live incident: a montalu@subdev usage alert mentioned nobody and landed in the wrong default channel — live-verified via `notify.notification_channel(env,"montalu")`/`mention_prefix` resolving to the box's generic fallback with NO mention, while `notify.resolve_owner()` on the SAME box correctly resolved "zbynek" with both populated). Found and fixed at 6 call sites in `watchdog/__init__.py` (job 3's `account_owner`, jobs 5/6/10/21 via `owner_by_sid`, the hosted-panes legacy branch, and — after an adversarial review — job 14's compact-stash-skip ping and job 20's goal-stall/drift/rearm give-up pings), all fixed the SAME way: wrap the raw call as `stream_redirect(pane_owner(pid, run))`, a new `notify.stream_redirect(raw_owner)` applying the EXISTING `STREAM_NOTIFY_OWNER` map to an arbitrary raw string (not just `_current_user()`, which is all `resolve_owner()` itself covers). Locked with a STRUCTURAL source-scan test (`TestPaneOwnerAlwaysRedirected`) asserting every `pane_owner(` call site in the file (besides its own `def`) is immediately preceded by `stream_redirect(` — cheaper than a full integration test per job, and it catches ANY future un-redirected call site by construction. Any NEW watchdog job that needs to address a Discord notification to "whoever owns this pane" must go through `stream_redirect()` from day one, or it silently reproduces this exact class of bug on the next stream account.
- **Anthropic's `oauth/usage` endpoint's `resets_at` field carries SUB-SECOND JITTER centered ON the reset boundary itself, not offset from it — a dedup keyed on the raw string re-fires on every poll, and TRUNCATING to a coarser granularity still mis-buckets real data that straddles the boundary.** #212: two live `fetch_usage()` calls 3 seconds apart, for the SAME weekly window, returned two DIFFERENT `resets_at` strings — confirmed live on montalu@subdev, and directly explains an observed 11-duplicate-alert burst across ~2.5h (98%→99%, every ~15 min = `USAGE_INTERVAL`). The first fix (truncate to whole-minute) was itself wrong in the SAME way the #196/#198/#111 entries above document for a different mechanism: real samples land on BOTH sides of the boundary (some `HH:59:59.xx`, some `(HH+1):00:00.xx`), so truncating still splits them into different buckets. The fix that holds: ROUND (add 30s, then truncate to the minute) rather than truncate — moves the bucket edge to `:30`, comfortably clear of the observed ~1s jitter — plus normalize to UTC first. A SECOND, adjacent bug in the same function: the fallback for a missing/malformed `resets_at` must NEVER return bare `None` — `check_usage`'s own `state["usage"]["alerted_window"]` uses `None` to mean "not currently alerted" (both the fresh-state default and the below-threshold re-arm), so a genuinely missing `resets_at` on the very FIRST real alert would compare `None == None` and silently never fire; the fix is a stable `"raw:<value>"` sentinel (never bare `None`, never the wall-clock-varying raw value) so a missing/malformed input still fires once and then correctly dedupes on repeat polls with the identical bad input. General lesson for ANY future dedup keyed on an externally-supplied timestamp: assume the source jitters AROUND its true boundary rather than moving smoothly away from it, verify with two real back-to-back calls before trusting a granularity choice, and never let a "couldn't parse it" fallback collide with the mechanism's own "nothing happened yet" sentinel.
- **`tmux has-session -t NAME` (a bare, un-anchored target) does PREFIX matching, not exact matching — live-verified against tmux 3.7b: with only a session named `montalu2-review` alive, `has-session -t montalu2` returns rc=0 ("exists").** Any "does session X already exist?" check built on a bare `-t name` will silently report a not-yet-provisioned account as already-done the moment ANY `<name>*`-shaped session exists — and keep skipping it forever, since the check itself has no way to notice the mismatch. Fix: anchor with the `=` exact-match prefix (`-t "=%s" % name`), confirmed working on the fleet's real tmux build. Note `tmux new-session -A -s NAME` (create-or-attach) is ALREADY exact-match — verified live, it creates a fresh distinct session rather than attaching to a same-prefix one — so this gotcha is specific to `has-session`/similar READ-side lookups, not to `new-session`. Found by an adversarial review of #263's `_tmux_session_exists`.
- **`curl ... | bash` with no `pipefail` reports `rc=0` even when curl itself fails (bad network, DNS, download host down)** — bash's own exit code is the LAST command in the pipe, so a `curl <dead-url> | bash` genuinely exits 0 while curl's own error only reaches stderr. Confirmed live: `bash -c 'curl -fsSL <invalid> | bash'` → rc 0; `bash -c 'set -o pipefail; curl -fsSL <invalid> | bash'` → rc 6 (curl's real exit code). A DOWNSTREAM "did the thing I installed actually land" check (e.g. re-testing `shutil.which`) still catches the failure correctly either way — but the PRINTED "rc=0" in the failure branch is actively misleading to whoever reads the log. Always prepend `set -o pipefail;` to any `curl | bash` (or similar pipe-to-interpreter) subprocess command whose own exit code is inspected.
- **A "goals-on-screen" (or any viewport-fragment) HARD gate checked BEFORE a transcript-resolution attempt turns a held draft into a PERMANENT dead end — compute the fragment LAZILY instead, and never `continue` silently once a fallback exists.** #266: `goal_autoarm`'s plain/draft split already resolved the payload from the session transcript (`find_active_transcript` → `_transcript_goal_line`), independent of what the pane's CURRENT viewport shows — but a `goals = re.findall(...); if not goals: continue` ran BEFORE that resolution was even attempted, so a draft in the box (including the codebase's OWN earlier stuck paste, a self-inflicted instance) visually pushing the printed `/goal` line out of the viewport blocked arming forever: the per-pane dedup marker only gets set once arming proceeds, so the identical doomed viewport check re-ran every 60s sweep with ZERO log output (a bare `continue`). Fix shape, reusable for any future "on-screen confirmation as a hard precondition" bug: compute the fragment/viewport signal lazily (`frag = X.group(...) if X else None`), let the STRONGER signal (the transcript) resolve first and unconditionally, and only refuse — WITH A LOGGED REASON — at the single point where every source (transcript, foreign transcript, viewport fallback) has been tried and genuinely come up empty. A silent `continue` guarding a resolvable-elsewhere condition is the tell.
- **Testing a NEWLY-verified plain/bare-box keystroke branch (one that used to be blind fire-and-forget) against this file's existing static (non-`model_stash`) `FakeTmux` fixtures makes EVERY pre-existing test in that class real-sleep through the settle-poll, one 60-800s meltdown per file — patch `time.sleep` at MODULE scope (`setUpModule`/`tearDownModule`), not per call site.** #266: converting `goal_autoarm`'s plain branch from `send_continue` (blind) to `_send_goal_verified` (types, then bounded-polls up to `GOAL_TYPE_SETTLE_POLLS × GOAL_TYPE_SETTLE_S` seconds waiting for the render before pressing Enter) turned `tests/test_goal_autoarm.py` from a 0.3s file into a 126s one — none of the ~15 pre-existing plain-branch tests use `model_stash=True`, so their STATIC capture never reflects the typed keystroke and the primitive genuinely exhausts its whole settle window on every single one (correctly refusing to verify — none of those tests asserted OK/FAIL, only that a `send-keys -l` fired at all, which still happens unconditionally before the poll). Threading `sleep_fn=lambda s: None` into all ~19 call sites (some through a shared `go()` helper, several direct) would have been fragile and easy to miss on the next new test; `m.patch("time.sleep", lambda s: None)` in `setUpModule` (restored in `tearDownModule`) fixes the whole file's timing in 8 lines with ZERO call-site changes, because `watchdog/__init__.py` resolves `time.sleep` off the shared `time` MODULE object at call time, not a name bound at import. Verified safe by construction: the poll loops are iteration-bounded (never wall-clock-asserting), and the primitive's FAIL outcome is genuinely reached (not hidden) whenever a test doesn't separately mock the primitive to force OK.
- **A `FakeTmux.capture_seq` (a scripted LIST of successive `capture-pane` replies, popped in order, falling back to the static `captured` once exhausted — the exact shape `tests/test_goal_rearm.py`'s own `FakeTmux` already has) is the right tool for proving a STALE-CAPTURE race a fresh-context adversarial review flags, and is worth adding to a sibling test file's OWN simpler `FakeTmux` rather than importing across test files (this repo has no `tests/__init__.py` and no established cross-file test import).** #266's review: `goal_autoarm`'s plain branch reused the capture taken at the very TOP of the pane's loop iteration for its OWN send, but several steps (transcript reads, a POSSIBLE `_foreign_transcript_goal` sudo subprocess with a 15s timeout) run in between — job 20's own sibling already re-captures FRESH immediately before sending for exactly this reason ("by now the sweep's own capture is several tmux round-trips old"). `capture_seq=[<top-of-loop pane>, <pane that stopped being bare>]` on `test_goal_autoarm.py`'s own `FakeTmux` (previously static-only) proved the fix's fresh-recapture-and-refuse behavior with a 4-line test-fixture addition (a list, popped by the SAME `if "capture-pane" in j:` branch, checked before the static/model_stash fallback) — no need to reach for the OTHER file's more elaborate `FakeTmux`.
- **A pre-send (ZERO-KEYSTROKE) refusal must never consume the same per-pane dedup window a REAL send attempt does — this is the #101 "transient refusal" lesson, and it recurs anywhere a verified-delivery primitive's precondition check can fail BEFORE typing anything.** #266's plain branch initially set the dedup marker (`ga[pid] = int(now)`) unconditionally before calling `_send_goal_verified`, so a pane that raced into a non-bare state between the top-of-loop capture and the send would burn the full `GOAL_ARM_WINDOW_S` (10 min) for a refusal that typed NOTHING — exactly the class the stash branch's `_GOAL_REARM_TRANSIENT_STASH_REASONS` carve-out already exists to avoid, just not yet applied to the plain branch. Fix: check the SAME precondition (`_input_line_text(fresh) != ""`) yourself, against a capture you already know is fresh, BEFORE setting the dedup marker or calling the primitive — a `SKIP-TRANSIENT` log, `continue`, no marker set, retried next sweep. Whenever a verified primitive is called from a NEW site, ask whether that site's OWN precondition failure is being folded into the "real attempt, consumes the window" bucket by accident.
- **A skip/refusal log line added to close a "silent forever `continue`" bug (see the goals-on-screen bullet above) needs its OWN once-per-streak dedup from day one, mirrored off whichever SIBLING log line in the same function already solved this** — the newly-visible log is otherwise the NEXT spam-every-sweep bug, discovered by the SAME adversarial review pass that asked for the log in the first place. #266: `goal-autoarm`'s pre-existing `skip cleared` line already logs once per session (`state["goalarm_cleared"]`, cleared once the pane un-clears) — the NEW `skip no-goal-on-screen` line needed the identical shape, just keyed on `pid` instead of `sid` (no transcript resolved in that branch, so there is no `sid` to key on) and cleared the moment ANY source resolves a goal again. The functional retry (arming keeps re-attempting every sweep — the actual point of the fix) is completely independent of the log dedup and must not be coupled to it.
- **A "provably one-directional, can only get SAFER" invariant claimed for a content-derived signal replacing a raw-mtime one should be HARDENED with a clamp to the original signal, even when a real-data scan finds zero counterexamples — the clamp costs nothing and converts "never observed" into "cannot happen."** #177: `_last_real_turn_ts`'s "job 10 can only fire MORE readily than before" claim rests on raw file mtime always being >= the true last-real-turn timestamp — true for every entry with a trustworthy `timestamp` field, but a single clock-skewed/corrupt FUTURE timestamp (a scan of 25 real transcripts found the worst observed skew was 0.0s — CC stamps entries off the same clock that sets mtime) would make the content-derived value read NEWER than mtime, which would make job 10 wait LONGER than the pre-fix behavior ever did — the one direction the fix must never move. `wedge_tmtime = min(_last_real_turn_ts(tpath), tmtime)` closes the gap by construction (a `min()` can only ever REMOVE a violation, never introduce one) — proven via a RED test seeding one future-timestamped entry over a genuinely-stale file mtime and asserting job 10 still pings.
- **`tmux send-keys -t <pane>` does NOT exercise a KEY-TABLE BINDING (`bind-key`) — it writes bytes straight into the pane's pty, bypassing the server's key dispatch entirely.** #267, adding the Shift+PageUp/PageDown scrollback binding: `tmux bind-key -n S-PageUp copy-mode -eu` registered cleanly (confirmed via `list-keys -T root`, which normalizes `PageUp`→canonical `PPage`, `PageDown`→`NPage` — write the conf using either spelling, tmux accepts both), but `tmux send-keys -t <pane> S-PageUp` / `-N 1 S-PageUp` / the canonical `-N 1 S-PPage` all left `#{pane_in_mode}` at 0 and one attempt even leaked a raw `2~` escape-sequence tail into the pane as literal text — the binding never fired. Directly invoking `tmux copy-mode -t <pane> -u` (bypassing key dispatch on purpose) DID enter copy-mode, proving the *action* was fine and the *dispatch path* was the gap. Only a genuinely ATTACHED CLIENT's input passes through the key-table lookup — reproduce with `pty.fork()` (same recipe this file's #236 window-size bullet already uses for a live client, one level deeper): `os.execvp("tmux", ["tmux", "-L", sock, "attach", "-t", session])` in the child, then `os.write(fd, b"\x1b[5;2~")` from the parent (the real xterm CSI sequence for Shift+PageUp; Shift+PageDown is `\x1b[6;2~`) — THAT correctly drove `#{pane_in_mode}` to 1 then back to 0 (the `-e` auto-exit firing once scrolled back to the bottom). Reuse this pty-attach-and-feed-the-real-escape-sequence recipe for verifying ANY future root-table/copy-mode `bind-key` change; `send-keys` alone can only ever prove the bound COMMAND works in isolation, never that the KEY actually reaches it.
- **A `bind-key` call is safe to LIVE-APPLY against a running tmux server — a materially different safety class than `window-size`/`default-size` (#236/#241's own hard-learned conf-only exception).** It is a pure key-table registration: no window geometry read or written, no `recalculate_sizes()` pass, nothing already rendered by the CC TUI touched at all — confirmed by binding it live against dev1's own real default-socket server (a side effect of simply running this repo's own `TestTmuxHistoryLimit` smoke test, which shells to the real `tmux` binary with no injected `run`) and finding zero disruption, `list-keys -T root` afterward showing the new `S-PPage` entry cleanly alongside the pre-existing session state. Any FUTURE tmux option being considered for live-apply should be classified this way first: does it re-derive a value the renderer already drew (unsafe, conf-only), or does it only add/replace a dispatch-table entry (safe to live-apply)?
- **Building a FAKE `pane_current_command`-named receiving-process fixture (e.g. a fake `claude` process to prove a conditional `bind-key ... if -F ...` dispatch) needs `stty raw -echo` before the process starts reading stdin, or every byte sent to it — including a lone control keystroke like Ctrl+O with no trailing newline — silently sits forever in the kernel's CANONICAL-mode line-discipline buffer and never reaches the reading process at all.** #338: a fixture built as `bash -c 'exec -a claude cat > <file>'` (argv0-renamed via `exec -a`, confirmed live to be enough for `pane_current_command` to report the fake name — tmux reads argv[0], NOT `/proc/pid/comm`) received ZERO bytes for `send-keys C-o`, a plain literal string, or even a direct write to the pane's own `/dev/pts/N` device bypassing tmux entirely — because a fresh tty defaults to canonical (cooked) mode, which buffers input until a newline/EOF terminates the "line", and a lone unterminated control byte just sits there. Prepending `stty raw -echo;` to the fixture's own startup command (`bash -c 'stty raw -echo; exec -a claude cat > <file>'`) fixed it immediately — byte-for-byte delivery, no newline needed. This never surfaced in the #267/#236/#241/#254/#289/#294 live proofs because none of them needed a FAKE claude-NAMED receiving-process fixture (#267's own proof only ever watched tmux's own `#{pane_in_mode}` STATE flag, never a byte actually delivered into a program's stdin) — #338 was the first ticket to build one. Reuse `stty raw -echo` in the startup command of any future fake receiving-process fixture built for a live pty-attach proof.

- **A staleness/drift detector that only tracks an INDEX or a BOOLEAN ("was this ever observed matching X") cannot tell "the TARGET moved" from "the TRACKED THING ITSELF moved" — both look identical to a check that only remembers WHICH thing last matched, never WHAT the matching text actually was.** #186: job 20's `_goal_template_drift` tracked `tvar` (which template variant a session was once observed matching) but not the payload's own value at that moment — so once `tvar` was set, EVERY later mismatch (whether from the shipped template being edited, the intended "drift" case, or the user hand-editing their own armed text, a deliberate override that must never be fought) looked the same and was unconditionally reverted. The fix: additionally record a HASH of the tracked thing's OWN content at the moment of confirmed match (`armed_hash`, set only in the "up to date" branch, always unconditionally on every re-observation — never only when something else visibly changed, see the mutation-testing bullet below). On a later mismatch, compare the CURRENT tracked-thing hash against that recorded value: unchanged since confirmation → the TARGET moved (safe to re-sync); changed (or never recorded — see the migration bullet below) → the TRACKED THING moved, treat as unrecognised and never guess. This is the general shape of the drift-vs-edit discriminator this repo already established for template IDENTITY itself (#64: exact hash of a normalized form, never resemblance) — reapply it ONE LEVEL UP whenever a future job needs to distinguish "did the reference change" from "did the observed instance change" and both currently collapse into the same signal.
- **A NEW persisted-state field that a fix's discriminator depends on needs an explicit MIGRATION policy for records written by the PRE-FIX code — "missing = treat as unconfirmed, never guess" is usually right, but check whether that silently deletes an EXISTING signal the old code used to provide.** #186's `armed_hash` is absent on every session whose `tvar` was set before this fix deployed (this repo's api-watchdog runs the WORKING TREE live, so a deploy takes effect on the very next 60s tick against REAL persisted state). The safe default (treat "armed_hash absent" as unrecognised, same as a genuine hand-edit) is correct and was explicitly verified NOT to be fixable by seeding a computed value for legacy records (seeding `goal_template_hash(line)` at deploy time would re-trust exactly the ticket's own victim session, since a hand-edited session's CURRENT payload is indistinguishable from a template-matching one by that measure alone). But the SAFE default has a cost: it removes whatever visibility the OLD code's failure path used to provide — here, a genuinely-stuck legacy session that would have eventually reached the pre-existing `GOAL_DRIFT_MAX_ATTEMPTS` "GAVE UP" Discord ping now short-circuits BEFORE ever attempting a delivery, so that signal is lost entirely unless the new "unrecognised" branch is given its OWN, narrower ping for the provably-legacy sub-case (distinct from a genuinely OBSERVED hand-edit, which correctly stays silent — the user's own deliberate edit is never worth pestering them about). When adding a migration-safe discriminator field, always ask: what did the OLD code's failure path used to signal, and does the new code's "safe, do nothing" default silently swallow that signal too?
- **Mutation-test EACH new regression test individually against the SPECIFIC mutant it was written to catch — a green full suite after adding N tests does not prove any ONE of them has teeth.** #186's adversarial review found two load-bearing lines in the fix with ZERO test coverage even though the whole new test class passed: (a) `armed_hash` must be re-set UNCONDITIONALLY on every "up to date" observation, not only inside the `if tvar != cur or dhash or dq:` branch that fires when something ELSE also changed — a mutant moving the assignment inside that guard passed all 19 existing/new tests, because none of them re-observed a session that was ALREADY up to date with nothing else different; (b) the `tvar = None` downgrade line itself — a mutant that dropped JUST that one line (keeping the `dhash`/`dn`/`dpinged`/`dq` clear) also passed everything, because its only observable effect needs a THIRD sweep (hand-edit, then the STALE pre-edit confirmed text typed back verbatim, with no fresh exact-template match in between) that no existing test constructed. The verification technique that caught both: `cp` the whole file to a scratch path, apply ONE targeted `str.replace` mutation with an `assert mutated != src` guard (so a no-op replace is never silently "passing"), run ONLY the one test meant to catch it, confirm a genuine `AssertionError` (not an unrelated crash), then restore the file with a plain `cp` and diff it back to identical before moving to the next mutant. Do this for every review-flagged "add a test for X" request BEFORE reporting the fix complete — a request to "add a test" that is satisfied by a test proven, by hand, to fail under the SPECIFIC reverting mutation is categorically stronger evidence than "the suite is green," and this repo has repeatedly shipped tests that were green for the wrong reason (see the many earlier entries in this file on that exact failure shape).
- **In a watchdog job, a ONE-WAY-DOOR state mutation (an action that PERMANENTLY forecloses a future capability, or consumes a one-shot signal flag) must be gated on `not dry_run`; a PASSIVE, IDEMPOTENT refresh (re-recording a value that merely reflects observed reality, safely re-derivable on the next real sweep regardless) does not need the gate.** #186: the pre-existing `dark_pinged` one-shot flag (`#238-review 🟡F4`) already established this distinction for job 20's dark-episode branch; the SAME bug recurred in the brand-new hand-edit branch — a `--dry-run` diagnostic sweep (this repo's own normal manual troubleshooting command) was downgrading `tvar` to `None` and setting `untracked_logged = True` UNCONDITIONALLY, so running the diagnostic against a REAL, healthily-drifting session would permanently disable its future auto-heal and consume its one-shot log line, with `save_state()` persisting the damage regardless of `dry_run` (confirmed: `run_once`'s own final `save_state(state_path, state)` call has NO `dry_run` check at all — this is a REPO-WIDE convention, not a bug specific to this branch: most bookkeeping mutations throughout `goal_rearm`/`_goal_template_drift` — streak resets, attempt counters, `dhash` refreshes — already happen unconditionally BEFORE the function's own `if dry_run:` check near the actual keystroke-send, and that is FINE for anything self-healing/re-derivable on the next real sweep). The checklist for any future watchdog job addition: does this write make a LATER real sweep behave differently than if the write had never happened (one-way door → gate on `not dry_run`, mirror the `dark_pinged` shape: check-then-mutate-then-log all inside the gate), or does it just re-confirm what's already true and get silently overwritten again on the next real observation regardless (passive refresh → leave it unconditional, exactly like the existing `armed_hash`/`dhash`/streak-reset writes already do)?
- **A content-validation classifier meant to catch "carries no real meaning" should test for the PRESENCE of a property (does it contain a letter, anywhere) rather than enumerate every ABSENCE shape one at a time — the enumeration approach is defeated by the first spelling variant nobody thought of.** #272's first cut refused `--goal`/`--achieved` only when the value matched a bare-numeric regex or an EXACT (casefold + whitespace-collapse only) denylist entry — a fresh-context adversarial review live-defeated it with a trailing period, a dropped diacritic, an extra space before a comma, "##457"/"#457.", and an unlisted `ok`/`áno`/`yes` family, all composing and "sending" successfully. The fix that actually holds: `not re.search(r"[^\W\d_]", s)` ("no Unicode letter anywhere") catches EVERY digits/punctuation/symbol/emoji-only shape regardless of exact spelling, in one line, with no enumeration at all — the denylist is then only needed for the much smaller set of REAL WORDS that are still meaningless ("n/a", "TODO", "hotovo"), and even there, normalize BOTH the stored phrase and the supplied value through the identical function (NFKD diacritic-fold + casefold + whole-string punctuation-run-to-space + whitespace-collapse) so a phrase and its accidental ASCII-typo sibling ("hotové"/"hotove") match without hand-duplicating every entry twice. Reuse this "presence-of-a-broad-property first, denylist only for the residual" shape for any future "is this string meaningful content" classifier in this repo, rather than reaching for pattern enumeration.
- **A Claude Code plugin marketplace REGISTERED via `extraKnownMarketplaces` in `settings.json` is NOT the same as a marketplace `claude plugin install` can actually resolve — writing that JSON key alone is insufficient, and this repo shipped exactly that assumption for months.** #273: reproduced empirically in an isolated scratch `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (real `claude` binary, CC 2.1.223, no real account touched): a fresh profile with `extraKnownMarketplaces.claude-plugins-official` written directly into `settings.json` (the exact shape `reconcile_managed_plugins`/`reconcile_caveman_settings` already produce) still fails `claude plugin install X@claude-plugins-official` with `Plugin "X" not found in marketplace "claude-plugins-official". Your local copy may be out of date — try 'claude plugin marketplace update ...'`.
