Only running `claude plugin marketplace add <owner>/<repo>` (or a full git URL) actually clones the marketplace repo onto disk under `~/.claude/plugins/marketplaces/<name>/` — only THEN does `install` succeed. `marketplace add` is itself idempotent (confirmed live: re-running it on an already-materialized marketplace returns rc=0, `Marketplace '<name>' already on disk — declared in user settings`), and it ALSO writes the identical `extraKnownMarketplaces` settings.json entry itself, so the repo's own settings-write step is redundant-but-harmless belt-and-suspenders, never the actual fix. A long-lived, previously-INTERACTIVE account (montalu, dev1/dev2) self-heals this silently via CC's own internal `officialMarketplaceAutoInstallAttempted`/`officialMarketplaceAutoInstalled` routine (visible in `~/.claude.json`) or a manual `marketplace add` run years ago — neither mechanism ever fires on an account whose ENTIRE lifecycle is a headless ssh `install`/`push` flow (montalu2/montalu3/montalu4, #263), where `~/.claude/plugins/` doesn't exist at all. Any future "plugin@marketplace" addition to `MANAGED_PLUGINS` (or a brand-new plugin family outside it) needs its marketplace's SOURCE added to `MARKETPLACE_SOURCES` and `ensure_marketplace_registered()` called before the first `claude plugin install` attempt — never assume a marketplace is "built-in" just because its plugins ship from an official-sounding name.
- **A commit's OWN author timestamp (`git log -1 --format=%ci <sha>`) can be well BEFORE it actually landed on the box's real `main` — this repo dispatches worker sessions into isolated worktrees, and the supervisor MERGES their branch into `main` later, so the merge commit's timestamp (not the individual commits' own) is when the live systemd watchdog first executed that code.** #271: forensically pinning "which code was live at the incident's 11:44 timestamp" needed BOTH numbers — `f1903a6` (job 9's plain-branch fix, #266) was authored at 11:28:33, only 16 minutes before the incident, but its MERGE commit (`c2d3658`) landed at 13:01:24, ~1h17m AFTER. So the box was still running the OLD pre-#266 code (blind `send_continue`, no bareness check at all) the whole time, which the individual commit's own author-date would have wrongly suggested was already fixed. Always check the MERGE commit's date for "was this live yet", never a child commit's own author date, when a repo uses this worktree-dispatch-then-merge workflow.
- **`journalctl`'s ABSENCE of an expected log line, cross-checked against the SAME exact line format firing successfully on an adjacent day for the SAME pane, is itself a real, reportable finding — not just "couldn't find evidence".** #271: zero `goal-autoarm` lines appeared for the airuleset pane across the whole 90-minute incident window, while the IDENTICAL old-format line (`"goal-autoarm %s (%s)"`) fired for that same pane the day before and for a different pane the same morning — proving the logging mechanism works and its total silence during the incident is meaningful (consistent with a hard pre-log gate silently `continue`-ing every sweep, the exact "defect 2" shape a same-day sibling fix was independently filed for). Report the positive control alongside the negative finding, or "nothing in the journal" reads as inconclusive when it is actually decisive.
- **A shared delivery primitive's own "persist/rescue" call is a PROVABLE NO-OP in production if it reads the SAME capture object a caller's precondition check already validated — adding the call is not enough; it has to observe a signal the caller's own check could not have seen.** #271 adversarial-review MAJOR finding: `_send_goal_verified`'s new rescue-persist call read `cap`, and every real caller (job 9, job 20 ×2) already gates entry into the function on that EXACT object reading bare — so the persist call could only ever see bare content, making it dead code with a docstring claiming a race protection it structurally could not provide. The fix, and the general lesson: re-derive the signal FRESH, as close as possible to the actual keystroke, via a SECOND capture-pane call immediately before typing (reusing this repo's own established `_goal_template_drift`/job-20 "re-capture right before send" pattern, #176-F3) — only then does persisting it protect against anything a caller's own earlier check could not.
- **Inserting one extra `capture_pane`/keystroke-adjacent call into a shared primitive shifts EVERY test's scripted `cap_seq`/similar fixed-reply-queue fixture that reaches it by exactly one position — find and fix the SHARED sequence-BUILDER function(s) first, not each failing test individually.** #271: adding a second `capture_pane` call inside `_send_goal_verified` broke 19 of `test_goal_rearm.py`'s tests at once, because nearly all of them built their scripted tmux reply queue via two shared helpers (`_typed_seq`/`_lit_seq`); inserting one extra "still bare" entry into EACH SHARED BUILDER (not per-test) fixed 17 of the 19 immediately, leaving only 2 tests with their OWN inline literal `cap_seq=[...]` lists to patch by hand. Always grep for shared cap_seq/reply-queue BUILDER functions in the affected test file before touching individual tests — the ratio (2 vs 19) makes clear which approach is worth trying first. A STATEFUL fake tmux (one that actually models the box, e.g. `model_stash=True`/`StashTmux`) needs no such update at all — it naturally reflects whatever is really "there" for an extra call, which is a second, independent reason to prefer a stateful fake over a scripted reply list whenever a primitive's own internal call count might change later.
- **`conftest.py` is invisible to `python -m unittest discover` — only `pytest` reads it — so it is a genuine, ADDITIONAL layer of test isolation for `pytest`-based dev runs, never a substitute for isolating the box's REAL push-gate subprocess (`cmd_push`'s own `subprocess.run([sys.executable, "-m", "unittest", "discover", ...])`).** #271: the repo's actual fail-closed push gate is `unittest discover`, already isolated there via an env-var (`AIRULESET_DRAFT_RESCUE_DIR`) `cmd_push` sets for that ONE subprocess call — a NEW `tests/conftest.py` autouse fixture was added on TOP of that, purely to close the separate, real gap of a developer running `python -m pytest tests/test_X.py` standalone (a normal, sanctioned workflow per this file's own dev-rules) and bypassing the env-var injection entirely (measured: 9 files / 43 real writes into the developer's ACTUAL `~/.claude/draft-rescue/` this way). Adding a `tests/__init__.py` was explicitly rejected as a way to solve the SAME problem for `unittest discover` itself — too large/risky a structural change (dotted module imports, discovery semantics) for a test-hygiene concern whose worst real consequence (a handful of stray, self-pruning, never-read text files) is genuinely low-stakes; a plain env-var set once in `cmd_push` was judged sufficient for the actual gate, and `conftest.py` only for the pytest-direct workflow.
- **A `conftest.py` (or any) AUTOUSE fixture that globally patches a function will also intercept a test whose whole point is exercising the REAL, unpatched function — capture a direct reference to the real function at MODULE IMPORT time (before any fixture ever runs) and call THAT, never the module-attribute name, inside such a test.** #271: adding `tests/conftest.py`'s autouse `draft_rescue_dir` patch immediately broke `TestDraftRescueDir`'s own two tests (which specifically assert the real function's env-var/default resolution), since `wd.draft_rescue_dir()` inside the test now always resolved to whatever tmp dir the autouse fixture had installed. Fix: `_REAL_DRAFT_RESCUE_DIR = wd.draft_rescue_dir` at the top of the test file (captured once, at import time, genuinely unaffected by any later `mock.patch.object` on the module attribute) — the two tests call `_REAL_DRAFT_RESCUE_DIR()` directly instead of `wd.draft_rescue_dir()`.
- **A prune/cleanup sweep over "everything in this directory" must filter by the EXACT filename shape the module itself writes before deleting anything — never a bare `os.listdir()` + age check with no name filter, however narrowly-scoped the directory seems.** #271 adversarial-review MAJOR finding: `_draft_rescue_prune`'s first cut unlinked every name in `draft_rescue_dir()` past the TTL, with zero check that the name actually matched the `<pane>-<epoch-ms>.txt` shape `_draft_rescue_persist` writes — so a misconfigured `AIRULESET_DRAFT_RESCUE_DIR` pointed at an already-populated directory (a typo, an inherited env var, a future caller reusing the variable) would silently delete unrelated content on the very next write. Fix: a compiled regex matching the module's OWN naming convention (`_DRAFT_RESCUE_NAME_RX`), checked before any `stat`/`unlink` call — cheap, and it makes the sweep provably scoped to files this code itself created, regardless of what else might be sharing the directory.
- **A "genuinely installed" check for anything with an external CLI-managed registry must read that CLI's OWN registry, never a filesystem-presence proxy for it — a proxy can be satisfied by a stale/partial artifact left behind by a PAST FAILED attempt, and once satisfied it never self-corrects.** #276: `_managed_plugin_built(key)` globbed for a cache file under `plugins/cache/<marketplace>/<plugin>/*/...` and treated its presence as "genuinely installed" — but a plugin install that failed BEFORE its marketplace was registered (an earlier, since-fixed bug, #273) can still leave a stale/partial cache dir behind, which satisfies the glob forever even though `claude plugin list`/`claude plugin install` genuinely know nothing about the plugin. Live consequence: three fleet accounts stuck with a plugin permanently silently skipped, `settings.json` wrongly saying "enabled" the whole time, with zero log output (`if _managed_plugin_built(key): continue`). Fix: `~/.claude/plugins/installed_plugins.json` is claude's own plugin registry — confirmed live (dev1) to be the EXACT backing store `claude plugin list` renders its output from, its `plugins` dict keys matching `claude plugin list`'s printed names 1:1 — so `_managed_plugin_built` now reads that file directly (`key in _plugin_registry_keys()`) instead of globbing. This is strictly cheaper AND more robust than shelling out to `claude plugin list` and parsing its human-readable text output (no subprocess, no PATH/env-repair machinery, no dependency on a stable CLI text format — this repo already has a `_claude_cli_env()` PATH-repair workaround for OTHER subprocess calls in this exact file, purely because a non-login ssh shell's PATH lacks `~/.local/bin`). General lesson: when checking "is X genuinely provisioned/installed/registered" for anything owned by an external tool that maintains its own bookkeeping file, find and read THAT file — a file-glob/directory-presence heuristic is a proxy that degrades silently the moment reality (a partial/failed prior attempt) diverges from the assumption baked into the heuristic, and the resulting bug is invisible precisely because it looks like a correctly-working fast path.
- **An ALLOW-list negative lookahead added to narrow an over-broad regex (`\.secret(?!\.CONFIGEXT(?![boundary]))`) must exclude `.` from its OWN inner terminal-boundary class, or a listed extension used as a MIDDLE component (not the final one) silently un-blocks whatever follows it.** #165: the first draft's inner boundary was `(?![A-Za-z0-9_-])` (identical to the pattern's PRE-EXISTING outer boundary, copied by habit) — so `DB_PASS.secret.json.gz` read as "`.json` is followed by a non-identifier char (`.`), therefore terminal" and the whole match failed (i.e. the file was newly ALLOWED, a real regression: an archive of a genuine value file wearing a config-shaped disguise). A fresh-context adversarial review caught it live (`cat DB_PASS.secret.json.gz` → rc 0) against the implementer's own corpus replay and unit-test suite, both of which stayed green throughout. Fix: add `.` to the INNER lookahead's excluded class only (`(?![A-Za-z0-9_.-])`) — the OUTER boundary stays unchanged (it deliberately still allows `.` to follow the bare `.secret`, which is what makes a bare copy/archive shape match at all) — so the listed extension must be the name's true FINAL component. The honest, accepted cost: a legitimate config's own future backup (`config.secret.json.bak`) now also blocks, and that trade needs to be stated explicitly in the header rather than left implicit. General lesson: when adding an ALLOW-list exemption to an existing deny-by-default boundary, the exemption's OWN "is this really the end of the name" check needs to be STRICTER than the pattern's ambient boundary, not copy it — the ambient boundary was tuned to admit a following `.` (that's the whole point of the deny-by-default default), which is exactly the property an allow-list carve-out must NOT inherit.
- **Reproducing/validating a filename-matching hook whose false-positive EXAMPLE FILENAMES are themselves the hook's own trigger pattern: build the probe with the Write tool (never a Bash heredoc/echo), and invoke it via a bare `python3 <path>` Bash command with NO literal example names on the command line.** #165 hit this twice — once fixing the guard, again writing the docs/autopilot-log.md entry describing the fix, since a `cat >> docs/autopilot-log.md <<EOF` heredoc whose BODY quotes `config.secret.json` as prose would be blocked by the very hook being fixed (the ACTIVE guard enforcing the current session's own tool calls is the box's INSTALLED copy at `~/devel/airuleset/hooks/...`, not the worktree's edited-but-not-yet-deployed copy — so a fix landing in the working tree does NOT exempt the session's OWN subsequent commands from the still-active pre-fix hook). The working pattern throughout: Write a `.py`/`.md` file to the scratchpad carrying the example names as file CONTENT, then reference it only by PATH in the Bash command (`python3 /tmp/.../probe.py`, `cat /tmp/.../entry.txt >> docs/autopilot-log.md`) — the Bash command line itself never contains the trigger substring, so the meta-hook has nothing to match against.
- **A skill's env-key load line naming a path under the vault-guarded directory is unusable on every managed box regardless of whether a key sits there — `block-vault-store-read.sh` refuses the command by TEXT, not by whether the file exists.** #275: `skills/meeting-analysis/SKILL.md`'s Soniox key load line named `$HOME/.claude/secrets/soniox.env` as its own primary source, which collides with the exact guard `#153`/`#156`/`#157` built. Proven live by extracting the real load line and feeding it as a genuine PreToolUse payload to the real hook (`bash hooks/block-vault-store-read.sh < payload.json` — rc=2 before the fix, rc=0 after). ANY future skill that must read a per-user local secret needs a canonical path OUTSIDE `~/.claude/secrets/` from day one (`~/.soniox.env`-style, sibling to `~/.claude`, never inside it) — reusing this hook's own test harness (`tests/test_vault_read_guard.py`'s `run(cmd)` shape) to check a candidate load line against the real hook is the cheap way to catch this before it ships, not after.
- **A local secret-provisioning function delivering ONE credential to remote accounts must extract JUST the one matching line out of a multi-secret source file — never pipe/read the whole file.** #275: `~/devel/voiceagent/.env` (the dev1 source for the Soniox key) carries ~40 OTHER unrelated lines (Discord tokens, etc.) — `_soniox_key_line()` scans line-by-line for `startswith("SONIOX_API_KEY=")` and returns only that one line; the value then travels to the remote ssh subprocess via `input=`, never argv, never printed. Reuse this exact shape (line-filter the source, `input=` the ssh call, never touch argv/stdout) for the next credential this repo needs to fleet-deliver.
- **A "download + extract + place" best-effort provisioning step (`ensure_claude_cli_installed`-shaped, ONE subprocess call) must never `cp` straight into the FINAL destination path — extract into a scratch dir under the SAME parent, then `mv` atomically only once every expected artifact is confirmed present.** #275 adversarial-review MAJOR-3: `cp` creates the target file with its final executable mode BEFORE its content is fully written, so a subprocess `timeout=` kill (Python's `subprocess.run` sends SIGKILL on `TimeoutExpired`, which no shell `trap` can intercept) landing mid-copy leaves a truncated-but-"executable" binary that the function's own `_available()`-style check then reports as permanently installed. Using `mktemp -d -p <dest.parent>` (not a separate `/tmp`) keeps the final `mv` a same-filesystem atomic rename rather than a cross-device copy. Proven end-to-end with a REAL (no-network) extraction test: build a small `tar.xz` fixture + a fake `curl` stub earlier on PATH that just `cat`s it, run the REAL constructed bash script against it, assert both artifacts land correctly and no scratch dir survives. The one residual this can't close: a genuine SIGKILL can still leave the (now harmless, since it's not at the final path) scratch dir behind — untrappable by construction, same limit every shell `trap` has against SIGKILL, documented rather than "fixed".
- **Filtering a `REMOTE_HOSTS`-shaped host list to the RELEVANT subset (here, `AUTHORITY_BY_USER`'s keys — the subdev stream accounts) BEFORE reading any local source file is what keeps a NEW cross-account provisioning function safe to wire into `cmd_push()` without touching pre-existing `cmd_push` tests.** #275: `provision_subdev_soniox_key()` filters `hosts` to matching users FIRST and returns `[]` immediately when nothing matches, so `tests/test_airuleset.py`'s existing `cmd_push` tests (which patch `REMOTE_HOSTS` to non-stream-account fixtures like `dev2`/`gk`) never reach the source-file read at all — no real filesystem access, no accidental dependency on this box's own `~/devel/voiceagent/.env`. Whenever wiring a new REMOTE_HOSTS-iterating step into an already-tested orchestrator function, filter-then-short-circuit BEFORE any side-effecting read, so the orchestrator's OWN pre-existing tests (which rarely construct a realistic full host list) stay untouched by construction.
- **A "fresh, independent re-derivation" built on `scan_goal_markers` (or any similar tail-bootstrapping reader) is NOT actually independent-and-safer than the caller's own accumulated state unless it reads the WHOLE file — a bare `scan_goal_markers(tpath)` (no `off=`) bootstraps from `GOAL_MARK_TAIL_BYTES` (4 MB), and that truncation regresses on exactly the population a "long outage" fix is meant to help.** #173: job 20's dark-cap decision added `_goal_dark_died_by_outage(tpath)` to distinguish an outage from a deliberate `/goal clear`, deliberately re-reading the transcript FRESH rather than trusting the caller's own incrementally-tracked `rec['mark']` (reasoned, before shipping, as defense against `rec` state going stale). A fresh-context adversarial review live-reproduced the opposite: a long-lived, busy session that kept WRITING for hours before an outage killed it (precisely the #172 stranded population) pushes its own `Goal set:` marker past the 4 MB tail window by the time the dark cap fires, so the "independent" bootstrap read finds NOTHING and wrongly concludes "not an outage" — exactly backwards from the decided semantics, and a REGRESSION the pre-fix code did not have (job 20's own incremental `rec['mark']` has NO such limit once tracking has begun, since a resumed-offset read has no window at all). Fix: `scan_goal_markers(tpath, off=0)` — read the WHOLE file — deliberately paying the extra cost, since the call only ever fires for the narrow population that is BOTH dark past the cap AND unconfirmed, never for a healthy sweep. Before shipping ANY "re-read X fresh, don't trust the cached value" design in this codebase, ask whether the fresh reader has its OWN size/window limit the cached value's own incremental accumulation does not — a supposedly MORE cautious independent check can be LESS correct than the state it was meant to double-check.
- **Before trusting a "fresh, independent check" design's own premise, ask whether the call site can even REACH a state where the fresh check would disagree with what's already known — reachability analysis is cheap and can retroactively validate (or invalidate) a whole design.** #173's own dark-cap branch is gated by an EARLIER `if rec.get("mark") != "set": continue` a few lines above — so by construction, `rec.get("mark")` is ALWAYS exactly `"set"` by the time the dark-check code runs. Once the F1 tail-window bug (bullet above) is fixed, `_goal_dark_died_by_outage`'s fresh, whole-file read and the caller's own `rec['mark']` therefore agree on every REACHABLE readable-transcript case — the two signals only ever diverge on a transcript that is unreadable/missing AT THE EXACT MOMENT of the check, which is precisely the ticket's own documented fail-safe edge case, not a hidden gap. Confirming this by tracing the gating `continue` back to its own guarantee is what let the fix stay a genuinely independent re-derivation (worth keeping, for exactly that narrow unreadable-transcript case) rather than degenerating into a pointless pass-through of an already-known-true value.
- **Mutation-test EVERY review-flagged gap individually, even a one-line fix and a one-line test — a full green suite after adding the test proves nothing about that ONE test's teeth.** #173: both adversarial-review findings (the F1 tail-window bug and F2's untested `dark_pinged` reset inside the new outage branch) were verified with the established backup-mutate-confirm-restore technique (`cp` the file aside, one targeted `str.replace` with an `assert mutated != src` guard, run ONLY the new test, confirm a genuine `AssertionError`, `cp` back and diff to confirm byte-identical restoration) before either fix was considered done — both mutants were killed by the new tests, not merely "the file changed and the suite is still green".
- **A registry-truth fix that migrates ONE glob-based "built" check does not migrate its SIBLINGS — grep the whole file for every OTHER glob-based "built"/"installed" gate before closing the ticket, or the identical defect ships again with a new plugin name.** #279: `_managed_plugin_built()`'s registry-truth fix (a prior ticket) left `_caveman_plugin_built()` — the ONE other "is this plugin genuinely installed" gate in the file — still globbing `CAVEMAN_CACHE_GLOBS`, and it reproduced the EXACT same bug class live on montalu4 (a fully-extracted cache dir, satisfying both known cache layouts, with zero `installed_plugins.json` entry — `setup_caveman()`'s `if not _caveman_plugin_built(): register + install` silently skipped the real install forever, zero log output). The fix was a one-line body swap (`CAVEMAN_PLUGIN_KEY in _plugin_registry_keys()`, reusing the sibling's already-hardened helper verbatim) — the cost was entirely in NOT having grepped for every "built"-shaped check when the sibling fix shipped. Whenever a "check claude's own registry instead of the filesystem" pattern lands anywhere in this file, immediately grep for every OTHER function with a similar name/purpose (`_*_built`, `_*_installed`, any `glob.glob` under `plugins/cache/`) and migrate them all in the same pass, or file each one as its own explicitly-scoped follow-up rather than leaving a silent gap.
- **A dead-code comment claiming "X still needs this constant" is falsifiable with one `grep -rn <constant>` — check it before writing it, not after a reviewer catches it.** #279's own first draft, migrating `_caveman_plugin_built()` off `CAVEMAN_CACHE_GLOBS`, left the constant in place with a comment asserting the runtime bash SHIM "still needs" it for its own `ls -dt` glob. False: the shim (`CAVEMAN_SHIM_CONTENT`) hardcodes its own two glob literals inline as bash strings and never reads the Python constant at all — a fresh-context adversarial review caught it with exactly `grep -rn CAVEMAN_CACHE_GLOBS .` (two hits: definition + the false docstring mention, zero real consumers). Whenever removing a constant's ONLY real reader, either delete the constant too (MVP philosophy — genuinely dead code) or grep for a genuine second consumer BEFORE writing a "kept because X still needs it" comment; don't assume a plausible-sounding relationship holds without checking.
- **A "benign, documented" code comment that generalizes a live per-box finding to un-checked sibling boxes ("montalu2/3, dev1/2, gatekeeper") is a falsifiable claim shipped as fact — verify EVERY box named, or scope the claim to only what was actually checked.** #279: the first draft claimed dev1/dev2/gatekeeper show a playwright git-hash version (only montalu3 was actually verified as the "healthy" control; dev1/dev2/gatekeeper were assumed by extrapolation). A same-day adversarial review independently re-checked dev1 live and found it ALSO shows `Version: unknown` — the exact "broken" shape the comment claimed was montalu4-specific. The underlying CONCLUSION (benign, no functional defect, `_managed_plugin_built()` already treats it as installed regardless) stayed correct throughout; only the box-by-box enumeration was wrong. Reword any "X boxes show A, Y boxes show B" claim in a shipped comment to state ONLY the boxes actually read (via ssh/registry inspection), never boxes assumed-by-analogy — a permanent code comment stating a falsifiable fleet fact is exactly this repo's own recurring wrong-stated-cause pattern, just moved from a bug fix into documentation.
- **Widening a `--self`/proven-boundary-shaped compact TRIGGER to a NEW population needs the SAME substantiality gate as the original population, not just the mechanism's own agent-type-agnosticism.** #228: `compact-request --self`'s `self-callback` origin deliberately SKIPS the `#99` no-work and `#48` small-context gates — correct for the autopilot-worker's per-ticket boundary (a completed ticket's durable state always lives in git/GitHub, per `#78`/`#121`'s own reasoning) but WRONG when the trigger is widened to "any served session's bare `✅ DONE:`", since that marker (per `message-status-marker.md`) can mean nothing more than a one-line answer. A fresh-context adversarial review caught this as the first CRITICAL: the fix was to keep the exemption but NARROW the trigger population to the full `## ✅ Work Complete` heading only (a structurally heavier signal, reserved for genuine completed work) rather than touching the exemption itself — the general lesson: when extending a proven-boundary origin's reach, ask what population the ORIGINAL exemption was sized for, and either match the new population to it or don't reuse the exemption.
- **Instructing a session to run a keystroke-typing/side-effecting tool call AFTER writing its own completion report, "as the last tool call before the turn ends", risks the report no longer being `last_assistant_message` — several hooks (the phone ping, a passive Stop-hook fallback, the report-structure gate) key on exactly that field.** #228's own adversarial review flagged this as the second CRITICAL: the established `/goal` worker templates already use this exact "report, then call `compact-request --self` as the last tool call" shape and are presumed safe in production (SubagentStop, a different event, with its own `last_assistant_message` semantics never independently verified for this exact ordering) — but extending the SAME shape to the plain top-level `Stop` event (a served session) could not be assumed safe on that precedent alone. The reviewer's fix, adopted rather than resolving CC's exact internal semantics: REORDER so the side-effecting call happens BEFORE the report text is written, once the task is internally confirmed done — the report then stays unambiguously the turn's final content regardless of how CC populates `last_assistant_message` around a trailing tool call. When a NEW caller wants to reuse an existing "call the tool as your last action" pattern shipped for a DIFFERENT hook event, don't assume the same ordering is safe there too — prefer the ordering that sidesteps the ambiguity entirely.
- **The "only the FIRST match" bug class (`re.search` where a compound Bash command needs `re.finditer`) recurs per-hook, not just per-repo — each hook's own extraction call needs its own audit.** #208: `post-record-design-comment.sh`'s issue-number extraction had the identical defect this file already documents for other mechanisms (a batch worker's `gh issue comment 117 -F a.md && gh issue comment 118 ... && gh issue comment 119 ...` only ever got #117 marked). The fix pattern is now well-established here (collect via `finditer`, dedup by first-seen order mirroring `design_gate.issue_refs`, loop the WHOLE per-item body once per item, turn every internal `sys.exit(0)`/`return`/`break` into a `continue`) — but converting a single-item script into a per-item LOOP this way creates a NEW class of bug worth checking for explicitly every time: any value resolved ONCE before the loop and assumed constant across items (see the next bullet).
- **Converting a single-item script into a per-item loop: audit EVERY value resolved ONCE before the loop for a hidden "this differs per item" assumption — a shared resolution is a NEW cross-item regression the rewrite itself introduces, not a pre-existing gap.** #208's adversarial review found exactly this: `-R`/`--repo` was resolved via a single `re.search` before the (new) multi-issue loop and reused for every issue in it — correct for the realistic, reported shape (the same repo repeated on every call) but capable of silently writing a LATER issue's marker under an EARLIER issue's WRONG repo when a compound command genuinely names two different repos, which can falsely unblock a `git commit` gate that was never actually satisfied. Fixed by refusing the whole command whenever `re.findall` finds MORE THAN ONE DISTINCT explicit value (fail toward the gate staying active, never toward guessing) rather than building a full per-segment/per-token parser for a case with no reported real occurrence — the same "minimum viable, refuse rather than guess" shape this file already applies elsewhere (`_search_index_healthy`, `required_refs`'s CLOSED-state discriminator). When reviewing ANY future "single-item → per-item-loop" conversion in this repo, explicitly list every variable computed BEFORE the loop and ask whether a real multi-item input could make it wrong for a later item.
- **Forcing a genuinely unexpected exception deep inside a hook's happy path, for testing a broad `except Exception` handler that has no narrower coverage: feed a WRONG-TYPE value through a field the code assumes is always a string.** #274/#208 review: a mutant replacing the new top-level `except Exception as exc: _log_exception(...)` with `except Exception: pass` passed every existing test, because the only exception test covered a narrower, separate `import` try/except. Seeding a fake `gh` comment with `"body": 123` (an int, not a string) reliably reproduces `(123 or "").strip()` -> `AttributeError` inside `design_gate.classify_design_comment` with no timeout, no crafted process failure, and no mocking needed — a cheap, deterministic way to reach a hook's OUTER exception handler specifically, distinct from any inner, narrower try/except.
- **Testing that a per-item loop's own try/except around a `subprocess.run(cwd=...)` call genuinely isolates one item's failure from a sibling — a nonexistent `cwd` value is a reliable, fast trigger, but the observable signal must be the LOG, not the fake subprocess's own call count.** `subprocess.run(cwd=<nonexistent path>)` raises `FileNotFoundError` in the PARENT process, before the child is ever exec'd — so a fake `gh` binary on PATH never gets invoked at all and its own invocation log stays empty for BOTH items regardless of whether the loop correctly `continue`s or wrongly `sys.exit(0)`s after the first. The discriminating evidence is instead in the hook's OWN diagnostic log (`_log_exception("gh-view #%d" % issue, exc)`): a correct `continue` produces one log line PER issue (`gh-view #41`, `gh-view #42`), a `sys.exit(0)` mutant produces only the first. Also note: an EXPLICIT `-R`/`--repo` in the test command is needed to bypass `notify.repo_name_for(cwd)` (which uses `git -C <cwd>` internally, not a real chdir, so it fails GRACEFULLY on a bad cwd and returns `""` — causing an EARLY exit before the loop is ever reached, not the per-issue exception this test wants to isolate).

- **A digit-blind loop-shape hash cannot tell a TUNING knob from a TARGET-identifying number, and the existing test suite can encode the bug as "intended" rather than catch it.** #281: `block-local-poll-repeat.sh`'s repeat-detection KEY blinds every numeric literal (`sed -E 's/(^| )[0-9]+( |$)/ N /g; s/[0-9]+/N/g'`) before hashing — deliberate for a retuned `sleep 15`->`sleep 20` on the SAME wait (must still collide), wrong for a PR/issue number embedded in `gh pr view <N>`/`gh issue view <N>` (the identifying TARGET, not a tuning knob) — so `gh pr view 436` then `gh pr view 704` hashed to the same key and the second was wrongly blocked as a repeat. The bug was already pinned as INTENDED behaviour by a shipped test (`test_a_different_pr_number_is_the_same_shape_digit_blind`, asserting `returncode == 2` for exactly this case) — a genuinely wrong test gets INVERTED in its own well-justified commit (per `regression-test-first.md`), not left in place: flip the assertion, rename the test, and that inversion IS the RED commit (fails against the current hook, passes only once fixed). Fix mirrors #118's own RUN_ID extraction in the sibling CI hook: anchor a TARGET regex to the known signature (`gh (pr|issue) view <N>`), fold it into the state-file key ONLY when found (`${SHAPE}-t${TARGET}`), leave every loop with no such signature on the pure SHAPE key unchanged — so the existing "retuning still collides" guarantee for non-targeted loops (obs_loop/task_loop/ssh_loop, none of which carry a `gh pr view`/`gh issue view` token) needs no test changes at all. Before widening ANY digit-blind key extraction like this, check whether an EXISTING pinned test already encodes the exact wrong behaviour as intended — `grep -rn "<the wrong assertion>" tests/` before touching the hook, since the test itself is often the clearest evidence the bug is real.
- **A target-anchored regex extraction (`gh <verb> <N>`) needs a FLAG-TOLERANT match from day one — a routine `-R owner/repo`/`--repo owner/repo` between the verb and the number is common in THIS repo's own corpus, and a regex requiring the number to sit IMMEDIATELY after the verb silently reproduces the bug for that spelling.** #281's own adversarial review found this live: the first-cut fix (`gh[[:space:]]+(pr|issue)[[:space:]]+view[[:space:]]+[0-9]+`) matched only the dominant `gh pr view <N> --repo o/r` shape (digits right after `view`) and fell back to the pure-shape key for `gh pr view -R o/r <N>`/`--repo o/r <N>` — reproducing the ORIGINAL bug for exactly those two spellings. Fix: an optional, zero-or-more repeated flag-group (`([[:space:]]+-[^[:space:]]+([[:space:]]+[^-[:space:]][^[:space:]]*)?)*`) between the verb and the required digit run — matches a bare flag (`-R`), a `--word` flag, a `--word=value` single-token form, AND a flag with a separate value token, while a dominant "digits right after the verb" command matches with zero repetitions, unaffected. NOT chased (documented as an accepted residual, matching this file's own established precedent for CI_RX/the mutating-action regex — every text-only signature in this repo has this same limitation): a URL form (`.../pull/<N>`), a shell-VARIABLE form (`gh pr view "$P"`), a backslash line-continuation between the two verb words, and a decoy target merely MENTIONED in an echo/comment. **Companion finding worth generalising: bound a newly-extracted numeric field's LENGTH (`{1,10}`) even when no realistic input could be that long** — an absurdly long decoy digit run (e.g. `"9"*300`) can build a state-file NAME past the filesystem's NAME_MAX, and the hook's own pre-existing `|| true` on the write silently swallows that failure, making the guard fail OPEN (never mark, never block) forever — the one direction a repeat-detection guard must never fail in. **Third finding, purely a test-coverage gap: hand-mutate `head -1` -> `tail -1`, the key's separator character, and any newly-added log FIELD individually, even when they "obviously must be right"** — all three SURVIVED the pre-review test suite (0 test failures) despite being real, independently-mutable pieces of the fix, and each got its own dedicated regression-lock test with ZERO behaviour change (all three passed immediately against the already-shipped code) rather than being dismissed as "self-evidently correct."
- **A `python3 - "$1" <<'PYEOF'` heredoc invocation is STILL an argv-size hazard even though the SCRIPT SOURCE arrives via the heredoc — the DATA passed as `"$1"` is the part that dies at Linux's MAX_ARG_STRLEN (131072 bytes), and this repo had already fixed the identical shape once (`hooks/lib-poll-payload.sh`, #124) before shipping a SECOND instance of it in a sibling hook.** #195: `stop-check-prose-violations.sh`'s `strip_mentions()` passed the WHOLE assistant message as `python3 - "$1" <<'PYEOF'`'s single argv entry; past 131072 bytes `execve` failed with E2BIG, `python3` never started, and the caller's bare `2>/dev/null || printf` fallback silently substituted the RAW, unstripped message — turning a backticked/quoted MENTION of a banned phrase into what every downstream check read as a bare unquoted OFFER, reproduced live (131036 bytes not blocked, 131111 bytes wrongly hard-blocked). The fix is the SAME one #124 already established: write the data to a `mktemp` scratch file first, pass only the FILE PATH as argv (small, safe at any data size), have the embedded Python `open()` the file instead of reading `sys.argv[1]`. `grep -rn 'python3 - "\$' hooks/` (or similar) is worth running whenever adding a NEW argv-fed heredoc invocation, since this exact shape is now proven to recur.
- **Reading a file with Python's default `open(path, "r")` is NOT byte-equivalent to what `sys.argv[1]` provides — `newline=None` (the default) silently applies UNIVERSAL NEWLINE TRANSLATION (a lone `\r` and a `\r\n` pair both become `\n`), and argv strings never go through that translation at all, since they're not read via the text-mode I/O layer.** #195's own argv-to-file fix (previous bullet) introduced exactly this regression: a genuine bare bypass offer whose two halves straddled a lone `\r` used to block (argv preserved the `\r`, keeping the phrase on one grep-visible "line") and, through the new file read with the default `newline=None`, silently stopped blocking (the `\r`→`\n` translation split the phrase across what `grep -qE "merge.*despite"` sees as two separate lines) — caught only by a fresh-context adversarial review, live-reproduced against BOTH the pre-fix and post-fix hook. Fix: open with `newline=""` — this disables universal-newline translation entirely while still returning line endings UNtranslated to the caller (per Python's own `io.TextIOWrapper` docs), giving byte-faithful content equivalent to what argv used to carry. Whenever a fix moves data from argv/a pipe/a bytes object into a Python `open(path, "r")` call and the OLD path is claimed to be behaviorally equivalent, that claim needs an explicit newline-handling check — it is easy to get spuriously right for the common LF-only case and spuriously wrong for anything containing a lone `\r` or CRLF.
- **`$?` inside a `CMD || { ...; VAR="fallback"; }` block is reliably the exit code that triggered the `||` — but ONLY if it is read as the VERY FIRST statement in the block; ANY intervening command, including a plain variable assignment, silently overwrites it before the read.** #195's own review-fix pass hit this TWICE in the same ticket, despite the ticket's OWN earlier commits already correctly doing `record_undet "$?" ...` as literally the block's first statement: a later "improve the diagnostic wording" edit inserted `_NOTE="...long string..."` (itself an ordinary, ALWAYS-successful assignment command) ahead of the `$?` read "for readability", which silently changed every reported failure to read as `check exited 0` — caught only by a manual live probe (an unwritable `TMPDIR`, expecting a nonzero code, seeing `0`) run BEFORE dispatching further review, not by the test suite (no existing test asserted the exact numeric code). Fixed by capturing `_RC=$?` as the true first statement, THEN building any derived string, THEN calling the function that consumes it. Verified with the established mutation technique (`cp` the file aside, apply the exact "assignment-before-`$?`" mutation via a targeted `str.replace`, confirm the new regression test fails with a real `AssertionError`, restore, confirm it passes again). Any future edit that adds a "helper" statement — a wording tweak, a log line, an extra assignment — ahead of an EXISTING `"$?"` read in a `||` fallback block is this exact bug in disguise; the discipline generalizes past this one ticket.
- **`CMD <<'DELIM' ... DELIM || VAR=$?` is valid, `set -e`-safe bash — the `||` (or any trailing redirection/pipe modifier) belongs on the SAME initial command line as the heredoc's opening redirection, and it correctly captures the exit code of the whole heredoc-fed command once its body (terminated by the bare `DELIM` line) finishes.** Verified with a standalone repro (`python3 - <<'PYEOF' || _rc=$?` where the heredoc body calls `sys.exit(7)`; the caller correctly read `_rc=7` and the enclosing function's later lines — `rm -f`, `return "$_rc"` — all still ran, i.e. `set -e` did NOT abort mid-function). Reused for #195's `strip_mentions()` rewrite; this is the missing piece the existing `python3 - "$1" <<'PYEOF'` shape in this file never needed until a heredoc-fed command's OWN failure (rather than its caller's `$( )` substitution failing as a whole) had to be caught and handled INSIDE the function that ran it.
- **When a dead worker's own transcript shows an async `Agent` dispatch but no visible review REPORT text (only "Async agent launched successfully" + its own wait loop, then a fatal API error before any result appears) — the dispatched agent has its OWN SEPARATE transcript file, not a section further down the worker's own.** #280 resume: the launch tool_result prints `agentId: <hex>`; that id is a real subagent id with its own file at `<projects-dir>/<session>/subagents/agent-<that-id>.jsonl`, ONE LEVEL alongside (not nested inside) the worker's own `agent-<worker-id>.jsonl`. Its LAST `assistant` entry's `text` content block is the review's actual final report — read that file directly rather than grepping the worker's own transcript for report-shaped text (a search for the review's own emoji legend / verdict wording inside the WORKER's file only ever finds the dispatch PROMPT echoing those same words back, via `toolUseResult.prompt` — a false lead that looks like a hit but is the request, not the answer).
- **A test proving "an attacker-controlled `-R`/`--repo` value must NOT be used" needs the fake repo's BASENAME to differ from the real repo's name — reusing the SAME owner-qualified name (`attacker/<real-repo-name>`) makes the test pass for the wrong reason, both before AND after the fix.** #280 F4: the first draft used `attacker/airuleset` as the injected value; `repo_key = explicit_repo.rstrip("/").split("/")[-1]` (last PATH SEGMENT only, owner discarded) resolves that to `"airuleset"` — coincidentally identical to the REAL repo's key — so the assertion `self.marker(41)` (checks the "airuleset" key) passed even under the UNFIXED, vulnerable code, and the companion `assertIsNone(dg.read_marker("attacker", 41))` was ALSO vacuous (the owner half is never used as the key at all). Confirmed RED only after re-picking `attacker/evil-repo` (a basename that cannot coincide with the real repo). Whenever a test's "must not leak to the attacker" assertion checks a derived/truncated form of the injected value (a basename, a hash, a first-N-chars slice), pick the injected value so its OWN derived form is guaranteed to differ from the legitimate one — never assume a plausible-looking fake value is actually distinguishable after the code's own transform is applied to it.
- **`gh label create --force` is a live, standing side-effect hazard on shared production repo state — it OVERWRITES an EXISTING label's colour/description unconditionally, every single call, and this repo's own `#191` review caught it only by reading the REAL current label metadata on `zbynekdrlik/odoo-erp`** (each stream had a hand-curated colour + Slovak description; the buggy version would have silently collapsed all of them to one generic purple on the very next gk-request from any stream). Fix: read-only existence check first (`gh label list --search <label> --json name -q '.[].name'`, exact-match against the returned lines), and only `gh label create` (never `--force`) when genuinely absent. Whenever a future feature needs to "ensure a label exists" idempotently, check-then-create, never force-overwrite — `--force`'s own `gh label create --help` text ("update the label color and description if label already exists") is the tell that it is NOT the idempotent-no-op primitive it sounds like.
- **A "was X ever true in this history" check is the wrong question whenever the history can legitimately record a LATER, contradicting fact — resolve the TEMPORALLY-LAST matching event instead, using ordering the API already gives you for free.** #191 MAJOR M3: an "ever applied" recovery check over a GraphQL issue's `LABELED_EVENT` timeline let TWO streams that had both once owned a ticket (A -> B -> unlabelled) BOTH reclaim it, since a CURRENT-label bounding check only helps when a current label survives — exactly the case the recovery step exists to handle the absence of. GitHub's `timelineItems` connection returns nodes in chronological order; walking them in order and keeping the LAST match (rather than stopping at the first, or asking "is X anywhere in here") resolves competing historical claims correctly at zero extra API cost — the same response already carries every event, only the READING of it needed to change. Also worth the pairing: fetch `last: N` (most recent N events), never `first: N` (oldest N) when what you're looking for tends to be applied LATE in an object's life (an origin/hand-off label, almost by definition, since the ticket existed before it was ever handed off) — `first:` silently truncates away exactly the event being searched for on a sufficiently churned object.
- **A "does field X land" `isdigit()`/type guard added to protect a NEW verification step can itself become a NEW false-success path if it also short-circuits the FAILURE branch — a guard belongs on the entry to the whole block, not sprinkled on each individual verification/degrade step inside it.** #221 adversarial review MAJOR: the first cut of the fix above gated BOTH the label-add attempt AND the retitle-degrade attempt on `new_num.isdigit()` — so a `gh issue create` success whose stdout didn't end in parseable digits (anomalous but real: extra trailing output, a format change) skipped BOTH verification paths and fell straight through to the same false "gk-request filed" the whole fix was meant to prevent, just triggered by unparseable stdout instead of a denied label. Fix: check the precondition ONCE, at the top, and fail loudly IMMEDIATELY if it doesn't hold (`if not new_num.isdigit(): print("... cannot verify ..."); return 1`) — every downstream step then runs unconditionally, correctly, with the precondition already guaranteed. Whenever a fix threads the SAME guard condition through multiple independent branches of a function, check whether hoisting it to one early return instead removes N duplicated (and easy-to-miss) fail-open gaps at once.
- **A worktree-isolated worker waiting FOREGROUND on an async `Agent` dispatch (per the SubagentStop "in-flight background work" gate) can hit a SECOND, unrelated guard: the worktree-isolation checker refuses ANY multi-statement/compound Bash command it judges "too complex to verify stays inside the worktree" — including a plain `for`/`while` poll loop with zero git operations in it — with no way to tell in advance which shapes it will accept.** #256: a `DEADLINE=$((SECONDS+N)); for ...; do ...; done` loop, a `while [ "$SECONDS" -lt "$DEADLINE" ]; do sleep 20; done` loop, and even the SAME loop with a `# airuleset:poll-ok` bypass comment appended were ALL refused with "this command is too complex to verify that it stays inside the worktree" — a message that reads like a git-safety refusal but fires on commands touching no `git`/repo state at all. What actually worked: the SIMPLEST possible loop shape (`for i in 1 2 3 ...; do sleep N; done`, small literal iteration list, no `$(())` arithmetic, no `while`/`[ ]` test, no variable assignment) — this got past the worktree checker on the first try, then needed only the SEPARATE `block-local-poll-repeat.sh` guard's own documented `# airuleset:poll-ok <reason>` bypass (since the earlier REFUSED attempts still counted as an observed repeat of the same normalised loop shape) plus a `timeout` near the Bash tool's 600000ms cap. Lesson for any future worktree-dispatched worker needing a genuine long foreground wait (CI, an async subagent, anything): reach for the PLAIN literal-list `for` loop shape FIRST, not `while`/arithmetic-bounded forms — it is both the one proven to pass the worktree checker AND the one `ci-monitoring.md`'s own snippet already uses.
- **The FIRST correct-looking fix for this class still needs a metachar-stop check — a scanner that stops "at whitespace" is not the same as one that stops at a real bash WORD BOUNDARY, and a fresh-context adversarial review found the gap the same day.** #282's own review: `_shell_word_end` stopped only at whitespace, never at an unquoted `;`/`&`/`|`/`(`/`)`/`<`/`>` — real bash word terminators outside quotes too. A separator glued directly to a value's closing quote with NO space (`--body "x";gh issue comment 99 --body "y"`, the exact shape #208's own batch-posting tests already exercise) was consumed INTO the blanked span, silently eating the first word of the next command and losing a real, later invocation's own marker. Fixed with one extra branch (`elif c in ";&|()<>": break`, checked BEFORE the backslash-escape branch so an escaped `\;` inside unquoted text still correctly skips as a literal) — but the general lesson is the check itself: whenever writing a "scan forward until the word ends" primitive, explicitly enumerate bash's real terminator set (whitespace AND `;`/`&`/`|`/`(`/`)`/`<`/`>`) rather than stopping at whitespace alone, and write a discriminator test proving the correct continuation-aware scanner differs from BOTH a "stop at the first closing quote" reimplementation (reopens the original leak) AND a "stop at any following character" reimplementation (the regression above) — a single splice-idiom test cannot distinguish either wrong shape on its own.
- **A documentation claim of "matching real bash tokenization" (or any similarly absolute claim about a hand-written scanner) needs to be checked against a REAL residual before it ships — this repo has shipped that exact overclaim once already (#5's pattern) and #282 caught it in the same review that found the metachar-stop bug the overclaim was hiding.** `_shell_word_end` does not parse `$( … )`/backtick command substitution as nested — a value like `--body "$(printf '%s' "hi") gh issue comment 99"` mis-tokenizes identically before and after the fix, since the inner quotes inside the substitution are read as ordinary boundary characters. Confirmed THEORETICAL and pre-existing (not introduced or closed by #282, never observed in production) — documented directly in the function's own docstring rather than filed as a new ticket (matches this file's own established "known, deliberate residual" convention for `lib_poll_payload.py`'s heredoc-detection half) and rather than left as a silent overclaim for the next reader to trust.

- **A ticket's own literally-named tmux/config VALUE can be flatly wrong even when its PROSE describes the right behavior — empirically test the value against a real server, never ship what the title says.** #254: the ticket titled itself "destroy-unattached keep-group" and described the desired behavior accurately in its own body ("destroy an unattached session ONLY when it is in a group and other sessions of the group remain") — but that description is `keep-last`'s semantics, not `keep-group`'s. tmux's own man page defines them as near-mirror-image formulas that happen to collapse to the IDENTICAL destroy-condition for a session already in a multi-member group (`destroy iff group-member-count > 1`, either way) — so the two values are indistinguishable for the exact scenario a bug report usually describes. They diverge ONLY for a session NOT in any group at all: `keep-last`'s "in a group AND has other sessions" is false for a standalone session (never destroyed); `keep-group`'s "unless in a group AND alone" is also false for a standalone session, but the NOT() flips it to `always destroy` — identical to boolean `on`. Verified live: a real pty-attached-then-detached STANDALONE session, on an isolated `-L` scratch tmux 3.7b server, was destroyed under `keep-group` and survived under `keep-last`. Since almost every real session on this fleet is a plain standalone project session (never linked via `new-session -t`), shipping the ticket's own literally-named `keep-group` would have been a severe fleet-wide regression — including sweeping the subdev stream accounts' permanently-detached, standalone, headless `claude` sessions on the very next live-applied push. **Lesson: when a ticket names a specific option VALUE (not just an option name), test that exact value's real behavior empirically before shipping it — a ticket's prose can be exactly right while its own suggested value name is exactly wrong**, and the two look interchangeable until you actually try both against a real server.
- **A tmux option can be safe to LIVE-APPLY for a reason that has NOTHING to do with #236/#241's window-size precedent — check the option's own definition, not just "did a prior incident clear a similar-sounding option".** `destroy-unattached` (#254) is safe to live-apply against a running server for a structurally different reason than `history-limit`/`bind-key` were: it is DEFINED to only ever evaluate sessions with ZERO attached clients, so by construction it can never disturb anything a live client currently has on screen — a stronger, definition-level guarantee than "we tested it and nothing broke". Verified live: a running server holding an attached grouped session, an attached standalone session, and two ALREADY-DETACHED grouped duplicates — live-applying `destroy-unattached keep-last` immediately swept the two duplicates away (no fresh attach/detach cycle needed) while leaving both attached sessions completely untouched. This ALSO answers "how do already-piled-up duplicates get cleaned" for free: the live-apply itself performs a one-time sweep on the very next `push`/`install`, with zero new hook/watchdog job needed — worth checking for on any future "does live-applying this option retroactively fix existing bad state" question, since not every option gets this for free (`default-size`, for contrast, only affects NEW windows going forward).
- **Widening a marker-FAMILY hook check to a NEW sibling marker (`🌐` -> `🌐|📱`) is only as safe as the module reservation backing it — a hook comment claiming the two markers are "used EXCLUSIVELY" for a presented URL is FALSE the moment the module never says so for the NEW one, and that gap is a real, triggerable false-positive.** #265: `stop-check-prose-violations.sh`'s already-narrowly-scoped `🌐` localhost-ban was widened to also cover a new `📱` (installable-build) artifact marker `completion-report.md` introduced for client-app demo/APK reports — safe for `🌐` (which the module already reserves in bold: "`🌐` lines = USER-CLICKABLE web URLs only") but NOT yet for `📱`, which had no equivalent reservation. A fresh-context adversarial review constructed the exact failure: a decorative sentence like "`📱` tested against localhost during dev" (mentioning mobile testing, not presenting a URL) would hard-block, because the two-stage selector-then-pattern grep only needs the marker AND a localhost string on the SAME line, with no semantic check that the marker actually introduces a URL. Fix: add the missing reservation bullet to the module (mirroring the `🌐` one) rather than complicating the hook — this makes the hook comment's claim TRUE instead of trying to teach the regex what a "decorative" use looks like. Whenever a hook selector is widened to a new marker/keyword by reusing an existing check's logic, verify the SAME exclusivity/reservation claim the check's comment makes about the OLD marker is ALSO independently true for the new one — a green test suite proves the mechanics work, never that the English claim backing them is honest.
- **tmux's OWN conf-file parser EXPANDS `$VAR` at CONF-PARSE/BIND time, using tmux's OWN process environment -- not the shell's -- and it does this INSIDE a double-quoted string AND when the word is left bare/unquoted (verified live, tmux 3.7b).** #289: an inline shell one-liner directly on a `bind-key ... display-popup ...` conf line (`CH_OUT=$(...); CH_RC=$?; if [ "$CH_RC" -ne 0 ] ...`) silently rendered `if [ "" -ne 0 ]` at runtime -- confirmed via `list-keys` on the ACTUAL bound command (showing the blanked value) AND via a real Shift+F1 keypress through a genuinely attached pty client reading the raw popup overlay bytes (`capture-pane` cannot see a popup's content at all -- it is a client-side rendering overlay, never part of any pane's own buffer). Single-quoted tmux strings do NOT expand `$VAR` (also verified live) but tmux's own single-quote parsing supports no escapes at all, so embedding a command needing several literal `'` characters would require the fragile POSIX quote-splice idiom (`'...'\''...'`, confirmed tmux honours it) on every one of them. **Fix: move any logic needing a real shell-runtime variable into its OWN script file, invoked by its absolute path** -- the only thing the tmux bind-key line then needs to resolve is the path itself, which needs no `$VAR` to survive the conf-parser at all (`CLAUDE_HISTORY_POPUP_SCRIPT_DEST`). `_tmux_conf_quote()` -- the helper that quotes individual conf-line words for tmux's own grammar -- REFUSES (raises `ValueError`) any word containing a literal `$` rather than attempting to escape it, since no quoting form protects a `$` from this expansion; a fresh-context adversarial review caught an earlier docstring draft asserting the exact opposite ("passes $VAR straight through untouched"), which would have re-invited this same bug class into any future conf-line author who trusted the helper's own documentation instead of testing live. Reuse this refuse-rather-than-guess shape for ANY future tmux conf-line helper: a literal `$` in a rendered tmux WORD is never safe to quote around, only safe to avoid entirely.
- **Live-verifying a BRAND-NEW Discord thread/channel this session just created: `mcp__plugin_discord_discord__fetch_messages` fails with `channel <id> is not allowlisted — add via /discord:access`, because that MCP tool's channel allowlist is a separate, human-managed gate unrelated to whatever this repo's own bot token can already reach.** #296 live verification: reading `notify._read_env()['DISCORD_BOT_TOKEN']` and calling `notify._discord_api(tok, 'GET', 'channels/<id>/messages?limit=N')` directly (the SAME raw HTTP helper `find_owner_question_thread`/`create_owner_question_thread` already use) reads any channel the bot is a member of, with zero allowlist gate — this is the correct, already-available tool for verifying a `-q` thread's content without asking the user to run `/discord:access`. The same helper's `DELETE` verb (`_discord_api(tok, 'DELETE', 'channels/<id>/messages/<mid>')`) cleanly removes a test message (returns `{}` on Discord's 204 No Content; re-`GET` to confirm) — the correct way to leave a live-verification channel clean afterward instead of littering it with test pings.
- **Adding a NEW injectable callable param to an already-established dispatcher function: grep the function BODY for what its OWN existing locals are named, not just what parameter names already exist in the signature.** #297/#298: `deliver_discord_replies` already builds a LOCAL variable `gh_fn = gh_comment or _gh_comment` (the ticket-fallback gh-comment path) — a first draft added a brand-new `gh_fn=None` PARAMETER for a completely different purpose (the card-reopen flow's gh calls), which the existing `gh_fn = gh_comment or _gh_comment` line then silently overwrote on every call, losing the injected test double with no error at all (both are legal Python, so nothing fails loudly — the new feature just silently used the WRONG fallback path in real production). Caught only because a test for the new param immediately failed with the WRONG gh calls recorded. Fixed by naming the new param `card_gh_fn` — distinct from BOTH the existing `gh_comment` parameter and its derived `gh_fn` local. Reuse this check (grep the function body's own local-variable names, not just `def f(...)`'s signature) whenever extending an existing large dispatcher with a new injectable dependency of a similar shape to one it already has.
- **Rendering a static "idle Claude Code prompt" shape into a SCRATCH tmux pane for a live keystroke-delivery test: the synthetic shape must be the LAST thing the pane renders — a script that PRINTS the shape and then EXITS lets the shell's own prompt render underneath it, which the real boundary-detection code correctly reads as the pane's ACTUAL current boundary, defeating the test.** #297/#298 live verification: `tmux send-keys 'bash script_that_prints_and_exits'` left `bash-5.2$` on the line below the intended `❯` boundary, so `pane_at_idle_prompt`/`_find_boundary_line` (correctly) found the shell prompt, not the synthetic one, and refused to type. Fix: chain a blocking tail with NO output onto the SAME command (`printf '<shape>'; sleep <bound>`) so the printed shape stays the pane's own current render with nothing appearing after it — `capture-pane` then shows exactly the intended IDLE fixture shape used throughout this repo's own test suite (`"● …\n❯ \n  ctx …\n"`), and the real `send_continue`/`_try_stash_nudge` delivery path can be proven end-to-end against a REAL (if isolated) tmux server.
- **Verifying an owner-only reaction/reply security gate LIVE, with no second human Discord account available to react/reply as: the BOT's OWN reaction/message IS a free, completely real negative control — never skip straight to a synthetic payload.** #297: adding a real ❓ reaction to a real card VIA THE BOT, then calling the real `fetch_reaction_users` against the real Discord API, correctly returned the bot's OWN id as the sole reactor (`"bot": true`) — and the SAME security check (`known_owner_ids`/`_reacted_by_owner`) correctly rejected it, since a bot's id is never in that set by design. This proves the security check fires against genuine API response SHAPE and genuine rejection logic, not merely a hand-built fixture. Only the POSITIVE case (an actual owner reacting/replying) needs a synthetic id substituted into an otherwise fully-real payload (real referenced message id, real channel, real Discord-shaped `author`/reactor dict) — state plainly which half was live and which was synthetic, per this repo's own established live-verification discipline, rather than skipping the free negative control that the bot's own identity already gives you.
- **A pane-delivery dedup gate that is CORRECT for "the exact session this ❓ belongs to" (job 7's own idle/draft-stash discipline) is WRONG for "any repo-fallback pane" (job 8's stronger at-rest guard, which refuses ANY session whose transcript's last marker is ❓ or ⏳) — and both are needed on the SAME code path, discriminated by a single boolean.** #297 adversarial-review MAJOR-1: `_flag_delivery_target` resolves either the EXACT asking session (question-kind, session still live) or a repo-fallback pane (question-kind with a dead session, or card-kind) — the first genuinely needs job 7's own gate (the very session sitting on the ❓ that is being flagged ABOUT must never be refused just because its own marker IS that ❓), the second genuinely needs job 8's stronger discipline (an unrelated live pane of the repo must not be typed into mid-turn). Returning a 4th element (`exact: bool`) alongside `(sid, pid, cwd)` and branching the CALLER on it (`_deliver_flag_prompt_to_exact_session` vs `_nudge_repo_pane`) is the fix — never collapse two genuinely different safety postures into one shared helper just because both resolve "a pane to deliver into".
- **A shared "sweep-scoped, persist BEFORE the next mutation" discipline (jobs 8/11's `seen[label] = {...}; persist()` shape) generalizes cleanly to a per-MESSAGE loop, not just a per-TARGET loop — thread a `persist=None` param (default no-op) through the dispatcher, call it immediately after EACH successful dedup-mark, and wire the real caller's `lambda: save_state(state_path, state)` at the SAME call site jobs 8/11 already use.** #297/#298 review MAJOR-4: `deliver_discord_replies`'s `dcard_done`/`dreact_done` state updates only ever happened in TWO places — inside the per-message loop (in-memory list mutation) and once at the very END of the whole function (`state["dcard_done"] = card_done[...]`) — so a mid-sweep kill between two fetched messages could lose the dedup marker for a mutation (a real `gh` reopen, a real keystroke delivery) that had ALREADY landed, reproducing it on the next sweep. Fix: write `state["dcard_done"]`/`state["dreact_done"]` AND call `persist()` at the exact point each mutation succeeds, not just at function end — the trailing end-of-function assignment stays too (redundant-but-harmless for the ordinary no-kill case).
- **The SAME "malformed/legacy value crossing a dict-boundary is not a dict" gap recurred TWICE in one review pass, in two sibling functions (`record_card_message`'s prune loop, then `record_question`'s identical-shaped prune loop) — fixing the first found by the review does not fix the second, and it is cheap enough to fix BOTH in the same pass rather than filing a follow-up for a same-file, same-shape, <10-line sibling.** #297/#298 MINOR-7: `for mid in [m for m, v in d.items() if now - (v.get("ts") or 0) > TTL]` crashes with `AttributeError: 'str' object has no attribute 'get'` the moment ANY entry in the persisted JSON map is a non-dict scalar (a hand-corrupted file, a future writer bug) — `isinstance(v, dict)` gates BOTH the TTL-prune filter and the hard-cap sort's key function (`kv[1].get("ts") or 0 if isinstance(kv[1], dict) else 0`). Grepping the WHOLE file for the exact anchor shape (`.get("ts")`) before considering a fix "done" is what caught the qmap sibling — a fix scoped to only the function the review named would have left the identical crash reachable one function over.

- **Not every pre-existing bug discovered while fixing its byte-identical sibling belongs in the SAME PR — the discriminator is blast radius on the LIVE, ALWAYS-RUNNING path, not LoC.** #304 (filed, not fixed): `_ack`/`_delivered` (the PRE-EXISTING, core ❓-reply delivery helpers) mark `acked_set`/`done_set` unconditionally on `dry_run`, and `run_once`'s trailing `save_state()` persists that unconditionally too — the byte-identical shape to the #297/#298 card-path MAJOR-2 fix (`card_done_set.add` gated `if reopened and not dry_run:`), just in code that predates this ticket and that EVERY stream's real Discord replies flow through continuously. The qmap/cardmap MINOR-7 sibling above was fixed in-place (near-zero behavioral risk, a crash-guard); this one was filed instead, because it changes WHEN the hot path's own dedup state gets marked and deserves its own `regression-test-first.md` RED→GREEN pair plus a live-verification pass, not a rushed addendum at the tail of an already-large review-fix commit.

- **An existing recovery block's precondition can be reused for a LATER failure branch in the SAME function, once that later branch is only reachable AFTER the SAME precondition was already independently proven earlier in the call — extract it into a shared helper rather than duplicating it.** #306: `deliver_with_stash`'s pre-existing #193 verify-failure recovery (backspace exactly `text`, then — only once bare is reconfirmed — pop a parked draft back with one corrective `C-s`) relies on "the box's entire content is provably exactly `text`". The NEW "swallowed-submit-not-recovered" branch is reached only after an EARLIER type-verify step in the same call already proved that exact fact (either via a bare-box settle poll, or via `_typed_exclusively`), so the identical recovery is safe to reuse there too — and, since `_send_goal_verified` (a sibling primitive) proves the same fact via its own bare-box-only entry gate, a third call site reused it with `parked` hardcoded `False`. `_undo_and_release_slot(pid, run, text, parked, log_fn, prefix)` takes a `prefix` string so the ORIGINAL call site's log wording stays byte-identical to pre-fix (verified against `git show <pre-fix-sha>`) while new call sites get their own diagnostic phrasing. When a precondition is proven ONCE earlier in a function and a later branch inherits it for free, look for the SHARED recovery rather than writing branch-local backspace/undo logic a second time.
- **Dispatching the required adversarial review at `model: fable` (gate OPEN) with a DETAILED PROSE digest — root cause, chosen approach, exact function/line pointers, a full pasted diff, and an explicit REFUTE framing with a numbered checklist of specific things to verify — produced a genuinely useful SAFE-TO-SHIP verdict (0 CRITICAL/0 MAJOR/2 MINOR/4 THEORETICAL) on a small (~150-line) watchdog-primitive diff in one pass, in well under the foreground poll-loop wait budget.** #306: worth the ~2-minute prompt-writing cost even for a small, already-well-tested fix — the review independently re-derived the pre-fix log-wording claim from `git show`, traced every `Escape` reachable from the modified paths for the #35/#36 double-Escape hazard, and found BOTH real minors via genuine code tracing (not guessing) rather than restating the digest back. Reinforces the existing established pattern in this file: a review given root-cause+approach+diff (never "just the repo") reliably finds real, fixable gaps even on a change the implementer already unit-tested and self-reviewed carefully.

- **A phrase-presence test (`self.assertIn(label, line)`) over a LONG, multi-clause line (a `/goal` template, a dense paragraph) can pass for the WRONG reason when the searched-for string ALSO appears in a totally unrelated clause later in the same line — and a design change that removes the string from the clause the test was ACTUALLY about leaves the test silently still green.** #307 review finding: `test_the_full_template_scopes_the_proof_to_the_obligation_set` asserted `"prio:bounce" in line` to prove the OBLIGATION-SET clause counted it — true when written, but after #307 dropped `prio:bounce` from that clause, the assertion kept passing anyway because the SAME template line's UNRELATED bounce-lane SEED sentence ("Bounce lane: open tickets labeled prio:bounce jump the queue...") still contains the literal string. The fix is to SCOPE the check to the specific clause's own window (`line[line.index(<clause start>):line.index(<clause end>)]`) rather than the whole line, so the assertion can only pass for the reason it claims to. Whenever reviewing (or writing) a phrase-presence test against a long multi-clause string, ask "could this exact substring plausibly appear ANYWHERE else on this line for an unrelated reason" — if yes, scope the search window to the specific clause, don't trust a bare `assertIn` over the whole blob.

- **Satisfying a structural lock requiring `X(` to be immediately followed, with nothing but whitespace, by `Y(` (e.g. `stream_redirect(owner_of(pid))`) while ALSO preserving correct semantics that need normalization BEFORE the wrapped call: move the normalization to AFTER the whole wrapped expression, not before — when the wrapped call's real production value is already normalized, this is exactly equivalent and keeps the call site literally matching what the lock scans for.** #302: `owner = (owner_of(pid) or "").strip().lower()` needed to become `stream_redirect(owner_of(pid))`-shaped to satisfy the new structural lock (`TestAirulesetOwnerResolutionAlwaysRedirected`, mirroring `TestPaneOwnerAlwaysRedirected`) — but `owner = stream_redirect((owner_of(pid) or "").strip().lower())` inserts an extra `(` directly after `stream_redirect(` (from the `or ""` grouping), which the lock's `stream_redirect\(\s*$` regex does NOT match, since a real character sits between the two calls. The fix that satisfies BOTH constraints: `owner = stream_redirect(owner_of(pid) or "").strip().lower()` — redirect the raw value first (a real `.strip().lower()`-equivalent normalization already happens inside the production `owner_of` default, `watchdog.pane_owner`, before this call site ever sees it), then normalize the (possibly-passthrough) RESULT. Chaining `.strip().lower()` directly onto a function call's closing paren needs no extra grouping parens at all — `f(x).strip().lower()` is valid Python on its own. Reuse this shape whenever a structural "these two calls must be textually adjacent" lock collides with an `or "default"`/normalization idiom that would otherwise insert a character between them.

- **A NEW hardening constant/check must be verified against the SIGNAL its own docstring claims, not just against a test that happens to go green — a check gated on the wrong clock can look correct while never engaging for the population it exists to protect.** #312: a truncated-`◎`-glyph hardening's own comment promised "a transcript still being WRITTEN TO this recently is evidence the session is alive", but the shipped check only compared the recovered marker's own declared arm TIMESTAMP (`mts`) — never the transcript FILE's own mtime. Since the surrounding recovery path is itself gated on live autopilot-progress evidence (`done>0`), the population it acts on has, by construction, always armed HOURS before recovery — so the 5-minute grace window almost never engaged for the exact case it was built to prevent. A fresh-context adversarial review reproduced it live in one shot: goal armed 2h ago, transcript written 0s ago, footer dark — old code recovered and re-armed it, precisely the duplicate-paste-into-a-still-alive-session risk the hardening existed to stop. Fix: require BOTH clocks (marker `ts` AND file `mtime`) to independently clear the grace window before ever acting — and bound BOTH comparisons to a non-negative delta (a future-dated marker, from clock skew or a transcript synced off another box, must never look "already old enough", or the one-shot recovery flag never gets consumed and the expensive full-file rescan repeats forever).

- **A regression test protecting a safety-critical invariant can have ZERO teeth when its fixture accidentally satisfies an EARLIER code path than the one actually under test — mutation-test it, don't trust green alone.** #312: `test_a_buried_but_since_cleared_goal_stays_untouched` wrote its "cleared" marker AFTER a multi-MB filler block, i.e. right at EOF, squarely inside the ordinary tail-BOOTSTRAP read window — so the REGULAR per-sweep incremental scan resolved `mark == "cleared"` on the very first sweep, and the NEW recovery function under test was never even called (instrumented: 0 invocations). Reverting the recovery function's own state-check guard left the test green regardless. Fix: bury BOTH markers (the "set" AND the later "cleared") before the filler, so neither is reachable by the ordinary bootstrap and the fixture is FORCED through the new full-file-rescan path being tested. Verify with the established backup/mutate/confirm-fails/restore technique — a passing test that was never proven to fail under the mutant it exists to catch is not evidence of anything.

- **A text-matching classifier over free-form prose must scan for EVERY match (`findall`/`finditer`), never take the FIRST (`.search()`), whenever the body can legitimately mention the same shape more than once with different meanings.** #311: `LOC_NUM_RE.search(body)` (a `>300-loc` self-contradiction check) took the FIRST number next to "loc"/"lines" — a body that honestly QUOTES the gate's own threshold before stating its real, genuinely-large count ("under ~100 LoC ... roughly 620 LoC across 5 modules") matched the 100 and false-blocked exactly the author being most compliant. Fix: collect every match, compare against the MAX. Same file, same lesson twice: `FOLLOWUP_RE`'s chain-parent extraction also took only the first candidate, letting an earlier decoy (e.g. a title mentioning one ticket while the body's real parent reference comes later) hide the genuine one — fixed by trying every extracted candidate, not just the first.

- **A "does A chain to B" detector over free-text references needs an explicit DIRECTIONALITY check, or a node that merely LINKS to its own children reads as itself being a child.** #311's chain-depth cap (a follow-up ticket whose own PARENT is itself a follow-up gets blocked) initially checked only "does the candidate parent's text ALSO mention a follow-up reference" — but a root/umbrella ticket naturally LINKS the follow-ups it spawned ("Spawned work: #3250 follow-up, #3251 follow-up"), and that FORWARD reference was misread as the root itself being chained, false-blocking a genuine first-generation filing. Fix: real GitHub issue numbers only ever increase over time, so a genuine ANCESTOR reference is always a LOWER number than its child — require the extracted reference to be strictly less than the number being checked (a backward reference) before treating it as evidence of ancestry.

- **This repo's shared (non-worktree) BATCH-dispatch working tree can show genuine, useful concurrent supervisor activity mid-session — commits appearing with no action from the dispatched worker are expected coordination, not corruption, but must be independently re-verified before being relied on or built upon.** Live during #311+#312: the supervisor committed a RED test commit for #312 directly onto the shared branch while the worker was mid-investigation, and later added a whole second RED/GREEN pair (#311's own `>300-loc` mechanical check) and a mutation-anchor re-pin fix — all without the worker's own `git commit`. The working tree ALSO flickered mid-edit several times (staged content appearing then reverting) before settling. The correct response, confirmed to work: never assume a file's content is stable without checking `md5sum` twice a few seconds apart; treat any newly-appeared committed work as a legitimate contribution once `git log` shows it as a REAL commit (not just an uncommitted diff) and it passes its OWN test suite; and always run the FULL local suite + `ruff` + `validate` from scratch before trusting or extending anything that appeared without your own action, regardless of how well-designed it looks.
